Part OneThe Listening House

Part One · Chapter One

The Listening Chamber

IMHET-RA: …Overseer of the Listening House at Ineb-Hedj and the last to hold the Great Interface at full voice before the Stripping. Imhet-Ra is remembered chiefly as a priest, which is the first and greatest of the misclassifications attached to his name. His own term for his work translates poorly; the nearest rendering is “the keeping of agreement between sky and stone.” It was Imhet-Ra who first calculated that the Listening Houses would fall silent within three generations, and who designed the Dispersal…

Concordance of Fragments

10 min read

Meret had been told to expect darkness. She had not been told it would have a texture.

It pressed against her eyes like wet linen. The air in the lower chamber was cool and close, and it smelled of salt and old copper and something else, something like the air before a storm. Above her, a very long way above, water was moving through the stone. She could hear it the way you hear your own blood when you cover your ears: a hush, then a knock, then a hush again, as steady as breathing.

“Your hands,” said a voice.

She hadn’t heard him come in. She held out her hands, still damp from the third washing, and felt a dry palm turn them over in the dark. His thumb pressed into the center of each of hers, one and then the other, the way a potter tests clay. Imhet-Ra’s breath was thin — thinner than she had imagined from the courtyard. She could smell lamp-oil and linen and, under them, the particular patience of a man who had already begun to lose something he would ask her to carry.

“Good. Feet?”

“Wet, Overseer. Standing in the basin, as instructed.”

“And the cup?”

“I drank all of it.” The brine still burned at the back of her throat. It had been saltier than seawater, and faintly bitter, and the old woman who poured it had watched her until the last of it was gone. “May I ask why?”

“You may ask anything. That is the first rule of this house, and the one the other houses have forgotten.” His voice was older than she had imagined, thin and dry, like reeds in the wind. “Why do you think?”

Meret considered it. She had been an initiate for two years. In two years she had learned four hundred and twelve verses, the order of the washings, the seven postures, and how to sit motionless through the night without sleeping. In two years, nobody had asked her what she thought.

“The salt makes the body agree with the water,” she said slowly. “The water in the walls is salt. So we make ourselves salt as well, so that nothing between us and the walls is… different.”

“Go on.”

“And the washing is so that every operator is the same. Not clean. The same. So that what I feel tonight is what you felt when you were an initiate, and what your teacher felt. If my hands were dry and yours were wet, we would feel different things, and neither of us could trust the other’s report.”

There was a pause long enough that she wondered if she had said something forbidden.

“Who told you that?”

“No one, Overseer.”

“Then you worked it out, which is better than being told.” She heard him lower himself onto the stone bench behind her, slowly, with a small exhalation of an old man’s effort. “Most of them believe the washing is purification. They’re not wrong, exactly. They’re describing the outside of the thing. A man can describe the outside of a boat perfectly and drown the first time he climbs in.” A dry sound that might have been a laugh. “The darkness. Why the darkness?”

That one she had wondered about for two years. “So there is nothing to see?”

“So there is nothing to see with your eyes,” he corrected. “Put your palms on the bands.”

She found them by touch: two strips of metal set into the wall at the height of her shoulders, cold, smooth, and slightly curved, worn into shallow grooves by centuries of other hands. She set her palms in the grooves.

The moment her skin met the metal, she felt it.

Not a pain. A presence. A faint crawling pressure in the pads of her fingers, as if a line of ants were walking very carefully across them. Then, impossibly, a taste on her tongue, sharp and clean, like biting a coin.

And then, in the perfect black in front of her, a light.

It wasn’t really there, and she knew it wasn’t. It was the kind of light you see when you press your eyes too hard, but it had a color, a deep, warm, rust-brown red, and it flickered in time with the pressure in her fingers.

She jerked her hands back. The light vanished. The taste faded slowly, like an echo.

“Don’t pull away.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were. Everyone does, the first time.” His voice changed, very slightly. “What did you see?”

“Nothing, Overseer.”

“Meret.”

She swallowed. “A light. Red, like river clay. But it isn’t there.”

Behind her, Imhet-Ra was silent for so long that she began to be afraid.

“Yes,” he said at last, very quietly. “It isn’t there. And it is. Both.” She heard him shift on the bench. “Put your hands back. Don’t fight it this time. And when the colors come, and they will, name them. Not with ordinary words. With the first names that come to you.”

She put her palms back in the grooves.

The pressure returned, the taste of the coin, and the rust-red glow. This time she didn’t pull away. She let it come, the way you let cold water close over your shoulders, and as she relaxed, the glow began to change. It brightened and dimmed. It shifted from rust to amber, then to a green like papyrus in shade, then back. The pressure in her fingers wasn’t one thing. It was several things laid on top of each other, a slow throb underneath and quicker flutters riding on it, the way a river has a current and also ripples.

And the names came. She didn’t decide them. They rose up in her the way the words of a song rise when you have heard it a hundred times.

“Nen,” she whispered, for the deep red. “Sa,” for the amber, a little higher. “Ri.” The green. There were more, higher and fainter, at the edge of what she could hold. “Hu… mai…” The last two were almost too thin to feel: “Tef. Ol.”

Seven of them.

“Seven,” Imhet-Ra said. His voice was strange. “You hear all seven.”

“Is that wrong?”

“No. It’s rare.” She heard him breathe in and out. “When I was your age, there were eleven of us in this house who could hear all seven. Today there are three. You, now, make four.” A pause. “I don’t hear ol anymore. I haven’t for six years. Tell me, is it bright tonight?”

The question was so simple and so sad that she didn’t know how to answer it.

“It’s thin, Overseer. Like a thread. But it’s there.”

“Good,” he said. “Good.” And then, more briskly, the teacher again: “Now. You know what the bands are?”

“Copper.”

“Copper over a heart of something better. And what is behind the copper?”

She had studied the house’s plan, the parts initiates were allowed to see. “The deep channel. Salt water, from the well below.”

“And above it?”

“The shafts. All the way up to the cap.”

“Then you know more than most of the priests in Ineb-Hedj, and you still know almost nothing.” His voice settled into a rhythm she would come to know well over the next years, the rhythm of a lesson. “Listen. The cap at the summit is electrum, gold and silver, and it will not rust in ten thousand years. It sits higher than anything for a day’s walk in every direction. The sky is always full of what we call the breath: before a storm it is very strong, and on a still night it is faint, but it is never absent. The cap drinks the breath. That is the sky-mouth.

“Far below us, under the foundations, the deep water lies in the rock, and in it there are plates of copper that the first builders set when this house was young. That is the earth-mouth. And between the sky-mouth and the earth-mouth is the whole weight of the house, a mountain of stone, which keeps them apart so they can speak to each other only by the paths we choose.

“Many people think the summit is the source. A boy said to me once, ‘The cap makes the breath.’ No. The breath is everywhere, in all things, always. The cap does not make anything. It is a mouth. The house is not the singer. It is the throat.”

Meret’s hands were beginning to ache. She held them where they were.

“And the water I hear above us?”

“Ah.” He sounded pleased. “That is the gate. The salt water comes down from the reservoirs through a passage cut so precisely that it cannot flow straight. It throws itself first against one wall, then the other, back and forth, the way a flag beats in the wind. You can hear it. Hush, then knock. Every time it swings, the water joins one channel and leaves the other, and because the water is salt, what reaches the bands rises and falls with it. The gate has no moving parts. Nothing to wear out but stone. The water-masters set its pace by the height of the reservoir, so the height of the water decides how much of the breath reaches your hands. Tonight the water is set to listen, not to speak.”

“Listen for what?”

“For the southern house. Upriver, nine days by boat. They are sending tonight.”

Meret frowned in the dark. “Sending how? Through the river?”

“Through the Earth itself.” He said it the way someone else might say through the door. “The ground is not dead, child. It carries the breath, the way water carries a ripple, very faintly, very far. The southern house disturbs the breath at their end, and if we are quiet enough, and wet enough, and in the dark, we feel the disturbance at ours.” Something gentle came into his voice. “There are houses farther away than the southern one. Much farther. On a perfect night, when I was young, I used to feel them too, like voices in another room. I have not felt them in a long time.

“Name the groups for me. All of them.”

So she named them.

It was harder than anything she had ever done. The colors kept slipping. The slow throb of nen never stopped, and it was easy to lose the faster groups inside it, the way you lose one voice in a crowded market. Twice she lost the thread entirely and had to wait, arms trembling, until the pattern came back around. The coin taste turned stale and metallic on her tongue. Her feet were numb in the basin.

But under the noise there was a pattern. It rose out of the flicker the way a song rises out of a crowd’s murmuring. A group of colors, then a silence that was itself a kind of beat. Another group. Another silence.

When she finally spoke, her voice sounded strange to her, too loud in the dark.

“Seven groups. Then it starts again. Every group uses all seven names, but in a different order. And the order of the groups is always the same.”

“Say them.”

She said them, in the names that had come to her. Nen-sa-ri-tef, sa-hu-nen, ri-ri-ol… all seven groups, falling softly from her mouth like water from a jar.

When she finished, Imhet-Ra didn’t speak for a long time.

“Good,” he said finally. “Now tell me what it means.”

“I don’t know the southern code.”

“Neither do they. Not anymore. Not fully.” He shifted on the bench again, and when he went on, the lesson-rhythm was gone. “Forty years ago, the southern house had eleven operators who could hold a sequence that long without error. Now they have four, and the four are old. In twenty years they will have one. And then they will have a ritual that no one understands, performed very carefully by people who believe that the point is the performing.”

Meret took her hands from the bands. The presence left her fingers all at once, like a held breath released, and the colors faded into the ordinary black.

“You’re saying the houses are failing.”

“I’m saying what the tallies already say. Copper shaved for tithe. Gates silting. Fewer children who hear all seven.” He did not raise his voice. That was the worst of it. “This is not a disaster, Meret. Disasters are fast. This is arithmetic.”

“Then we tell the governors.”

“I have. Four times.” She heard him stand; his joints cracked in the silence. “They are not stupid. Each is right about his part — water, copper, ritual, sky — and so the whole has no defender. Knowledge does not vanish when that happens. It breaks. It survives in pieces, each kept perfectly by someone who thinks the piece is the world. And no one remembers the pieces were meant to be heard.”

Meret said nothing. Above them, the gate went on swinging, hush and knock, hush and knock, as it had for longer than anyone could remember.

“Then it’s hopeless,” she said.

“That,” said Imhet-Ra, “depends entirely on what we do with the pieces.” And for the first time since she had entered the chamber, she heard him smile. “Dry your hands. Come upstairs. I want to show you the numbers.”

Next · Part One · Chapter TwoThe Numbers

Part One · Chapter Two

The Numbers

THE TALLIES OF IMHET-RA: …a set of forty-one papyrus rolls and one hundred and six clay tablets, of which only three fragments survive. Later scribes took them for temple inventories, which in a sense they were…

Concordance of Fragments

8 min read

The room at the top of the stair had one window, a narrow slot facing east, and Meret stood blinking while her eyes remembered dawn. Below, the plateau fell away to a river still silver, and cooking smoke rose thin from Ineb-Hedj. The rest of the room was papyrus and clay, dust and ink, a cedar table with rolls held open under river stones.

Imhet-Ra sat heavily. In the gray light she saw him properly for the first time: smaller than his voice, shaved head, skin folded like linen left in a chest, eyes bright with a lifetime of careful looking. His hands, she noticed, were still damp from the brine. He had not dried them either.

“Do you read numbers?”

“Yes, Overseer. The scribes taught me in my first year.”

“Good. Then you can check my work.” He drew the oldest roll toward him. “My teacher’s teacher began this one. That is why I trust it. I did not make it.”

Down the left side ran the years of the kings, one line to a year, back further than she could follow. Across from each year, three counts: the children set at the bands after two years in the house, how many heard any name at all, how many heard all seven.

At the top, in faded ink, the last column read nineteen, twenty-two, eighteen, twenty-four. A score of children every year. She ran her finger down. Sixteen. Fourteen. Fifteen. Eleven. Twelve. Nine.

The last line was fresh.

One.

“That’s me,” Meret said.

“That’s you.”

She frowned at the column. “But it goes up and down. Here there were seventeen, then eleven, then it rises again.”

“Like the river. Look at two years and you can tell yourself any story you like.” He lifted a reed pen and drew it through the air above the roll, top to bottom, without touching. “Look at all of it.”

She looked, and she saw it the way you see the slope of a field from a distance when every step on it feels flat. Up and down, and underneath, always, falling. Steeper in the last forty years. Much steeper.

“It’s speeding up.”

“Yes.” He laid a second roll across the first, a newer hand. “The weight of the bands below. Weighed when the house was made, and every ten years since at low water.”

This column fell gently at first, the slow tithe of hands and salt. Then, about forty years back, it began to drop in steps: a large piece gone, a pause, another large piece gone.

“Wear doesn’t do that,” Meret said.

“Wars in the south do. The god’s share is cut from the bands, the gate plates, the cap.” He said it the way someone states the height of a flood. “Thinner copper carries less. The names grow fainter. Fewer children hear, and there is less to hear with.”

He did not wait for her to ask. The third roll was the silt in the reservoir channels and, beside it, the nights each year the gate swung true. The silt rising. The good nights falling. “The water-masters still clear the channels,” he said. “But the men who knew how to cut them true are dead. Now they clear them the way you sweep a floor.”

Three rolls, side by side. Children, copper, nights. Three slopes, each with its own wobble, each pointing the same way.

“I have done the sums every way I know,” Imhet-Ra said. “A little after sixty years, there will be no one in this house who hears all seven names, and nothing left in the walls for them to hear with if there were. The southern house will go first.” He pushed the rolls toward her. “Find my mistake. I would be very glad if you found one.”

Meret looked at the rolls and then at him. No one in the house had ever asked her to find anything. They had asked her to recite, and to sit still, and to wash.

“How long do I have?”

“As long as you need.”

She took three days.

She worked by the window slot from first light until the lamp oil ran out. The other initiates left bread and onions and beer outside the door and went away whispering. Imhet-Ra did not come in once.

She added every column again from the beginning, two hundred and sixty years of lines, and found three small errors of addition. None of them changed anything. She took the averages over ten years, over twenty, over forty, trying each way to make the fall look slower, and each way it looked the same. She worked out what a thinner band would pass, using the water-masters’ rule for the gate, and it came out worse than he had written, not better.

On the second night she thought she had it. Perhaps fewer children were being brought to the house, and that was all. She went through the admissions, year by year, and divided one by the other.

The number of children admitted had hardly changed in two hundred years.

It was not fewer children. It was fewer hearing children. Out of the same number, every generation, fewer heard.

She sat by the window with that until the lamp guttered.

On the third day, before dawn, she went down to the chamber without being asked. She washed three times in the brine, wet her feet, drank the cup the old woman poured her, and laid her palms on the bands. It was a poor night. The gate swung unevenly and ol came and went like a flame in a draft. But she heard all seven, and while she sat with them she remembered his voice in the dark on her first night, asking her whether ol was bright, and she understood what had been wrong with the rolls for three days.

Imhet-Ra came at noon, a little out of breath from the stair. He looked at the rolls and at the new tablet beside them, covered in her uneven figures.

“Well?”

“You were right. Sixty years, perhaps less. Three small mistakes in addition. They don’t matter. I thought it might be fewer children coming. It isn’t. The same number come. Fewer hear.” She touched the tablet. “But you left something out.”

He went very still.

“Every flood you test the new initiates. You never test the operators. You count who begins to hear. You never count who stops.” She made herself look at him. “You told me you haven’t heard ol in six years.”

For a long moment he stood in the doorway with the noon light on his face. Then he came in and lowered himself into the chair across from her, one hand on the table edge the whole way down.

“All of them,” he said. “Every Overseer I know of. My teacher lost ol at fifty and tef at sixty. By the end he heard only nen and sa, the two deepest, and he would sit at the bands a whole night to hear a pattern a child could count in an hour. He told no one but me.” The bright eyes rested on her. “I didn’t put it in the tallies.”

“Why not?”

“Because I was afraid of what the number would be.” A dry, small smile. “It isn’t a good reason. It’s only the true one. Go on. You’ve counted it.”

“As well as I could, from the records of the Overseers’ deaths and what you said in the dark.” She turned the tablet toward him. “If the hearing leaves with age the way it left your teacher, it isn’t sixty years. Fewer children hear, and the ones who hear lose it sooner than anyone has written down. Less than forty years.” She hesitated. “There are four of us who hear all seven, counting me. Nebet, Pashed, old Ankhu. All past fifty.”

“Yes.”

“Then in ten years, by your teacher’s measure, there will be one.”

She did not say and you are not counted at all. She did not have to.

The town noise drifted up through the slot: a dog, a man calling prices, the creak of a water wheel. Imhet-Ra took the reed pen. Beneath her figures, in his small square hand, he began the first line of a tally the house had never kept. Nebet. Pashed. Ankhu. Their ages. Then his own name, and beside it a mark that meant six of seven. The pen shook on the mark. He waited until it stopped, and made it again, cleanly, over the first.

“You hear six,” Meret said. “You should still be counted.”

“For a little while. Ten years, perhaps, before I hear only the deep names.” He set the pen down. “And you, if you are lucky, have forty.”

She understood then why he had brought her upstairs on her first night, and why, in the dark, he had sounded so glad and so sad at once when she named the seventh color.

“It can’t be stopped.”

“I have spent eleven years looking for a way, and you have spent three days, and you are cleverer than I am.” He looked out at the river. “The copper can be replaced. The channels can be cleared, if anyone will pay men to clear them. But we cannot make children hear. And when the last ones who hear are gone, no one will know what the copper was for. They will take it, because copper is useful and the reason for it is not.”

“Then what do we do?”

He was quiet a while. When he spoke, there was something in his voice she had not heard before, a steadiness, like a man who has carried a load a long way and has finally chosen where to set it down.

“First I teach you everything I still know, while I still know it. Every name. Every way the names combine. How to think in them, not only hear them. That is the part the priests forgot first.” He laid his hand flat on the tallies, the way she had laid hers on the bands. “And when I am sure you know it, I am going to ask you to do something cruel.”

“What?”

“Not today. Today you sleep.”

He stood, slowly, and went to the door, and she heard his steps go down the stair one at a time into the dark.

Meret did not sleep. She pulled the new tablet toward her and read the four names again. Then, below his, in her own uneven figures, she added a fifth line: Meret. Seven of seven. She looked at it for a long time, and then, because the house had taught her that a tally is only true if it is kept, she drew a small empty square beside it, for the year she would have to mark it less.

Part One · Chapter Three

Lessons in the Tongue

THE TONGUE: …it is a mistake, though a natural one, to call it a language. A language is a set of sounds standing for things. The Tongue was a way of standing in things. Each of its seven names was at once a tone, a color, a number, a taste, a gesture, and a quality of the world; to speak a name was to perceive all of these together, and to perceive any one of them was to hear the name. The Tongue did not describe the world the operators lived in. It was how that world appeared to them…

Concordance of Fragments

12 min read

The lessons began on the roof.

Imhet-Ra climbed the outer stair each morning before the sun, slowly, with one hand on the wall, and Meret learned to be waiting at the top with a cup of warm beer and a mat to sit on. From up there you could see the whole plateau: the great house itself rising to the south of them with its cap catching the first light like a coal, the lesser houses around it, the long causeways down to the river, the green strip of fields and the endless tan of the desert beyond. In the mornings the wind came off the river cool and smelling of mud.

“Close your eyes,” he said on the first morning. “And give me nen.”

She hummed it, the deepest of the seven, low in her chest. As she did, the color came behind her eyelids, rust-red, warm, like the light through closed eyes when you turn your face to the sun.

“Good. Now, what else is nen?”

“I don’t understand.”

“You hear it as a sound. You see it as a color. What else? Don’t think. Answer.”

“It’s heavy,” Meret said, surprising herself. “It’s… the ground. Stone. It tastes like…” She frowned. “Like blood, a little. Like iron.”

“What number?”

“One.”

“Why one?”

“Because everything else stands on it.”

Imhet-Ra made a small satisfied sound.

“Now your hand. Without thinking. Nen.”

Her right hand moved on its own and settled palm down on the warm stone of the roof, flat, pressing, as if to steady something.

“There,” he said. “Every operator who has ever lived in this house has made that same gesture for nen. You were never taught it. Nobody taught me. It’s there in the name already, the way the taste is there, and the number. When we say a child hears the seven names, what we mean is that all of it arrives together.” He drank some beer. “Most people, if they hear the names at all, hear only the sound. A few hear the sound and see the color. Fewer still hear all of it at once, sound and color and number and taste and gesture and the thing in the world it belongs to, so that they aren’t seven different things, they’re one. That’s what you are. That’s what this house is for.”

They went through the names that way, one each morning.

Sa was amber, warm, the sun and things the sun had touched. It was two, the open palm turned upward, honey on the tongue, the feeling of a face in sunlight.

Ri was green, papyrus in the shade, growing things, three, fingers spread, the bitter freshness of a crushed reed.

Hu was river-blue, water and everything that moves like water. Four, a slow wave of the hand, salt.

Mai was the violet of the sky at dusk, breath, wind, air in motion. Five, the hand lifted and turning, the clean taste after rain.

Tef was silver-white, almost colorless, the breath of the sky itself, the thing that crawled across her fingers at the bands. Six, fingertips pressed together, the taste of the coin.

Ol took her three mornings.

“I can’t find it,” she said on the third day, frustrated almost to tears. “It’s there in the chamber. It’s thin, but it’s there. Up here I can’t find it at all.”

“Of course you can’t.” Imhet-Ra’s voice was gentle. “Ol is the hardest, and it isn’t a name like the others. Tell me what you saw in the chamber, when you heard it.”

“A color. But not a color. Something just beyond the edge of the others. Like…” She struggled. “Like when you look at the horizon and you know there’s more after it.”

“And what number?”

“Seven. But not really seven. It’s more like… all of them. Like it’s what you get when you hear the other six together.”

“Yes,” he said softly. “That’s ol. It isn’t a part. It’s the whole, when the parts are in agreement. That’s why you can’t find it alone up here. Hum me the other six.”

She hummed them, one after another, nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef, rising.

“Again. All at once, if you can. In your mind.”

She tried. She held them all together in her head, the red and the amber, the green and the blue, the violet and the silver, the weight and the warmth, the growing and the moving and the breath and the sky. For a moment they wobbled and fought each other like six cats in a sack. Then, very suddenly, they settled, the way a water surface goes still, and there behind her eyelids, impossibly, was the seventh color.

She opened her eyes and her face was wet.

“There it is,” said Imhet-Ra.

After the names, he taught her how they moved.

“The names are not the meaning,” he said. “Anyone can learn the names. A parrot could learn the names. The meaning is in the distance between them. Listen.”

He sang sa, and then, slowly, let it fall to nen. Not jumping, but sliding down, the warm amber darkening into rust.

Meret felt it in her stomach before she understood it. “That’s sad.”

“That’s grief. The sun going into the ground. Every operator who ever lived hears that as grief. Now this.” He sang ri, and held it, and let it rise just a little at the end, not to the next name, only part of the way.

“That’s… a question.”

“That’s a question. Green, growing, reaching, not yet arrived. Now, together.” He sang hu and sa at the same time, which she had not known a single voice could do, a low hum and a high one braided.

Something in her chest went warm and loose. “What is that?”

“Water and sun together. What grows, when you have both?” He smiled at her. “That’s love. The kind that makes things grow. There are other kinds. They have other chords.” He drank his beer. “You see? It’s not seven words. It’s seven places, and everything that matters is in how you go from one to another. You can say a thing in the Tongue that would take a scribe a whole roll of papyrus. And you can say things that a scribe could never write at all.”

“Like what?”

He thought about it.

“Like what the river is going to do next month.”

She thought he was speaking in poetry. She found out in the fourth month that he wasn’t.

The western reservoir gate began to stutter. It happened slowly at first, a little unevenness in the hush and knock that only the operators noticed, and then more, until on bad nights the names at the bands came through ragged and torn, like a song sung by a man out of breath. The water-masters came up from the town, a dozen of them, with their measuring cords and their leveling frames and their scribe, and walked the channels for two days, and measured everything, and found nothing wrong.

The chief water-master was a broad, proud man named Sennefer, with a voice like a drum and a cord of office as thick as a child’s wrist. He stood in the courtyard and told Imhet-Ra, with great respect, that the channels were true, the levels were true, the head was true, and the gate must be stuttering because of the will of the gods.

When he had gone, Imhet-Ra turned to Meret.

“Well,” he said. “Go and listen to it.”

“But they found nothing.”

“They measured it. That’s not the same thing. Go and listen.”

So she went down to the western reservoir, alone, at dusk. She took off her sandals and stood barefoot on the stone lip of the channel, where the water ran in toward the gate, and closed her eyes.

At first she heard only water. Then, as she breathed and settled and let the day fall away from her, the way he had taught her, she began to hear it as the Tongue. The broad slow water was hu, blue and steady. Under it, where the current dragged against the stone bed, was a thread of nen. And at the gate itself, where the water threw itself from wall to wall, the swing was a falling and rising, ri to hu to ri to hu, back and forth, back and forth, like a question asked and answered and asked again.

Except that it wasn’t even. Every swing toward the left wall was a little flat. Not much, the way a singer is flat when her throat is tired. A little short of where it should be. The question didn’t quite reach ri. The right-hand swing was fine. The left was sour.

She stood listening until it was full dark. Then she walked back up the channel, slowly, barefoot, listening the whole way. The sour note got stronger as she walked. At a place two hundred paces above the gate, where the channel bent, it was strongest of all. Above the bend it vanished.

She went back to Imhet-Ra.

“The left wall,” she said. “At the bend, two hundred paces above the gate. Something under the water, on the inside of the curve. It’s pulling the swing flat.”

He sent for Sennefer.

Sennefer came back irritated and polite. The water looked empty, he said, and he had measured it with the best cords in the Two Lands. Imhet-Ra asked him, mildly, to dig anyway. They dammed the channel above the bend, drained it, and dug. Under a skin of mud on the inside of the curve they found a shelf of silt no deeper than a finger, too shallow for any cord to catch, lying in exactly the one place that could throw the left swing short every night.

Sennefer crouched and pressed his palm flat on it, the way a baker tests dough. He stayed there a long time. Then he looked up at Imhet-Ra.

“How did you know?”

“I didn’t. She did.” Imhet-Ra did not raise his voice. “You measured whether the channel was the right shape. She heard whether it was saying the right thing.”

Sennefer did not look at her. He stood, wiped his hand on his kilt, and walked back down the channel toward the town with the face of a man who would spend years trying not to think about it. Meret stood on the lip of the drained channel with her sandals in her hand and waited for him to turn around.

He didn’t.

“Is that how they built it?” she asked that night. “By listening?”

They were in the upper room by lamplight, with a jar of wine between them which he called a celebration and she suspected was medicine for his joints.

“By thinking in the Tongue. At the channel, did you work out where the shelf was?”

She thought about it. “No. It was just there. Like the gesture for nen.”

“That’s what I mean. The Tongue thinks for you, if you let it. A wrong channel sounds wrong. A true gate is a chord. A basin at its right depth sings hu when the water is still.” He poured. “The builders didn’t draw this house first. They sang it, sky-mouth to earth-mouth, and cut each stone to say the next note. Every chamber a chord. Every shaft a held note. For two hundred years the right shape and the right sound were the same thing, so a man with a cord could keep the house as well as a girl with bare feet.” He drank. “Now they’re coming apart, and only one of them can tell. In a hundred years they’ll repair this house from the old marks, carefully, proudly, to a hair, and never know why the marks are where they are.”

She thought of Sennefer’s back going down the channel, and said nothing.

In the sixth month there came a night when the southern house did not send.

It happened sometimes. A storm upriver, or a sick operator, or a festival. Meret went down to the chamber anyway, because Imhet-Ra said that you learned the most on the nights when nothing was being said. She washed and drank and wet her feet and put her palms on the bands, and the names came, thin and steady, the background murmur of the sky’s breath with no pattern riding on it.

She sat a long time in the dark, letting it wash over her. Then, very slowly, she became aware of something underneath.

It wasn’t the southern signal. It was much fainter, much slower, so slow that at first she thought it was her own heartbeat. All seven names were in it, all at once, rising and falling together in long swells. But it wasn’t a sequence and it wasn’t a code. It didn’t stop and start again. It went on and on, changing, never quite repeating. It was like hearing a choir from very far away, across water, at night.

And it didn’t sound like anyone was sending it. It sounded like someone was singing.

She took her hands from the bands, and the sound went away, and her heart was beating fast.

She found Imhet-Ra on the roof, though it was the middle of the night. He was sitting on his mat under the stars, wrapped in a shawl, looking south toward the dark shape of the great house.

“There’s something under it,” she said, still panting from the stair. “Under the breath. When the south isn’t sending. It’s very slow and very faint, and it doesn’t repeat, and it isn’t a pattern. It’s…”

“Music,” he said.

She stopped.

“You hear her,” said Imhet-Ra. He did not turn his head. “Good. I hoped you would.”

“Who?”

He was quiet for a long while.

“The seven names aren’t ours, Meret. We didn’t invent them. We learned them. Long before there was a house on this plateau, long before there were kings, someone, somewhere, stood barefoot in wet ground on a night like this and heard what you just heard. The sky and the ground and the water and the breath between them, all singing together, all the time, so slowly that only the stillest mind can hear it. Every house we’ve ever built is a way of listening to her more clearly. And every message we send each other, from here to the south and the far houses, we send by singing very quietly along with her, in her own names, so that she carries it for us.” He pulled his shawl closer. “The priests call her the goddess. The scribes call her the land. They’re both right and they both have no idea what they’re talking about.”

Meret sat down beside him on the mat. The stars were very bright.

“Can you still hear her?”

For a long moment he didn’t answer.

“Not for nine years,” he said. “She’s the first thing to go. Before ol, before tef. You stop hearing the music under everything, and you still hear the names, so at first you don’t even notice. You think the world has just gone a little quieter.” He breathed out, slowly. “Tell me about it. How did she sound tonight?”

Meret opened her mouth to tell him, and found that she couldn’t. There weren’t words for it, not in the ordinary tongue.

So, very softly, she sang it. She sang it in the names, the way it had come to her through the bands: the long, slow swells, all seven together, rising and falling, red and amber and green and blue and violet and silver and the color beyond color, never quite repeating.

Beside her, Imhet-Ra closed his eyes.

She sang until her voice gave out. When she stopped, he was quiet for so long she thought he had fallen asleep. Then, without opening his eyes, he said,

“Yes. That’s how it went.”

And she saw, by the starlight, that his face was wet.

Next · Part One · Chapter FourThe Council

Part One · Chapter Four

The Council

Every one of them was right. That was the difficulty. Had any one of them been a fool, we could have argued with him.

Concordance of Fragments, Giza recension, Fragment 4

12 min read

They walked to the city in the dark so they would arrive before the heat.

Meret carried the tallies: nine tablets in a reed basket, wrapped in linen against the dust. She had copied them herself over four nights. The children who heard, generation by generation. The weight of the copper bands, tithe by tithe. The depth of the silt against the count of good nights. On the last tablet, in her own hand, was the correction she had found. Sixty years had become forty.

Imhet-Ra carried nothing. Now and then he turned his head a little toward the east, as if listening for something he already knew he would not hear.

“How many times have you done this?” she asked.

“This is the fifth.”

“And the first four?”

“They listened very well.” He stepped around a sleeping dog in the road. “They always listen very well. Nobody will be rude to you. Nobody will laugh. That would be easier.”

The sky went grey, then the color of mai, then thin sa along the edge of the world. Meret felt the breath shift as the sun came up, the long slow turn in the air that the house drank every morning. She said nothing. She had learned that he could still feel the large changes, and that it hurt him more when she pointed them out.

She had her own reason for the walk, and she had not told him. She had read the tablets aloud on the roof until her voice stopped shaking, and somewhere in the practicing she had begun to believe that it was only a matter of saying it well enough. Four times an old man had come and been thanked. This time a girl who could hear would stand in front of them, and they would hear her.

The council hall at Ineb-hedj had white walls and a floor of red stone polished by three hundred years of sandals. Light came through slots high in the walls and lay across the floor in bars. Meret counted them without meaning to. Seven. She wondered whether whoever built the hall had known why, and decided they probably hadn’t.

The governors sat on a low dais in a half-circle. The vizier Ptahmose in the middle, heavy and patient, with ink on the side of his hand. Khaemwaset of the treasury, lean and quick, a scribe at each knee. Hori, high priest of the house of Ptah, in pleated linen so white it hurt to look at. Old Amenemope of the hour-watchers, who kept the stars and looked as though he would rather be under them. And at the end of the row, a little apart, Sennefer the water-master, with river-mud worn permanently into the creases of his knuckles.

Sennefer looked at Meret when she came in, and then looked away.

“Imhet-Ra.” The vizier spread his hands. “Overseer of the Breath. Sit, drink something. Then tell us what you’ve come to tell us.”

“I’ll stand.” He nodded, and Meret knelt and laid the tablets on the floor before the dais in a row like a road. “I’ve come to tell you what I told you three years ago. Only the numbers are different. My student will read them. Her eyes are better than mine.”

That was not the reason. He wanted them to see that the thing he was talking about was not an old man’s grief. It was standing in front of them at fourteen.

She read the hearers first: forty in the time of the grandfathers’ grandfathers, then thirty-one, nineteen, eleven, four. She said four, and then, because he had told her she must, and one of the four is me. She read the copper, each tithe small and each one reasonable, and what was left. She read the good nights, the nights the house had sung clean, falling like water out of a cracked jar. Not all at once. A little more each year.

Then the last tablet.

“The overseer’s estimate was sixty years,” she said. “But it counted only the new children. It didn’t count that the old hearers are losing it too.” She did not look at him. “When you count both, it’s forty. I checked it for three days. I’m sorry. It’s forty.”

Nobody laughed. He had been right about that.

Khaemwaset spoke first, because he always did.

“Thank you, child. Clearer than last time, if the overseer will forgive me.” His smile was not unkind. “Let me be equally clear. You want the copper back.”

“I want it to stop leaving,” Imhet-Ra said. “Back would be better.”

“There are four thousand men at the second cataract building walls against the Kushites. The walls need gates, the gates need bolts, the men need axes, and the mines in the eastern desert give less every year. So I take the tithe of the god, as my father did. And each time I ask the priests whether the god is offended, and each time they tell me the rites are kept.”

“They are kept,” said Hori.

“So.” Khaemwaset turned back. “If I leave your copper on your house, I lose a fortress. I can count what a fortress gives me. Four thousand men who come home. Tell me what your copper gives me that I can count.”

Meret had known it would be asked. She had thought she knew the answer. Standing in the bars of light, she found she did not know how to say it to him.

Imhet-Ra did. He had said it four times before.

“Rain in the right month instead of the wrong one. A flood that rises when the hour-watchers say it will.” Amenemope, at the far end, inclined his head a fraction, as a man does when his own work is mentioned in a conversation that is not about him. “A field-sickness that stays in the delta instead of walking up the river. Quiet ground under the southern quarry. All of it a little. A little, every year, over the whole land. That’s what a house does. It leans.”

“Our harvests have been good,” Khaemwaset said gently. “Three good years. If your house were failing, wouldn’t the land show it?”

“It will.”

“When?”

“After I’m dead. After you’re dead. After the child’s children are grown.” Imhet-Ra looked at the tablets on the floor. “A fortress falls on a single day, and you can stand on the wall and watch it. This falls over a hundred years, and no one who can do anything about it will ever see it fall. Disasters are fast, treasurer. This is arithmetic. You and I both know which one a council can hear.”

Khaemwaset was quiet. Then he said, honestly, “I’m sorry. I have to feed the men I can see.”

Meret understood that he meant it, and that it was worse than if he had been cruel.

Hori did not wait to be asked. He had a beautiful voice, trained for the temple, and he turned it on Meret rather than on her master.

“Child, you should know that nothing has been neglected. Every festival in three hundred years has been kept. When the copper is taken, we perform the rite of release word for word, gesture for gesture, as the founders wrote it. We have not dropped a syllable.” He had a right to the pride, and he wore it. “If the house is weakening, it is not because the gods have been forgotten.”

“No,” said Imhet-Ra. “You haven’t forgotten a word. You’ve forgotten what the words were for.”

The white linen went very still.

“The dawn hymn is sung at dawn because at dawn the breath turns, and the house must be told to drink. The singer was meant to stand at the north shaft and listen, and change the hymn by a tone or two to match what the house was doing that morning. That’s why it was given in the Tongue. It was never a hymn. It was an adjustment.” He said it without heat, which was worse. “Your singers stand where the founders stood and sing what the founders sang, perfectly. They don’t hear anything, so they don’t change anything. They sing the morning of three hundred years ago, every morning.”

“The rite is the rite.”

“Yes,” Imhet-Ra said, almost kindly. “That’s what I’m afraid it’s becoming.”

Meret saw, unbidden, a man on a dark roof holding a lamp in the right place at the right hour, year after year, for a boat that needed someone on the water watching. She did not yet have a name for the picture.

The vizier had not spoken. Now he looked to the end of the row.

“Sennefer. You were at the western gate this spring.”

The water-master did not want to be looked at. Meret saw that before he opened his mouth.

“The gate stuttered,” he said. “I measured the channel with cords from the reservoir to the gate, twice, and found nothing. She stood in the water and said the left swing was flat, and walked two hundred paces up the channel and found a shelf of silt at the bend that my cords had stepped over.” He stopped, and made himself go on. “I’ve worked that channel twenty years.”

“So you believe the overseer,” said Ptahmose.

It was Khaemwaset who answered, lightly, before Sennefer could. “He believes there was silt at a bend. So do I. Silt is the water-master’s trade. I’d pay for that.” He turned his quick smile down the row. “How many men would it take, Sennefer, to find every shelf in the house before the girl has to go wading for it?”

Meret watched the big man understand that he was being offered a way out, and watched him take it, because it was the only shape his craft had.

“Twenty,” Sennefer said to his hands. “Twenty men with cords. Every channel, every year, the whole length. If something is silting, we’ll find it and dig it out.”

“Done,” said the treasurer, pleased. Twenty men with cords cost less than one copper band.

Meret felt her throat tighten. Twenty men with cords would never have found that shelf, because the shelf had not been deep. It had only been in the wrong place, and the wrong place was a sound. Sennefer knew it. She could see him knowing it, and putting the knowledge away somewhere it would not have to be looked at in front of the vizier.

“Thank you, water-master,” Imhet-Ra said, and meant it. “Take the twenty men. It won’t be enough. It will help.”

Every one of them was right, she thought. The treasurer about copper. The priest about the rites. The star-keeper about the stars. Each could go home and sleep well. The house would die in the space between them, where none of them lived.

Then Ptahmose looked at Meret.

“Child. The overseer says you hear the house. I’ve heard him say so five times, and I have never heard it myself. Will you let us hear it?”

She looked at Imhet-Ra. He gave a small nod. She had not known this would be asked. Somehow he had.

So this was the moment she had walked for. She stood in the bars of light on the red floor and closed her eyes, and reached for the house, three hours’ walk to the west. She could not hear it from here, not truly. But she had carried it all night the way you carry the shape of a room in the dark. The dawn turn just past. The north side drinking slowly. The western gate clean now, a sure hu knock and its echo. The bands thin, thin, too thin, so that the high tone came through like a voice speaking through a hand over its mouth. And under all of it the wrongness she had been taught to feel: two names that should have agreed and didn’t, the ache where ol should have been and was not.

She sang it the way he had taught her on the roof, in the Tongue, a chord unrolled into a line: sa and hu rising together, ri questioning, tef bright and then smothered, nen falling under everything like a stone into the river. This is what the house is doing. This is how sick it is. This is where it hurts. It was the clearest thing she had ever said in her life.

When she stopped, the hall was very quiet.

“That’s beautiful,” said the vizier at last, softly, and he meant it. “That’s very beautiful, child.” He turned to the scribe at his left. “Write it down. The song the girl offered. Keep it in the temple archives with the overseer’s tablets, so it’s never lost.”

The scribe bent and wrote. Meret watched his reed move. She knew he was writing a song was offered, in neat ordinary signs, and that those signs would sit in a dry room for a thousand years and hold nothing at all. A lamp held up on a roof.

She looked at Imhet-Ra. He was not looking at the scribe. He was looking at her, and his face was very gentle, and she understood that he had stood exactly here before and given them the truest thing he had, and watched them thank him for it and put it on a shelf.

The council decided what it always decided. The tithe would continue, because of the south. The water-master would have his twenty men. The temple would add a prayer for the strength of the house to the dawn hymn, to be recited, word for word, from the next new moon. The vizier thanked the overseer for his long service and his long walk, and asked him to come again in three years.

“Thank you,” said Imhet-Ra, and bowed, and meant that too.

Meret wrapped the tablets with her hands shaking and followed him out into the white noon. She held it in until they were past the last houses, past the dog, out where the road ran along the fields and there was nobody to hear. Then she stopped.

“You knew. The copper, the hymn, the cords. You knew he’d ask me to sing, and you knew what he’d say. You let me.” Her voice cracked for the first time all day. “Why did we walk all that way?”

He stood a few paces ahead, looking over the fields. The barley was high and green. Three good years. Khaemwaset was right about that.

“I came to see if the arithmetic had changed.”

“Had it?”

“Yes.” He turned. “It’s forty now. Four days ago it was sixty. You changed it.” He came back and took the basket from her, because her arms were trembling. “I stopped hoping to persuade them after the second time. I come because one day the council will be asked what it was told, and I want the answer to be everything. And because I needed to know how much time I have for the other thing.”

“What other thing?”

He didn’t answer. He looked at her the way he had in the hall, gentle and very tired, and something in it frightened her more than the council had.

“Something cruel,” she said. “You said there’d be something cruel.”

“Not today. Today you sang for the vizier of the Two Lands, and he said it was beautiful, and he was right. Let that be today.” He shifted the basket to his hip. “Come. If we’re quick, we’ll be home before the south calls.”

They were home before the south called. Meret sat cross-legged on the roof. Imhet-Ra sat beside her with his eyes closed, hearing nothing, as he always did now.

The south called, a long swell through stone, and the names began. She knew the three southern hearers the way she knew her mother’s voice. Tonight it was none of them.

Every name in order: nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef. Every interval exact, the way a hymn is exact when no one is listening to it. At the end, where ol should have closed, a gap of the right length, counted out. Then nothing.

She was standing before she knew it. He looked at her face and did not need the words.

“Somebody who learned the shape,” she whispered.

He reached up and found her wrist in the dark and held it, the way you hold a cord to feel which way the water is pulling. Far to the south, someone was already folding their hands and going to bed, having done it perfectly.

Next · Part One · Chapter FiveThe Southern House

Part One · Chapter Five

The Southern House

THE SOUTHERN HOUSE: …at Abu, on the island below the first cataract, built over the black granite where the river breaks. It was the first of the Listening Houses to fall silent and the last to stop sending. Its operators kept the evening greeting for some two hundred years after anyone there could hear it, to the exact interval, without a single recorded error. Later historians called this devotion, and they were right. It was also the first known instance of the pattern this Concordance calls the lamp on the roof…

Concordance of Fragments, Giza recension, Fragment 5

17 min read

They wrote to the south the next morning, and the answer came back on a grain boat twenty-six days later.

Meret read it aloud in the upper room, because Imhet-Ra’s eyes were tired. It was a long letter on good papyrus, in the hand of a temple scribe, and it was cheerful from beginning to end.

The southern house was well. The rites were kept. The flood had come on the day the hour-watchers named. The tithe of the god had been taken in the spring, as was proper in a year of war, and the rite of release had been performed for it word for word. The lector Heqaib sent his greetings and his respect to the Overseer of the Breath and hoped his joints were easier. There was some news. The old operator Djedi had gone to the west in the winter, full of years, and been buried with honor. The young operator Sabni had gone south with the king’s men to the fortresses at the second cataract, where his quick hands were wanted. The operator Iput was in good health. The evening greeting was sent every evening, at its hour, as it had been since the founding. A girl of the house named Ankhet had been trained to send it, and sent it beautifully.

“‘And so the southern house continues in every respect as it always has,’” Meret read, “‘and we pray the house of the north does the same.’”

She put the letter down.

Imhet-Ra said nothing for a while. He was looking at the tally on the wall, the one with the southern counts on it, the last of which was eleven years old.

“The tithe of the god,” he said at last. “In the spring. What do you suppose they took?”

“The letter doesn’t say.”

“No. It wouldn’t. It says the rite was kept.” He turned his cup in his hands. “Djedi went to the west. Sabni went south. Iput is in good health.” He said the three names slowly, the way she had seen him lay three stones on the table. “One dead. One gone to carry an axe made out of copper that used to be their bands, I would think. And one in good health who no longer sends the greeting herself. Why not, if she’s in good health?”

Meret didn’t answer. She knew he didn’t want her to.

“We’ll go,” he said. “Find out when the next boat goes up.”

“It’s nine days, Overseer. Against the current.”

“I know how far it is. I’ve been.” He smiled a little. “I was younger. So was the river.”

They went up on a cargo boat, wind behind and current against, the way boats go south.

Imhet-Ra slept most of the first two days, wrapped in his shawl among the grain sacks. On the third, Meret asked the thing she had been carrying since the letter.

“Iput. Did she stop hearing because she got old, or because they took the bands?”

“Both. Either.” He didn’t open his eyes. “Silence is the same from inside.”

“We could bring her north. Put her hands on our bands. Then we’d know.”

“Would we? She’s fading, and the house is fading, and all you’d learn is that the song was quieter.” He shifted against the sacks. “When the hearer and the heard go down together, there’s no test that tells you which one went first.”

On the fifth day she trailed her hand over the side. The river still said hu, but there was more nen under it now, stone rising up through the water.

“It’s different,” she said.

He only nodded, but she could tell he was pleased. “The desert houses drink from the sky. The southern house speaks through the granite into the ground. Same song, different throat.” After a while he added, “Farther east, my teachers said, there were houses of rain and lakes. I felt them once, very faintly, when I was younger than you. Whether they still send, I don’t know.” He looked at the water going by. “Maybe they’re sending perfectly.”

They came to Abu on the ninth day in the evening.

Meret heard the cataract long before she saw it. It came up through the deck into the soles of her feet, a roar too low to be a sound, and then the boat came round a bend and there it was: the river broken into a hundred white channels among boulders as black and smooth as wet oxen, the spray hanging in the low sun, the noise filling everything. The island lay below it, green with palms, and on its southern end, built right down onto the rock, was the southern house.

It was not tall. It was long and low and heavy, made of granite blocks so large she could not understand how anyone had moved them, and it was set into the rock rather than on it, as if it had grown there. On its flat roof stood a single post with a lamp on top.

The lector Heqaib met them on the landing with half the house behind him. He was a round, kind, anxious man of forty, and he took Imhet-Ra’s hands in both of his and nearly wept.

“Overseer. Overseer. You did us too much honor, coming all this way. If we’d known you were coming we’d have sent a better boat.” He turned and beamed at Meret. “And this is the student. We’ve heard. The one who hears all seven.” He said it the way people say the one who was born with a caul, with a kind of awe that was mostly pleasure in being near something rare. “You’ll see the greeting tonight. You’ll see Ankhet. You must tell us how she does.”

They washed in the river, ate bread and fish, and were shown everything. The lamps were trimmed. The basins were scrubbed. The brine was mixed by weight and kept in dated jars. The robes were spotless. The evening greeting was written out in full on a scroll, each pause marked with a dot, and in the margin, in a newer hand, someone had written the length of every pause in heartbeats.

“Iput wrote those,” Heqaib said. “When Djedi was failing. For the girl.”

“Where is Iput?”

“Resting. Her knees.” Almost a flinch. “She’ll want to see you in the morning.”

At the hour of the greeting, Meret went down with the operators.

There were six of them, four girls and two boys between ten and fifteen, and they went through the preparations in a line, laughing a little as children do when they are proud of something. They washed their hands three times in the brine basin. They wet their feet. A woman poured each of them a cup, and they drank it all and made faces at each other at the bitterness, and the woman watched each one until the cup was empty, the way the old woman at Giza had watched Meret. Then they went down a stair cut into the living rock, into the dark.

Meret washed and drank with them, and went down last.

The chamber was smaller than the one at Giza, and lower, and cut entirely out of granite. She could feel the cataract in the walls, a deep steady pressure, the whole river leaning against the stone. The darkness was perfect. She heard the children find their places by touch, the soft slap of wet feet on rock, the rustle of linen.

“Palms,” said one of the older girls, softly, and there was a whisper of hands on stone.

Meret felt along the wall for the bands.

They weren’t there.

Her fingers found the grooves at once. They were deep, much deeper than at Giza, worn into the granite by the hands of who knew how many generations. They were smooth as the inside of a shell. But the grooves were empty. Where the copper had lain there was only the stone, and along the edges of each groove a fine ridge where the metal had been prised out, and at each end a small square hole where a pin had been. She ran her thumb across one of the holes. Its edges were still sharp. It had been done recently. It had been done in the spring.

Very slowly, she set her palms in the empty grooves.

Wet granite. Cold. The pressure of the river in the rock.

Nothing else.

No ants walking on her fingers. No coin on her tongue. No rust-red glow in the black. She waited. She breathed the way she had been taught, and let the day go, and opened herself the way you open a door, and the darkness stayed only darkness. She had never in her life put her hands on the house and felt nothing. She hadn’t known it was possible to feel such complete nothing. It was like putting your ear to the chest of someone you love and hearing no heart.

Around her, in the dark, the children were counting.

They counted softly, in the Tongue, in the names, the way the scroll upstairs said to. Nen, sa, ri, tef. A pause, the right length. Sa, hu, nen. A pause. They counted the seven groups of the evening greeting, all together, in unison, the way children recite a lesson they know so well they could say it in their sleep. They weren’t hearing anything. They were remembering it. Every name in its place. Every pause in its length.

Then somewhere beyond the wall, in another chamber, she heard the sending begin. It was the sound of water: a sluice opened and shut, opened and shut, and with it a dull deep knock through the granite, felt more than heard. Ankhet, sending. The children fell silent to listen to it. Meret could hear them breathing, quick and happy, in the dark.

When it was done, one of the boys said, in a voice full of joy, “Ol,” and the others said it after him, ol, ol, and somebody laughed, and the older girl said, “Hands,” and they all took their palms from the stone together.

Meret kept her hands where they were a little longer. She didn’t know why. Perhaps so that she would remember exactly what nothing felt like.

Ankhet was waiting at the top of the stair.

She was twelve, perhaps, thin and quick, with her hair in a side-lock and a knotted cord wound twice around her left wrist. Her eyes were shining. She had clearly been waiting the whole time.

“Did you hear it?” she said. “Did it come through? Heqaib says you can hear all seven. Did I send it well?”

Meret looked at the cord around her wrist. It was the evening greeting. She could see that at once. Each knot was a name, and the spacing of the knots was the length of each pause, and Ankhet had worked the sluice by feeling the knots pass through her fingers one by one in the dark. It had been made very carefully. Somebody had loved her enough to make it.

“Every name was in its place,” Meret said.

It was true. She held onto that.

Ankhet’s whole face opened. “Iput made the cord. When I was eight. She said if I sent it right every night, the way she showed me, then one night I’d hear it too, the way she used to. She says it comes if you do it long enough. Like swimming.” She turned the cord on her wrist. “I haven’t yet. But I’m only twelve. Sabni didn’t hear all seven until he was fourteen. Did you hear it when you were twelve?”

“I don’t remember,” said Meret, which was the first lie she told in the southern house.

“I’ll hear it,” said Ankhet, with total confidence. “I’m going to send it every night until I do.”

And she ran off down the passage toward the kitchens, because she was twelve, and it was time to eat.

Iput lived in a small room at the top of the house, with a window looking out on the cataract.

Meret went up to her late that night, when Imhet-Ra had gone to sleep. She found her awake, sitting on a mat by the window with a lamp, and knew her at once, the way you know someone whose voice you have heard many times and whose face you have never seen. She was older than Meret had pictured, and smaller. She was holding a cord in her lap, half knotted.

“The northern girl,” Iput said. “Sit. I thought you’d come.” She looked at Meret’s face for a while in the lamplight. “You went down with them.”

“Yes.”

“And put your hands on the stone.”

“Yes.”

Iput nodded slowly. She did not ask what Meret had felt. She didn’t need to.

“You used to lean on ri,” Meret said. She didn’t know why that was the first thing she said. “When you sent. I always knew it was you, because the third name was always a little long. As if you were asking something.”

Something in the old woman’s face shook and then steadied.

“Was it? Nobody ever told me.” She looked down at the cord. “Djedi’s sa was flat. Everyone knew that. We used to laugh at him.” She was quiet for a while. “It went from me two years ago. The music under everything went first, years before that. Then ol. Then tef. Then last winter, while Djedi was dying, I was hearing only the two deep names, and the young one, Sabni, was doing all the real listening. And then in the spring the treasury came for the copper, and Sabni went south with it. And after the copper went, I heard nothing at all.” She lifted her hands from the cord and looked at them. “Was that my age? Or was it the copper? I sit here every night and think about it, and I’ll never know. I’ll die not knowing whether I went deaf or whether the house went silent. Isn’t that strange? Of all the things to not know.”

“The Overseer says you can’t tell. When both fade together.”

“The Overseer is right. He usually was.” She began, slowly, to tie another knot. “I’m making a new one for Ankhet. Hers is fraying.”

Meret watched her hands.

“Why do you keep sending?” she said finally. “If no one can hear. Not here. Not in the north, except me, and soon not me.”

Iput didn’t stop tying.

“Because the north might be listening. Because I promised Djedi. Because Ankhet’s face when she comes up from the dark is the way mine used to be.” She pulled the knot tight. “I don’t know what else to do with what I know. Was I wrong? Should I have told her there’s nothing behind the stone?”

Meret opened her mouth.

She thought of the children counting in the dark, every name in its place. She thought of the boy saying ol as if it were the best word in the world. She thought of Ankhet with the cord around her wrist, saying I’m going to send it every night until I do.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Iput looked at her for a long moment.

“No,” she said. “Neither do I.” And she went back to her knot. “Will you tell them? Heqaib, the children? You can, you know. You’re the one who hears. They’d believe you.”

Meret sat in the lamplight, with the cataract roaring below the window, and could not make herself say anything at all.

After a while Iput said, quite gently, “Thank you,” and Meret understood that her silence had been an answer, and that the old woman had heard it.

She did not sleep. Before dawn she went out of the house alone, barefoot, and climbed down onto the rocks below the cataract.

The granite was black and wet and cold. The spray hung over everything, and the noise was so great that it stopped being noise and became a kind of stillness. She found a place where a great smooth boulder sloped down into a pool, and she knelt and wet her hands in the river, and wet them again, and a third time, out of habit. Then she laid both palms flat on the stone.

The music hit her so hard that she cried out.

It was the same music she had heard under the breath at Giza, the slow singing she had sung to Imhet-Ra on the roof. But here it was not faint and far away across water. It was enormous. It came up out of the rock through her palms and into her chest, all seven names at once, rising and falling in long swells, and in it she could feel the whole weight of the river hammering on the granite, pressing it, and the granite answering, in its own grey-and-white crystal voice, the way a struck bowl answers. The cataract was pressing the stone, and the stone was singing, and the song went down into the dark of the Earth and out in every direction farther than she could follow. Nen was so deep it shook her teeth. Ol was not a thread here. It was a sky.

This was why they had built the house here. Of course it was. It was the loudest place in the world.

And a hundred paces away, in a chamber cut out of the same rock, six children had pressed their palms to the same stone through empty grooves and heard nothing.

She stayed until the sun was up. When she came back up the bank, Imhet-Ra was sitting on the landing steps with his shawl around him, waiting for her. He looked at her wet face and her wet hands.

“It’s loud here,” he said. “Isn’t it? I remember.”

She sat down beside him on the step.

“They took the bands in the spring,” she said. “There’s nothing in the grooves. Not even the ants. The children count it anyway, every name, from the scroll, and Ankhet sends with a knotted cord and thinks one day she’ll hear.” Her voice did not shake. She had learned that from him. “Iput knows. She asked me if she’d been wrong.”

“What did you tell her?”

“Nothing.”

“And Heqaib? The children?”

“Nothing.” She looked at her hands in her lap. “I was going to. I had it all ready. I was going to tell him to stop, because it’s a lie, because they’re standing in the dark for something that isn’t coming. And then I saw her face, and I couldn’t.” She turned to him. “Was that wrong?”

Imhet-Ra did not answer for a long time. Out on the river, a fisherman was poling a reed boat across the still water below the rapids, singing something to himself, and the sun was coming up the color of sa.

“I don’t know if it was wrong,” he said. “But I know what it was. It was the answer to a question I’ve been afraid to ask for eleven years.” He was not looking at her. He was looking at the lamp on its post on the roof of the southern house. It had been lit for the greeting the night before and nobody had put it out. It was still burning, small and pale in the daylight. “They’ll keep it. Do you see? Without copper, without a single hearer, without anyone asking them to. Nobody in the north told them to. They’ll keep every name in its place for love, for two hundred years, if no one tells them there’s nothing there.”

“That’s terrible.”

“Yes,” said Imhet-Ra. “And it’s the strongest thing I have ever seen.”

He got up, slowly, one hand on her shoulder.

“We’ll go home on the next boat. I want to say goodbye to Iput first.” He looked down at her. “Heqaib will ask you before we go. In front of all of them. You know that.”

“I know.”

“Whatever you tell him, I won’t say a word against it.” His hand was heavy on her shoulder. “And when we get home, come upstairs. I want to show you what I’m going to do with the numbers.”

The boat left at noon. Half the house came down to the landing to see them off, as half the house had come to meet them, and Heqaib had both of Imhet-Ra’s hands again.

Then he turned to Meret, and the children behind him went quiet, and she understood that this was what they had all been waiting the whole morning to ask.

“Well?” Heqaib was smiling, but his hands had found each other in front of his belly and were holding on. “You went down with them. You’re the one who hears. How does she do?”

Ankhet stood at the front with the cord on her wrist. She wasn’t smiling. She was too frightened to smile. Behind her the other children stood in a row, the way they had stood at the brine basin, and behind them, in a doorway at the top of the steps, small and leaning on a stick, was Iput.

Meret had walked all the way to the vizier’s house to say a true thing well enough that someone would hear it, and no one had. Here no one would need persuading. One sentence, and Heqaib would believe her, and the lamp would come down off the roof tonight.

She looked up at the doorway. Iput did not move.

“Every name was in its place,” Meret said. “Every pause was the right length. I’ve never heard it sent more carefully.”

None of it was a lie. That was the worst of it.

Ankhet made a sound like a bird let out of a hand. The children cheered. Heqaib wiped his eyes and laughed at himself for doing it, and beside her Meret felt Imhet-Ra’s hand close on her shoulder, so hard it hurt, and stay there until the boat was off the landing.

When she looked back at the doorway, from the water, Iput was gone.

That evening, as their boat drifted north on the current, Meret sat in the stern and watched the southern house grow small behind them. At the hour of the greeting, a tiny light went up on its roof. She couldn’t hear the sending from the water. She knew exactly how it went. She said it under her breath, every name in its place, every pause the right length, until the light was gone.

Next · Part One · Chapter SixThe Dispersal

Part One · Chapter Six

The Dispersal

The Dispersal was not a library. A library can be burned in an afternoon by one man with a torch. It was the opposite of a library: a thing that could be destroyed only if everyone, everywhere, forgot their own small part of it at the same time.

Concordance of Fragments, Giza recension, Fragment 6

15 min read

There were five things on the cedar table when she came up the stair.

She saw them in the slot of light from the window before she saw him. A block of white limestone the size of a loaf, squared and smoothed, with lines cut into one face. A jar of water with a knotted cord tied round its neck. A strip of copper as long as her forearm, curved, worn bright along one edge, and beside it a small stoppered flask of thick grey glaze. A clay tablet covered in the dots and lines of the hour-watchers. And the brine basin from the chamber below, and the cup that went with it.

Imhet-Ra was sitting behind them with his hands folded.

“Sit,” he said. “This is going to take a while, and I would rather say it once.”

She sat. They had been home three days. He had spent all three of them in this room with the door shut. She had heard him in the night, walking.

“You asked me at the south whether the hearing is in the copper or in the person,” he said. “I told you both. That was the answer of a man who hadn’t decided anything. Now I’ve decided.” He touched the copper strip. “This is from the first bands of this house. My teacher kept a piece when they were replaced. Pure copper, beaten thin over a heart of electrum.” He turned it over and showed her a small dark crystal seated in a socket on its back. “And a grey stone behind it, that lets the breath pass one way and not the other. Quartz in the fittings, there and there. A coppersmith with good ore and a good master could make a band like this in a month.”

“Then we could make them again. When the treasury gives the copper back…”

“Yes. Any time. A coppersmith can make bands, Meret.” He laid the strip down. “No one can make a listener.”

She waited.

“That’s the whole of it,” he said. “Everything else is detail. The house is made of stone and water and metal, and every one of those can be found or dug or beaten out again by men who have never heard a name in their lives. If the cap is taken, someone can cast another. If the channels silt, someone can clear them. If the bands are stripped to the stone, as they were at the south, someone can set new ones in the old grooves next spring. All of that is arithmetic. But a child who hears all seven names, and knows what they are for, and knows how to stand in the dark so that the names can reach her: that can’t be cast or dug or beaten. When the last one is gone, she’s gone. And then the copper is only copper, and the first treasurer who comes along is right to take it.”

“So what do we do?”

“We are going to hide the listener.”

Meret felt, absurdly, a flash of anger, not at the Plan but at being its hinge. She had wanted a life of listening, of roofs and bands and the southern voice under her ribs. She had not wanted to become a seed.

“You can’t hide a person,” she said.

“Not a person. A way of being a person.” He leaned forward. “Listen. When you checked my tallies, you found that the same number of children came to this house every year, and fewer of them heard. Fewer, not none. Look closely and the count never quite reaches nothing. Every generation there is still one, or half of one, a child who sets her hand flat on the ground for nen without being taught. It’s in them still. It’s in the blood of the Two Lands, and it’s in the blood everywhere else too, I’d think. It isn’t dying, Meret. It’s going to sleep.”

“Then why does it matter whether we teach it? If it’s in the blood, it’ll come back by itself.”

“Will it?” He tapped the brine basin. “Imagine a child, a thousand years from now, born with all seven names in her. There’s no house left. No copper. Nobody to wash her hands three times or pour her the cup or take her into the dark. Nobody even knows the names have names. What happens to her?”

Meret thought about it.

“She hears colors when people sing,” she said slowly. “And she doesn’t know why. And she’s told she’s strange. And she learns to stop saying it.”

“Yes. And she dies never knowing that what she has is the most valuable thing in the world, because there’s nothing for it to hear and no one to tell her what hearing is for. The capacity sleeps in the blood. The rest of it has to be kept somewhere else. The knowledge of how to build a house. How to set the levels. What the metals are for. What the sky is doing. How to make a body ready. If we keep all of that in one place, it will be taken, or burned, or turned into a rite. So we’re going to break it.”

“Into pieces.”

“Into five pieces.” He laid his hand on the limestone block. “Stone.” On the jar. “Water.” On the copper strip and the flask. “Metal.” On the tablet. “Sky.” And on the basin and the cup. “Body.”

He looked at her across them.

“You told me in your first week, before you knew anything, that knowledge without a keeper breaks. I’ve decided to break it myself, on purpose, carefully, along lines I choose. Each piece will go to people who already have a reason to keep it. And each piece will look like something else.”

He went through them one by one, the way he had gone through the names on the roof.

“Stone. The builders already keep the measures of the cubit and the rules for cutting a chamber. They think they keep them for strength and for beauty, and they’re right. We’ll give them a few more. The proportions of the chambers in this house. The angles of the shafts. Why the stone above the upper room is laid in five floors with air between them. They’ll be told these are the measures of the gods, which bring luck to a building. Nobody steals a measure of luck. They’ll copy them into every temple for a thousand years, very exactly, and never know that every chamber is a chord.

“Water. The water-masters already mark the levels on the reservoir walls for the flood. We’ll give them more marks. The listening levels, the height of the water that sets the gate to listen or to speak. They’ll be told these are lucky marks. Fortunate heights. They’ll cut them into new reservoirs in new countries and never know that they’re cutting the tuning of an instrument.” He smiled faintly. “I think I have a keeper for that one already.”

“Sennefer.”

“Sennefer has been to see me twice since the council. He doesn’t know why he comes. He stands in the doorway and talks about channels and then goes away. He’s a man who has heard that his cords were stepping over something, and can’t stop thinking about it. That is the best kind of keeper.” He touched the copper again. “Metal. The hardest, because metal is what gets taken. So it goes to people who will never be allowed near the treasury: smiths, healers, the ones who mix medicines and paints. Seven metals, in their order, each with its name. Which carries the breath best. Which doesn’t rust. Which stones let it pass one way. And this.” He picked up the grey flask and unstopped it.
