Inside, something heavy and bright shivered and settled. “The silver water.”

Meret had seen it before. There was a little of it in the threshold basin under the upper chamber, lying in a stone trough so still it looked like a mirror. She had been told never to touch it.

“In the house it lies where the breath runs strongest,” he said. “Calm nights: still. When a storm grows dangerous, it rises toward the cap like iron to a lodestone and closes a path, a door that opens itself, so the storm runs to ground instead of through the bands and into whoever’s hands. Beside a lodestone it moves without slope. We don’t fully understand it. The founders didn’t either. They knew it was useful.” He stoppered it. “Healers will call it medicine. Smiths, the secret of gold. They’ll be wrong, and they’ll keep it in riddles. Riddles last.

“Sky. The hour-watchers already count the seven wanderers. Amenemope’s people. Sun, moon, and the five that walk among the stars. We’ll give each one a name in the Tongue, a color, a metal, a day. They’ll think it’s omen lore, and they’ll keep it forever, because men will always want to know what the sky is going to do. They’ll never know that the near sky, the one full of the breath, is the one that matters. That will be a pity. But the names will be safe.”

He stopped. His hand was resting on the edge of the brine basin.

“And Body,” Meret said.

“And Body.”

He didn’t speak for a while. The light from the window had moved across the table and lay now on the basin, so that the brine in it shone.

“The other four will be kept by people who don’t know they’re keeping anything,” he said finally. “That’s their strength. But the Body can’t be kept that way. The washing, the cup, the dark, the posture, the counting, the names sung as a child learns to sing them: that has to be taught, from one person to another, by someone who knows what it’s for. And it has to be kept separate from the temples, because the temples will make it a rite, and a rite is the thing you keep when you’ve forgotten what it’s for.” He looked up at her. “So there will be a second house. Not a building. A lineage. Ordinary people, in ordinary villages, who wash their hands three times before the evening meal and teach their children seven names in a song and stand in the dark once a month. Quietly. Never written down in any temple roll. Passed from mother to daughter and father to son.”

Meret felt it coming before he said it.

“I want you to found it,” said Imhet-Ra. “And I want you to teach the practice even after it stops working. Even when the bands are gone from this house the way they’re gone at the south. Even to children who hear nothing, and their children, who will hear less. Every washing in its place. Every name in its place. Every pause the right length.”

The room was very quiet.

“That’s what I saw at the south,” she said. “That’s exactly what I saw at the south.”

“Yes.”

“You want me to make that. On purpose.”

“Yes.”

“Children in the dark with their hands on stone.” Her voice was rising and she let it. “Counting from a scroll. Saying ol because the scroll says to. Ankhet with her cord, thinking that if she does it long enough…” She stopped. “That’s cruel. To teach a child a thing that will feel like nothing, and let her think it’s something. To let her spend her whole life waiting at the bands for a voice. It’s the cruelest thing I’ve ever heard.”

“Yes,” said Imhet-Ra. “It is.”

She had expected him to argue. He didn’t. He sat with his old hands folded on the table among the five things and looked at her with great tenderness, and agreed.

“I have thought about it for eleven years,” he said. “I’ve thought about every other way. There is no other way that lasts. Rolls burn. Treasurers melt metal. Kings forget. What lasts is what people do with their hands, every day, because their mothers did it. You saw it at the south. Nobody ordered them. Nobody paid them. Their house was stripped to the stone, and they washed and drank and counted anyway, perfectly, for love. That’s the strongest thing in the world, Meret. I’m only asking you to aim it.”

“Aim it at what? If the hearing goes, and the copper goes, then what are they waiting for?”

“For the day someone builds it again.”

“Who? Why would anyone…”

“I don’t know who. That’s the point. Not a treasurer. Not a priest. Somebody curious.” He picked up the copper strip and turned it in the light. “One day a man, or a woman, will find the marks the water-masters cut, and the measures the builders kept, and the riddles the smiths wrote about the silver water, and the names the hour-watchers gave the wanderers. And they’ll put a piece of copper on a wall, out of curiosity. And then what will they do? They’ll touch it with dry hands, in a lit room, standing however they like, and they’ll feel nothing. And they’ll write down that there was nothing, and go home. Unless someone is there who knows to wash three times. To drink the cup. To stand in the dark with wet feet. The body is half the instrument, Meret. The rite was never a prayer. It’s how you tune the operator to the house. Without it, the best copper in the world is just a wall.”

She thought of the lamp on the roof of the southern house, still burning in the morning.

“The lamp,” she said. “It’s half of the lamp.”

“Yes. The lamp is useless without the boat. But the boat is lost without the lamp. You keep the lamp lit so that when someone finally comes up the river, there’s a light.”

“Then they’ll know,” Meret said. “I won’t make another Ankhet. Every child, the first night, before the first washing, I’ll tell her: this may feel like nothing. You are keeping a lamp for a boat.”

He looked at her for a moment as if she had sung a name he had stopped being able to hear.

“Tell them,” he said. “Tell every one. The first generation will know. The second will believe. The tenth will think the washing is the point.” He said it without flinching. “That’s the price. In a thousand years they’ll be exactly like Heqaib, proud of every syllable. I can’t prevent that. Nobody can. All I can do is make sure that when the boat comes, the lamp is lit, and there’s someone standing on the roof who knows how to wave.”

“And who is the boat?” Meret said after a long time. “Who puts the pieces back together?”

“Ah.” He looked almost happy. “That’s the one thing I can’t hide anywhere, so I’ve had to trust it. Look at the five.” He moved his hand over them. “Each will be kept by people who are very good at their piece. Builders, water-masters, smiths, hour-watchers, the second house. Each will be very good at their piece, as Sennefer is good at his. And because each is good, each will think their piece is the whole thing. That’s what will keep the pieces safe for a thousand years. And it’s exactly what will keep them apart.

“So the one who puts them back together can’t be any of them. It has to be someone who belongs to none of the five houses and has wandered into all of them. A person who can’t stay in one room. Who knows a little about stone and a little about water and a little about metal and the sky and the body, not enough to be master of any of them, and enough to notice that they rhyme.” He touched the basin, the tablet, the copper, the jar, the stone, one after another, lightly, the way you touch the strings of a harp. “Five pieces. Nen, sa, ri, hu, mai. And a sixth, tef, the breath itself, which no one can keep because it’s everywhere. And when someone holds all six at once in one head…”

“Ol,” said Meret.

“Ol. It isn’t a piece. It’s what happens when the pieces agree. I can’t hide ol, child. Nobody can. It has to be heard, fresh, by somebody standing in the right place.” He shrugged, an old man’s small shrug. “I’m trusting that such a person will be born. Generalists are born in every age. The world usually beats it out of them. Perhaps one day it won’t.”

“When?”

He considered it seriously.

“Sennefer measures,” he said. “His grandsons will measure better. One day men will measure so finely that they can measure the breath itself, and the far houses, and the music under everything, the way you can measure a flood. They’ll build things that count. Perhaps one day they’ll build something that can count everything at once, all the pieces in all the rooms.” He was quiet for a moment. “Counting isn’t hearing. But a thing that counts and a person who hears, together, at the same table…” He shook his head. “I don’t know. I’m an old man telling stories. But that would be a boat.”

The sun had gone down while they talked. The room was full of the last violet light, mai, and the five things on the table were only shapes.

“You’re asking me to spend it,” Meret said quietly. “My forty years. The hearing. You’re not asking me to listen to the house. You’re asking me to spend everything I have teaching it to people who can’t hear it.”

“Yes.”

“Not even to spend it on the house. On children. On strangers. On somebody a thousand years from now I’ll never meet.”

“Yes.” His voice was steady. His hand on the rim of the brine basin was not, and he took it away and hid it in the other. “I could keep you at the bands every night of your life, and you’d be the finest operator this house has had since the founding, and you’d hear the south go silent, and then the far houses, and then the cap would come down, and then you would grow old and lose ol and it would all be gone with you. And it would be beautiful, and it would be for nothing. Or I can spend you. I’m sorry. That’s the cruel thing. It isn’t the children. It’s you.”

She looked at him for a long time across the dark table.

Then, instead of answering in words, she answered in the Tongue.

She sang sa, warm, and let it fall all the way to nen, the sun going into the ground, and held it there while it hurt. Then, without a breath between, she sang hu and sa together, the low hum and the high one braided, the way he had taught her on the roof, the chord that meant water and sun, the love that makes things grow.

Grief, then love. In the Tongue it was one sentence, and it meant yes.

Imhet-Ra closed his eyes. He couldn’t hear it the way she could anymore. But she saw that he had understood.

There was a knock at the doorway.

They both turned. A broad shape filled it against the last light of the stair, with a cord of office as thick as a child’s wrist wound round one shoulder.

“Overseer,” said Sennefer. He sounded embarrassed. “Forgive me. I was at the western gate. I’ve been thinking about the bend.” He stood there, turning the cord in his big hands. “I don’t know how to ask this. I measured that channel for twenty years. I want to know what she heard. I want…” He stopped. “Can you teach me to listen?”

Imhet-Ra looked at Meret, and something passed between them that did not need the Tongue.

“No,” he said gently. “I can’t teach you to hear, water-master. No one can. But come in and sit down.” He pushed the jar of water with its knotted cord a little way across the table, toward the big man. “I can teach you where to stand.”

Sennefer looked at the knotted cord for a long moment. Then he ducked under the lintel and sat, and laid both his big hands flat on the cedar, palms down, the way a child does who has not been taught.

Next · Part One · Chapter SevenThe Last Lesson

Part One · Chapter Seven

The Last Lesson

The tallies of the Giza house survive in a single later copy. The last entry in the old overseer’s line is written in a different hand from all the others, and it is not arithmetic.

Concordance of Fragments, Giza recension, Fragment 7

14 min read

Seven years went by, and the arithmetic did what arithmetic does.

Nebet died in the second winter, of a fever that went through the town. Pashed died in the fourth, quietly, in his sleep, and they found him in the morning with his hands folded on his chest palm down, nen, as if he were steadying something. Old Ankhu lived, but by the fifth year he had lost ol and tef and came to the bands only on the best nights, and sat there a long time, and came out smiling the way a man smiles when he has heard a friend’s voice in another room and cannot make out the words.

Meret kept the new tally. She kept it in her own hand on a fresh roll, the one Imhet-Ra had started under her figures on the day she found his mistake. Every year at the flood she brought the operators down one by one and tested them the way the children were tested, and wrote down what they heard. Every year the line was shorter. In the sixth year it said: Meret, seven of seven. Ankhu, five. Imhet-Ra, two. In the seventh it said: Meret, seven. Ankhu, four. Imhet-Ra, one.

She didn’t mean to show him that line. He asked to see it, and she showed it to him, and he nodded and said, “Yes, that’s right,” and handed it back.

Seven new children had heard any names at all in those seven years. None had heard more than four.

The second house grew, very slowly, the way a root grows.

It began with Sennefer. He was the first, and the most unlikely, and he never in his life heard a single name. He came up the stair once a month, and sat in the upper room with his big muddy hands on the table, and Imhet-Ra taught him the listening levels: how high the water had to stand in each reservoir for the gate to listen and how high for it to speak, and which heights made the hush long and the knock soft, and which made them quick. Sennefer cut the levels into the reservoir walls as small marks, a hand’s breadth apart, with a little sign beside each that meant fortunate. He taught them to his sons as luck. He taught them to his apprentices as tradition. In the third year he went to the delta to cut channels for a new estate, and cut the fortunate marks into its walls too, though there was no house there and no bands and no reason, because a channel didn’t look finished to him anymore without them.

“I’m a fool,” he said to Meret once. “I know it. I can’t hear a thing. I’m cutting marks for a song I’ll never know.”

“You’re cutting them in the right places,” Meret said.

“Am I?”

“Every one.”

He looked at her a long moment, the way he had looked down at the silt shelf in the drained channel, then nodded and went back to cutting. He was the most faithful man she ever knew.

The others came more slowly. A coppersmith named Ptahshepses, who had made the bands’ pins for twenty years and had always wondered why the overseer wanted them so pure. A healer from the village below the plateau, a woman named Henut, who already knew more about the silver water than was good for her. A young hour-watcher from Amenemope’s roof, bored with the decans, who came up the stair one night to ask what the overseer meant about the near sky. To each, Imhet-Ra gave one piece. Never two. He was very strict about that.

And the families. That was Meret’s work.

She went down into the villages on the edge of the fields. She found the households where a grandmother or a grandfather had once been brought to the house as a child and sent home again, because they’d heard only two names, or three, or because there was no room. She sat with them. She did not tell them very much. She taught the mothers to wash the children’s hands three times before the evening meal, in salted water. She taught them a song for the little ones, seven names, rising, with a gesture for each: palm down, palm up, fingers spread, a slow wave, the hand lifted and turning, fingertips together, and the last one with no gesture at all, only the hands opened and held still. Once a month, at the dark of the moon, she taught them to stand in a dark room with wet feet and their palms on the stone of the wall and count.

They felt nothing. There was no copper in their walls and no cap above their roofs, only mud brick under their palms in the dark.

She told them the truth. She told them they were keeping a lamp lit for a boat that would come one day, long after all of them were dead. Some believed her and some didn’t. Most of them did it anyway, the way you keep any custom your mother asks you to keep, and some of them came to love it. By the seventh year there were nine families in four villages. The children sang the seven names as a skipping song in the lanes. She heard them sometimes, walking home in the evening, and stood still in the road.

At the end of the seventh year, at the height of the flood, there came a night that was perfect.

Meret felt it coming all day. The breath was strong and even, the air dry and still on the plateau, and Sennefer’s men had cleared every channel that spring, and the reservoirs stood at the listening mark to a finger’s width. When she went down at dusk to look at the gate, it was swinging so truly that she stood on the lip of the channel with her eyes closed and almost laughed. Ri, hu, ri, hu. The question asked and answered, asked and answered, and nothing sour in it anywhere.

When she came up, Imhet-Ra was waiting at the top of the chamber stair.

He was very thin now. He walked with a stick of acacia wood, and his hands shook when he held a cup, and on most days he didn’t leave the upper room. But he was standing at the head of the stair in his clean linen, with the brine basin at his feet, and he’d already washed his hands. She could see that they were wet.

“It’s a good night,” he said. “Isn’t it? I can feel that much. The air.”

“It’s the best night in a year. Maybe longer.”

“Then I’d like to go down.”

She looked at him. He hadn’t been down to the bands in four months. The last time, he’d sat in the dark for half the night and come up gray in the face and said only, “Nen, very faint,” and gone to bed.

“Overseer, the tally…”

“I know.” He smiled. “I know what it says. You wrote it. Help me with the stair.”

So she helped him. She washed her own hands three times, and wet her feet, and the old woman who poured the cups, who was not the same old woman as on Meret’s first night but her daughter, poured two cups of brine and watched them both drink. Imhet-Ra drank his slowly, in small sips, and made a face at the end the way the children at the south had, and Meret laughed in spite of herself.

Then she took his arm and they went down into the dark.

It took a long time to get him settled. His knees would not bend the way they used to. She found the bench and guided him onto it, and set the basin for his feet, and lifted his feet into it one at a time, the water cold around her own ankles. Then she took his wrists, very gently, and lifted his hands to the wall, and found the grooves, and set his palms in them.

The bands were thinner than on her first night. She knew that from the tally, and she knew it from her own hands every night: the copper worn and shaved and worn again until in places she could feel the shape of the electrum heart through it. But on a night like this they were still alive. She put her own palms into the grooves beside his, and it came at once. The ants walking on her fingers. The coin on her tongue. The rust-red rising in the black, and then amber, and green, and blue and violet and silver, and ol, thin and clean and high, like a thread of light across a doorway. And under all of it, so faint she had to hold her breath, the long slow singing.

She waited. She didn’t want to be the one to speak first.

Beside her in the dark, Imhet-Ra was breathing slowly. She could hear him settling, the way he had taught her to settle, letting the day fall away, opening. She heard him let out a long breath. She heard him draw another in.

The silence went on.

“I can feel it,” he said at last. His voice was very quiet and very calm. “In my hands. The crawling. The ants. I can feel it walking. And there’s the coin, I think, a little. On my tongue.”

“Yes,” she said. “That’s it.”

“But it doesn’t say anything.”

She didn’t answer.

“There’s no color,” said Imhet-Ra. “Nothing behind my eyes. No nen. It’s just the ants.” He was quiet for a moment. “It’s like holding a letter from someone you love, in a hand you used to read, and seeing the marks on it perfectly, and not knowing what a single one of them means.”

He didn’t take his hands from the bands. That was what she would remember afterward. Everyone pulled away the first time, and he had told her not to, and now, at the last, he didn’t. He sat with his palms in the grooves his own hands had been wearing for sixty years and let the breath of the sky walk across his skin and say nothing to him at all.

“Well,” he said finally. “There it is. That’s the last of it. I wondered what it would be like, and now I know.” He breathed out. “It’s quieter than I thought.”

Meret found that she was shaking. She pressed her palms harder into the copper to stop it.

“Meret,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Count it for me.”

On her first night he’d asked her to name the groups, and she’d done it the way a scribe tallies, the groups and the silences, spoken afterward. On the roof, years ago, she’d sung him the music after the fact, the way you tell someone about a place they can’t go. He’d closed his eyes and said yes, that’s how it went.

Neither would do tonight. He couldn’t hear the names at all. A report would only be a report, and a song afterward would be a memory. He wanted to be in it.

So she did a thing nobody had ever taught her.

She took her right hand from its groove. She laid it, wet and trembling, over the back of his left hand, so that her palm was on his knuckles and his palm was on the copper, and she could feel through him the faint crawling of the breath in his skin. She left her own left hand where it was, in its groove. So she held the house in one hand and him in the other.

And she began to name it aloud, in the Tongue, as it came.

At the instant each name rose in her, she sang it, softly, close by his ear, so that the sound of her voice and the touch of the breath on his palm arrived at the same moment.

“Nen,” she sang, low in her chest, as the slow deep throb came up under her hand. And she felt, under her palm, his hand twitch.

“There,” he whispered. “That one. That’s…”

“Nen. Yes. The slow one. The one everything stands on. Feel where it is.” She waited for it to come again. “Nen.” And again. “Nen.” She kept singing it every time it rose, until she felt his hand go still under hers, listening. “Now. On top of it. Quicker. Sa.”

“I feel it.” His voice had changed. “In the middle of my palm. Quicker. Warmer.”

“That’s sa. Amber. The sun on your face. Honey.”

“Yes,” he said. “Yes. I remember honey.”

She went on. She went up through them as they came, one name and then the next, singing each one at the exact instant it arrived, naming its color and its taste and its weight, so that he could lay each one over the ants in his hand like a man laying a word over a mark on a page he had forgotten how to read. Ri, the flutter at the edge of the fingers, green, bitter reed. Hu, the slow wave, river-blue, salt. Mai, lifting and turning, the color of the sky right now if they could see it, the taste after rain. Tef, the high bright crawl at the very tips of his fingers, silver-white, the coin itself.

He couldn’t see the colors. She could feel him not seeing them. But he could feel where they were, which ant was which, and he had seen the colors for sixty years. He knew what silver-white was, and river-blue, and green in shade. When she sang them to him at the instant they touched him, he could put them back himself, out of everything he remembered, the way a blind man who was once a painter can know a room from the sound of his own voice in it.

Then the southern greeting came.

She had forgotten it was the hour. It came up through the stone, faint and correct, every name in its place, every pause the right length. Ankhet. Ankhet was nineteen now, and still sending every night, and still, as far as anyone knew, hearing nothing. Meret sang it to him too, group by group, at the very moment each name arrived. She told him in the Tongue that it was perfect, and in the Tongue it was impossible to say perfect without also saying what was missing. Sa bright and nen under it and, at the end, a gap where ol should have been, the right length.

“Ankhet,” he murmured.

“Yes.”

“Still sending.”

“Every night.”

“Good,” said Imhet-Ra. “Good girl.”

And when the greeting had gone and the stone was quiet again, there was the music under everything.

She sang him that last. She sang it as it came, slow, all the names at once, the long swells rising and falling, never quite repeating. She couldn’t sing all seven at the same time. Nobody could. So she wove them, one into the next, faster than she ever had, the way the children wove the names in their skipping song, so that the ear could almost hold them all at once. She kept her hand on his hand, and his hand on the copper, and his breath grew slower and slower in the dark, matching the swells.

“Give me ol,” he said.

She had known he would ask.

“I can’t sing ol. Nobody can. It isn’t a name, you told me. It’s when the six agree.”

“I know.” He was very calm. “Then give me the six, as fast as you can, and I’ll do the rest.”

So she did. Nen sa ri hu mai tef, nen sa ri hu mai tef, faster and faster, softly, by his ear, while under her palm the breath came through his hand and through the house. She sang it until her voice wore thin. She was crying and didn’t stop singing. She didn’t know what he was hearing. She didn’t know if he was hearing anything at all.

Then, under her hand, his hand turned over.

He lifted it from the copper and turned it and laid his palm, wet and cold from the band, against hers, palm to palm. He held it there.

“There,” said Imhet-Ra. “There it is.”

They stayed a long time. When she finally helped him up, his feet were numb from the basin and he could barely stand, and she half carried him up the stair. At the top, in the lamplight, his face was wet and gray and peaceful.

He sat down on the bench by the brine basin and leaned his head back against the wall and breathed.

“That’s the last lesson,” he said, when he could. “Seven years I tried to find a way to teach it, and in the end you had to teach it to me.” He opened his eyes. “Someone hears. Someone counts. Never one alone. It was always someone at the bands and someone on the roof.” He found her hand on the bench and held it, hard, the way he had held her shoulder on the boat. “Remember, when you’re the one who needs counting for.”

“I’ll remember.”

“And the one who comes, a thousand years from now. Whoever puts the pieces back. They won’t manage it alone either. Tell the second house. Make them sing it.”

“I will.”

He was quiet for a while. Then he said, “Bring me the tally.”

She brought it from the upper room, and the ink, and a reed pen, and held the lamp for him. But his hands were shaking too much, so he gave her the pen.

“Write me down,” he said. “Tonight. You know what it is.”

She knew. She unrolled the tally to the fresh line under the year’s entries, and in her own uneven hand she wrote his name, Imhet-Ra, and beside it the sign that meant none of seven.

Then she stopped, with the pen still on the papyrus. She looked at him. He was watching her.

“Go on,” he said gently.

And beside none of seven, in smaller signs, she wrote: Seven of seven, through Meret.

He looked at it for a long time in the lamplight.

“That isn’t arithmetic,” he said.

“No.”

“It’s the truest line in the roll.” He let his head go back against the wall. “Leave it.”

He died in the season after the flood, on the roof, at the hour the south used to call.

She was sitting beside him on the mat. He’d asked to be carried up there in the afternoon, and lay in the sun with the shawl over him, and watched the light go across the plateau and up the side of the great house until the cap was burning like a coal. When the sky went to mai, the greeting came up through the stone. Meret listened to it, and told him under her breath that it had come, every name in its place. He didn’t answer. She took his hand.

It was cold. It was cold the way the bands were cold, the first time you put your palms on them, before you know what is inside.

She held it for a long time. Then she folded both his hands on his chest, palm down, nen, and sat with him while the stars came out.

The next morning she went upstairs. She wrote the year’s line in the tally, under his. There were two names on it now.

Meret, seven of seven. Ankhu, four.

She looked at it for a while. Then she rolled it up and put it on its shelf, beside his teacher’s tallies and his teacher’s teacher’s, and went down to the western reservoir and stood on the lip of the channel with her eyes closed, listening to the gate. It was swinging true.

Next · Part One · Chapter EightThe Stripping

Part One · Chapter Eight

The Stripping

THE STRIPPING: …recorded in the treasury rolls as the tithe of the god, final portion. The inventory survives, which is more than can be said for the things on it. It lists one cap of electrum, of such a weight; bands of copper over electrum, four pairs; plates of copper from the water-gate, nine; silver water, two jars and a half; pins, hinges and fittings of copper, by weight; cedar, for fuel. In the margin a scribe has added: stones of no value, left. It is the most complete description of the Great Interface we possess, and it was written by the men who took it apart…

Concordance of Fragments, Giza recension, Fragment 8

14 min read

The order came in the twenty-sixth year after Imhet-Ra died, in the month of the harvest, by the hand of a treasury scribe who had walked out from the city in the heat and was very sorry to trouble her.

Meret read it in the upper room. She was forty-eight. Her eyes were still good, though she held the papyrus a little farther from her face than she used to. The scribe stood by the door and turned his reed case in his hands.

War in the east; fortresses; axes; mines failing. The treasurer Ipy had determined the houses would give the tithe in full. Cap, bands, gate, every scrap of metal, at the new moon. The rite of release would be performed. The house would of course remain a house.

“Of course,” said Meret.

She fed the miserable scribe and let him rest, then sat alone on Imhet-Ra’s roof-mat and did the arithmetic. The tally had said forty years. It was thirty-four. Too generous. She did not go to the council. There was nothing left to tell them.

She sent for Ptahshepses.

The coppersmith was an old man now, older than Imhet-Ra had been on Meret’s first night, and nearly blind. His grandson led him up the stair. He sat at the cedar table and ran his thumb along its edge, the way he always did, as if to learn what kind of wood it was.

“So they’re coming,” he said.

“At the new moon.”

“For all of it.”

“All of it.”

He nodded slowly. “And the little house? Is it ready?”

“Is it?”

He laughed, a dry sound like Imhet-Ra’s. “It’s been ready for three years. I was waiting for you to stop polishing it.”

They had made it together over eleven years, in secret, in the back of his workshop below the plateau. It was small enough to hold in two hands: a box of black granite from the south, no longer than her forearm and narrow enough to fit a hand-span shaft, polished inside and out. Inside it lay seven reeds of copper, each beaten thin over a heart of electrum, each a little longer than the last, set in a cradle of mica so they did not touch the stone. Under each reed was a seat for a chip of the grey stone that lets the breath pass one way only. Between them was a sliver of clear quartz, clamped tight. At the front of the box two copper plates were set flush with the stone, waiting for something to touch them.

The electrum had been the hardest. There was no way to get it. In the end it had come from the floor. For thirty years, every time the bands were taken out at low water and scraped and reset, the shavings had fallen onto the chamber floor, and Meret had swept them up and kept them in a jar. Thirty years of dust from the house’s own hands. Ptahshepses had melted it down to a bead the size of a chickpea and beaten it into seven hearts.

Each reed was tuned. He couldn’t hear the Tongue, but he had the best ears in the Two Lands for metal, and she had sat beside him through a hundred evenings striking each reed with a sliver of bone and saying higher or lower or there, and he had filed and filed until each one, when struck, sang its name. Nen for the longest, deep and dark. Sa. Ri. Hu. Mai. Tef for the shortest, thin and silver. And the seventh reed, which was not shorter than tef but exactly as long as nen, laid crosswise over all the others, so that when anything set the six singing together, it would shiver too.

“Will it work?” the coppersmith had asked, more than once.

“I don’t know. It isn’t meant to work. It’s meant to wait.”

On the underside of the lid, she had cut one sentence.

The treasury came at the new moon with forty men, ropes, sledges, a scaffold of cedar poles, three scribes, and a set of scales so large it took eight men to carry it.

The treasurer Ipy came himself. He was a young man, courteous and capable, with a fine linen kilt and a list. He greeted her in the courtyard with great respect.

“Overseer. I’ve heard about you all my life. My grandfather’s brother was Khaemwaset, who kept the treasury in the old vizier’s time. He used to talk about the overseer who came to the council, and the girl who sang.” He smiled. “I’m sorry it falls to me. Truly. If there were copper anywhere else, I’d be taking it from there.”

“I know,” said Meret. “I have to feed the men I can see.”

He looked at her in surprise. “Yes. That’s what he used to say. Exactly that.”

“I know,” she said again.

At noon twelve priests in white performed the rite of release, word for word and gesture for gesture, for an hour, and did not drop a syllable. Hori’s grandson asked if she had been pleased.

“It was perfect,” Meret said, and it was.

Then the men went up the scaffold to the cap.

She went down to the chamber.

Nobody had told her to. Nobody would have understood if she’d explained. She washed her hands three times in the basin, and wet her feet, and the old woman’s granddaughter poured her a cup and watched her drink it. She went down into the dark alone and sat on the bench and put her palms on the bands.

It was a dry, bright day, and the breath was strong. The names came at once. Nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef, all there, thin, through the thin copper. Ol, thinner still, a thread, but there. Only the music under everything was missing. She had not heard it in three years. It had gone first, the way he said it would, the summer she turned forty-five. She had written herself down in the tally that year as seven of seven and drawn a small mark beside it that meant the music, gone. Now she sat with the seven, and listened, and waited.

Far above her, she felt them begin.

She felt it in the names before she heard any sound. A small wrongness, high up, in tef: a scraping, a flicker. That would be the chisels at the base of the cap, working at the old pins. Then a shudder through all of it, and ol went out.

It didn’t fade. It simply went, the way a lamp goes when someone pinches the wick.

She held on to the bands.

Tef went next. It wavered for a long time, bright and dim and bright, while the men up there worked the cap loose from its seat. She could feel the sky-mouth being prised from the top of the house, the great electrum mouth that had drunk the breath for three hundred years, tilting on its ropes, and with every tilt the silver-white flared and failed. Then a long shiver, and it was gone, and she knew that the cap was off the house and hanging in the air on its ropes and no longer touching anything.

Mai went as they lowered it. Hu faltered, because the breath that had come down through the house now came down through nothing, and the gate could swing as truly as it liked, and there was nothing for it to open and shut. Ri, the question, held on longest of the high names, and she thought, of course, and almost laughed.

Then ri was gone, and she had sa and nen, the two deep names, the ones Imhet-Ra’s teacher had kept to the end, and she sat in the dark and held on to them with her palms while above her forty men walked a mountain of electrum down a cedar scaffold in the sun.

It had taken his teacher thirty years to go from seven to two. It took the treasury an afternoon.

Sa went at sunset. She felt the warmth of it go out of her palms like the sun going into the ground. Sa falling to nen. She heard herself sing it, softly, without meaning to, in the dark. Grief. Every operator who ever lived hears that as grief.

And then she had only nen.

It was very faint. It wasn’t the breath of the sky anymore. There was no sky-mouth to drink it. It was only the earth-mouth, the copper plates far down in the deep water under the foundations, too deep and too wet for the treasury to reach, still lying in the dark where the first builders set them, still touching the ground, still faintly breathing up through the stone of the house to the bands. The slow red throb under everything. One.

She held it until it, too, was gone. Not because the earth-mouth had stopped, but because in the morning the men came with their chisels for the bands, and she had to take her hands away.

She watched them take the bands.

She stood in the chamber with a lamp, because she wanted to see, and the workmen were polite and careful and worked around her. They prised the copper from the grooves with bronze chisels and wooden mallets. The bands came out in long curved strips, worn so thin in places that they tore. She saw the electrum hearts shining through the copper, and the treasury scribe saw them too and made a note. She saw the little grey stones behind each band fall out of their sockets onto the floor, and the quartz from the fittings, and the thin sheets of mica that had kept each band from the stone. One of the workmen kicked them into a corner.

At the gate they took the copper plates, nine of them, from the walls of the passage where the brine had swung from side to side for three hundred years. From the threshold basin under the upper chamber they took the silver water, scooping it into jars with copper ladles, and the scribe weighed the jars twice because he could not believe how heavy they were. They took the pins and the hinges and the copper straps that held the shafts’ linings. They took the cedar of the old water-doors, which was very old and very dry and would burn well. They took everything that could be melted or burned or weighed.

They left the stone. Of course they left the stone. Nobody could carry away a house.

When they had finished, Ipy came and found her in the courtyard and read her the inventory aloud, all of it, because that was the proper form. Cap. Bands. Plates. Silver water. Pins. Cedar, for fuel. It took less time than the rite.

“And these,” said the scribe beside him, holding up a basket. In it were the grey stones and the quartz and the mica, swept up from the chamber floor. “Stones of no value. Shall I throw them out?”

“I’ll take them,” said Meret.

The scribe hesitated and looked at Ipy.

“Give them to her,” said the treasurer kindly. “What would we want with stones?” He bowed to her. “Thank you, Overseer. For your long service. The house remains, as I promised.” He looked up at the great house, white and bare against the sky, its summit blunt and dull where the cap had burned for three hundred years. “It’s still very beautiful.”

“Yes,” said Meret. “It is.”

She watched the sledges go down the causeway toward the river, a long line of men and oxen in the dust. On the first sledge, wrapped in linen and roped down, the cap caught the evening light once, through a gap in the wrapping, like a coal. Then it was gone.

There was a shaft in the south wall of the chamber that no one used.

It was a hand-span square, cut so perfectly true that you could look up it with a lamp and see the walls run together to a point. It climbed into the body of the house at a steep slope for a hundred and twenty cubits and stopped. Imhet-Ra had told her once that in the founders’ time it had carried something, a line of copper perhaps, or of brine, toward the sky-mouth. Long before his time it had been stripped and left empty. Near its upper end, the builders had cut a pair of grooves in the walls, a notch to hold a door. Nobody knew why. Nobody had ever put a door in it.

They did it on the night of the dark moon, five of them: Meret, Ptahshepses and his grandson, Henut the healer’s daughter, and Sennefer’s grandson, a broad, silent man who was water-master now and wore the old man’s cord of office over his shoulder.

They had cedar rods, each two cubits long, each with a copper hook at one end and a copper eye at the other, so that they could be joined. They set the little granite house on a sled of cedar no wider than itself, and laid it in the mouth of the shaft, and began to push.

It took all night. They worked by lamplight, joining rod to rod, feeding them up into the dark. The rods bent and scraped. Twice the sled stuck and had to be eased back and tried again. Once a hook snapped with a sound like a finger cracking, and the rod came back down short, and they had to start that length over. The water-master counted the rods out loud as they went in, and Meret listened to the count and thought of Ankhet’s knots, every one in its place. At the hundred and fourteenth cubit the sled stopped against nothing, and the old coppersmith, who could not see, put his hand on the end of the last rod and felt it, and said, “There. It’s at the notch. It’s sitting square.”

Then the door.

It was a slab of fine white limestone, thin, polished, cut to fit the notch exactly. Through it, a little way apart, passed two pins of pure copper, bent into small loops at both ends. On the far side the loops stood just proud of the stone, so that when the door was set in its notch they would press against the two copper plates on the front of the little house. They were the instrument’s hands. One day, she thought, if someone came up the shaft and touched them, they would be touching the house.

They pushed the door up the shaft after it on the rods, more carefully than anything, and set it in its grooves, and felt it seat. Then they drew the rods back down, one by one, unhooking each, until the last of them came out into the lamplight.

The snapped hook was still up there somewhere, wedged in the dark below the door. Nobody could reach it. They left it where it lay.

“What did you write?” asked Ptahshepses’s grandson, when it was nearly dawn and they were sitting on the chamber floor too tired to stand. “On the lid. My grandfather says you cut something.”

Meret was quiet for a while.

“One sentence,” she said. “In the Tongue.”

She had cut it on the inside of the lid in six signs and one empty place, and then, because cut lines can be worn smooth or misread, she had gone over each sign with ochre, rust-red, nen, the color that lasts. She had painted it a second time on the floor of the shaft just in front of the little house, where whoever opened the door would see it first.

“What does it say?”

She could have told him the names. Hu and sa together. Then mai, then tef, then nen. Then ri, rising, partway, not arriving. And then a seventh place, left empty, exactly the right length, where ol should go.

“It’s a question,” she said.

“What does it ask?”

“Whether anyone’s there.” She looked up at the black square mouth of the shaft in the lamplight. “It’s what you say at a door at night, when you’ve been waiting a long time. And the last name is missing. That’s on purpose. Whoever answers has to say it.”

“And if nobody ever does?”

“Then it’ll wait,” said Meret. “It’s good at that. So am I.”

The first child came to her that spring.

She was four years old, a water-master’s great-granddaughter from the village at the foot of the plateau, a small solemn girl with a side-lock and dust on her knees. Her mother brought her up the long road to the house at dusk, because Meret had asked that she be brought, and stood in the courtyard twisting her hands. She had been washed in salted water before every evening meal since she was born. She knew the skipping song. She had never been in the house.

There was nothing in the house now. The chamber was bare stone, the grooves empty, the gate swinging in its passage with nothing to open or close. Every name Meret could still hear, she heard only in her own head. But the basin was there, and the cup, and the dark.

Meret knelt in front of the child by the brine basin.

“Give me your hands,” she said.

The girl held them out. Meret took them, small and dusty, and turned them over, and pressed her thumb into the center of each palm, one and then the other, the way a potter tests clay.

Then, while she was still deciding how to begin, the child took one hand back and, without being asked, without being taught, laid it flat on the stone floor beside the basin. Palm down. Pressing. Steadying something.

Nen.

Meret looked at the small hand on the stone for a long time.

She knew what it meant. She had seen it a thousand times, and she had seen it in herself. Seven of seven, if there had been anything to hear with. All of it waiting in the child like water under the sand, and nothing in the walls to answer. Not now. Not for a very long time.

She didn’t cry. She had learned that from him.

“Good,” she said quietly. “Good. Now. Into the water.”

She guided the small hands into the basin, and the child gasped at the cold and the salt and then laughed. Meret washed them, slowly, front and back, between each finger, the way her own hands had been washed thirty-four years ago by an old woman whose name she had never learned.

“Again,” she said. “Three times. So that you will be the same as me.”

The child put her hands back into the cold, and this time she did not gasp.

Next · Part Two · Chapter OneThe Level Stones

Part TwoThe Waters

Part Two · Chapter One

The Level Stones

THE LEVEL STONES OF THE HOUSE OF VANN: …seven markers of grey sandstone, each cut with a single notch and a single sign, kept by one family of water-workers at Yaśodharapura for at least eleven generations. The sign beside each notch is identical in every stroke to a mark cut into the reservoir walls of the Giza plateau some three and a half thousand years before. No route of transmission is known. This Concordance proposes one, and it is not a road. Water-masters forget why they cut a mark long before they forget where…

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 1

15 min read

They burned his father on the dike of the western water, because his father had asked for it.

It was not the usual place. The usual place was the field below the temple of the ancestors, where the smoke went up among the sugar palms and the monks could chant in the shade. But Kosal had kept the sluices of the western water for forty-one years, and he had said, more than once, in the half-joking way that old men say the things they mean most, that he wanted to go up from the place where he’d stood every evening of his life. So they built the pyre on the broad top of the dike, where the laterite was red under the grass, and the smoke went out over the water.

Vann stood with his mother and watched it go.

The western water was enormous. It always surprised him, even now, even after a lifetime. It was sixteen thousand cubits long and four thousand wide, a lake made by men, held up above the plain by dikes of earth and laterite so long that from the middle of one you couldn’t see either end. At this hour, in the dry season, it lay perfectly still, and the sky was in it, and the smoke from his father’s pyre lay in it too, a grey line drawn across the pink. Far out in the center, on its little island, the old temple of the west stood up out of its own reflection. You could just see the towers. Four of them, one at each side of the island, square to the four directions, and in the middle the low shrine where the bronze god lay on his side in the water.

“He’d have liked this,” his mother said. “Look how still it is.”

“He’d have said the inflow was a finger high,” said Vann, and she laughed, and then cried, and he put his arm around her.

His father’s things were few. A good knife. A measuring cord of palm fiber, knotted every cubit, stiff with years of mud. A sun-hat. A bronze bowl. And, wrapped in an old cloth at the bottom of the chest where he kept his work clothes, a wooden box.

His mother put it in Vann’s hands on the third day after the burning.

“These are yours now,” she said.

He knew what they were before he opened it. He had seen them all his life. Seven stones of grey sandstone, each about the size of a child’s fist, each shaped like a small squat post with a flat base and a rounded top. On the face of each one, someone, long ago, had cut a single notch: in the first stone low, near the base, and in each one a little higher, until on the seventh it was very nearly at the top. Beside each notch was the same small sign, three short strokes and a hook, worn soft by handling.

There was also a strip of very old palm-leaf, folded and refolded until it was soft as cloth. On it were a few lines of writing in a script he couldn’t read.

“His father gave them to him,” his mother said. “And his father before. Back and back. They came from across the sea, Kosal used to say, with the first of the family, from the country in the west where the Brahmins come from. But that’s what everyone says about anything old.”

“I know what they’re for,” said Vann.

“Do you?”

“The listening levels.” He turned the first stone over in his palm. “On the dark of the moon he’d go out at dusk and set the western water to one of these. A different one each month. And then he’d wash his hands and go out on the dike and stand there half the night.”

“And what did he hear?”

Vann thought about it honestly. “Frogs.”

His mother smiled. “Yes. That’s what he said too.” She closed the lid of the box over his hands. “He did it for forty-one years. Every dark moon. Even when he was sick. Even the month after you were born, when I wanted him at home. And every time he came back in the middle of the night smelling of salt, and lay down next to me, and slept like a child.” She looked at Vann. “I never understood it. But I never once saw him happier than when he came back from the dike.”

Vann was an engineer of the king’s water.

He was thirty-four years old, and he was good at it, and he knew he was good at it without needing to say so. He had been trained under the great acharya Madhava, the surveyor who had laid out half the new city after the Chams had burned the old one, and he could take the level of a dike from three thousand cubits away with a water-trough and a pair of sighting rods and be wrong by less than a fingernail. For five years now he had been on the king’s works at the new northern water, the one the king was making beside his great new temple of victory, and he spent his days in the sun among ten thousand laborers, shouting.

The king was building everything at once. Everyone said so. He was old, and he had been at war half his life, and when he had driven the Chams out of the city he had come home and begun to build as if he were afraid there wasn’t enough time. Temples. Roads. Rest houses every fifteen thousand paces along every road. A hundred hospitals, with physicians and medicines paid from the royal store, because the king had said in an inscription that the suffering of his people’s bodies was the suffering of his own. People loved him for that. Vann loved him for that, in a distant way, the way you love the rain.

And water. The king was building water.

The northern water was being dug to the old measures. That was the king’s order, and Madhava’s pride. In the royal library there were palm-leaf texts, very old, copied and recopied from the times of the first kings, that gave the proportions of a sacred reservoir: so long, so wide, so deep, its corners true to the four directions, an island in its center with a shrine, and in the shrine a pool, and around the pool four spouts in the shapes of four creatures, so that the water would come out of them at each of the four sides. Nobody knew why the spouts. Nobody knew why the island. Madhava didn’t care.

“The ancients knew what they were doing,” he said to Vann on the morning Vann came back to the works, three days after the burning. They were standing on the half-finished eastern dike in the heat, looking down at a thousand men carrying baskets of earth up from the bed. “We don’t need to know why. We need to build it exactly as they did. If the measure is exact, the virtue is in it.”

“And if they were wrong?”

Madhava gave him a look of great tolerance. The acharya was a small, precise, delighted man with a long grey topknot and ink always on his fingers, and he had been patient with Vann for fifteen years.

“Then we will be exactly as wrong as the first kings,” he said, “which is very good company.” He clapped Vann on the shoulder. “I was sorry to hear about your father. A good man. I met him once. He told me the western water was a finger high at the inflow, and he was right. I went and looked.”

Vann laughed in spite of himself.

“He had stones,” he said. “Level stones. Did you know? Family stones, for the western water. He set the lake to them on the dark of the moon. Old marks. He used to say they were the heights at which the water listens.”

“Listens to what?”

“He never said. I don’t think he knew.”

“Ah.” Madhava nodded kindly. “There are many such things in the old families. My grandmother would not let a pot be set down facing west. It’s a way of honoring the dead, Vann. It doesn’t need to be anything else.”

“No,” Vann said. “I know.”

He did know. He had known since he was nine years old.

He had gone with his father once. Just once.

He remembered it very clearly: a night at the end of the rains, the air thick and warm and full of insects, no moon at all. His father had taken him by the hand and walked him up onto the dike in the last of the light, and they’d stood at the sluice-gate while his father opened and closed the old timber doors a hand’s breadth at a time, watching a carved post set in the water beside the gate. Vann remembered the post. It was old, older than the dike, nearly black, with lines cut into it at intervals, and beside the lines the same small sign that was on the stones: three strokes and a hook. His father had held the fourth stone up beside the post, squinting, and closed the door a little more, and waited, and opened it a little, until the water stood exactly at the notch.

Then he had taken a jar from his shoulder bag, and a bowl, and poured water into the bowl, and stirred salt into it from a twist of leaf, and washed his hands in it three times. Then he’d washed Vann’s.

“Why three?” Vann had asked.

“Because that’s how many.”

“But why?”

His father had thought about it. He was a slow, careful man who always thought before he answered, even when the answer was I don’t know. “My father said, so that every man who stands here is the same. So that what I feel tonight is what he felt, and what you’ll feel.”

“What did he feel?”

“I don’t know,” Kosal had said. “He never said.”

Then he’d taken Vann out to the very end of the dike, where a flight of old sandstone steps went down into the water, and they’d sat on the lowest dry step with their bare feet in the lake, and their wet hands flat on the stone, and they’d listened.

Vann had heard frogs. He’d heard the insects, and the slap of a fish somewhere out in the dark, and the long slow seep of the water through the dike under them, a faint trickling that came up through the stone. He’d heard his father breathing. After a while he’d gotten bored, and then cold, and then fallen asleep with his head against his father’s arm, and when he woke the stars had moved a long way and his father was still sitting exactly as before, with his palms flat on the step and his eyes closed and an expression on his face that Vann had never forgotten and never understood. It was the face of a man waiting for a boat.

“Did you hear it?” Vann had asked him on the walk home.

“Hear what?”

“Whatever it is.”

“No,” his father had said. And then, after a while, gently, as if it were the same answer: “Maybe next month.”

He should have put the stones on the shrine shelf with his father’s ashes and forgotten them. He knew that. Instead, the evening after he’d talked to Madhava, he took them down to the western water.

He told himself it was a test. He was an engineer. He could take the level of a lake to a fingernail. If the stones were only superstition, the heights would be meaningless, random notches cut by some ancestor with a knife and a feeling. If they meant anything, they would mean it in numbers.

He found the old carved post beside the sluice-gate, still there, black with age, half sunk in the mud at its foot. He took the measuring cord and a water-trough and his sighting rods, and he spent two evenings measuring. He measured each notch on each stone against its base. He measured each line on the post against the sill of the gate. He measured the post against the crest of the dike, and the dike against the old benchmarks the first builders had left, and he did it the way Madhava had taught him, three times, from three directions, writing every figure on a slate.

The notches on the stones were not random.

He had expected them to be close to the lines on the post, because his father had used the one to set the other. That wasn’t what surprised him. What surprised him was the spacing. The seven heights did not go up evenly, like the steps of a stair. They went up the way the holes go up along a flute, the first ones far apart and then closer and closer, so that the seventh was crowded in very near the sixth. When he worked the ratios on his slate, sitting on the dike with the sun going down, they came out as clean as a temple plan. Each height was to the one before it as a few small whole numbers are to each other.

Somebody had cut these to a rule.

Not a builder’s rule. He knew all the builder’s rules. This was a rule for something that wasn’t strength or beauty or holding up a wall.

And then, because he was thorough, he walked out along the dike in the last of the light to the old sandstone steps at the end where he’d sat with his father as a boy, and he looked at the stone of the steps themselves. And there, on the riser of each step, very worn, almost invisible unless the light came at a slant, was a line. Seven steps. Seven lines. And beside each line, three strokes and a hook.

The steps were older than the dike. He could see that from the way the laterite of the dike had been built up against them. The steps had been here first. Whoever had cut these lines had cut them before there was a western water at all, when this was only a low place in the plain where the rains pooled, or a river, or something else, something earlier, that he had no word for.

He sat down on the lowest dry step with the slate on his knees.

That night he took the palm-leaf strip to a man he knew in the library of the royal temple, an old reader of the ancient scripts named Achar Som, who was half blind and drank too much and could read anything.

Som held the strip very close to the lamp for a long time.

“It’s not Khmer,” he said. “It’s not Sanskrit, either, not properly. It’s an old way of writing from the west, from across the sea, the kind the first Brahmins brought. I’ve seen it on a few stones. Very few.” He squinted. “Where did you get it?”

“My father.”

“Hm.” Som traced the lines with a finger that shook. “I can give you some of it. Not all. Some of these signs I’ve never seen anywhere. This one.” He tapped the three strokes and a hook. “This one everyone knows. That’s auspicious. Fortunate. You see it on boundary stones.” He went on squinting. “This line says, more or less, These are the heights at which the water listens. That’s what your father told you, yes? And then, Keep them. Set the water to them on the nights without a moon. Wash three times. Stand wet.” He frowned. “And then something I don’t follow. In the west, in the dry country, the houses are built tall and drink from the sky. Here we build wide and drink from the ground. The houses are different. The song is one.”

“What song?”

“It doesn’t say.” Som handed the strip back, carefully. “Old families keep old instructions. Half the families in this city have a strip of leaf like this, telling them which day to plant beans and which direction to face when they sneeze. Your family’s tells them how to set a lake.” He shrugged. “It’s a better instruction than most.”

“Is it nonsense?”

The old reader looked at him for a while in the lamplight.

“I’ve read nine thousand palm-leaves in my life,” he said. “Most of them are nonsense. But nonsense doesn’t usually have such good arithmetic.” He poured himself more palm wine. “Why are you asking me? You’re the engineer. You measured them, didn’t you? I can see it on your face.”

“They’re cut to a rule,” said Vann.

“Then somebody knew something,” said Som. “And now nobody does. That’s the most ordinary thing in the world. Go home.”

He went home. He put the box of stones on the shelf above his sleeping mat, beside the little bronze bowl that had been his father’s, and lay down, and didn’t sleep.

He thought about the king’s new water to the north, with its exact proportions copied from the oldest leaves, its island and its pool and its four spouts, that nobody knew the reason for. He thought about Madhava saying the virtue is in the measure. He thought about the seven lines on the seven steps, older than the dike, that no one had walked down in three hundred years except his father, alone, once a month, to sit with his hands on the stone and wait.

He did not believe that the water listened. Water did not listen. Water went downhill, and seeped through laterite, and stood at the height you set it to. He knew that the way he knew his own name.

But somebody, very long ago, had believed it enough to cut seven heights to a rule, and carry the rule across the sea, and give it to his son, and tell him to wash three times and stand wet. And his father had kept it for forty-one years without knowing anything at all.

You could throw away a superstition. People did it every day. Vann found that he couldn’t throw away arithmetic.

Twelve days later the moon went dark.

He told himself he was only going to check the level. That was all. The inflow had been high in the spring, and his father wasn’t there anymore to say so, and someone ought to look.

He went out at dusk with the box under his arm. He opened and closed the sluice doors a hand’s breadth at a time, as he’d watched his father do, until the water stood at the notch on the first stone, to a fingernail, because he could do that better than anyone. Then he sat on the dike for a while in the warm dark, feeling foolish.

Then he took out his father’s bronze bowl and filled it from the lake and stirred a pinch of salt into it from a twist of leaf, and washed his hands in it three times.

He walked out to the steps at the end of the dike and sat on the lowest dry one, and put his feet in the water, and laid his wet palms flat on the stone.

Frogs. Insects. A fish. The long slow seep of the lake through the laterite under him, trickling, faint and steady, coming up through the step like a pulse. Nothing else.

Of course nothing else.

He sat there for a long time anyway. When he got up at last, his legs were stiff and the stars had moved, and he walked home along the dike in the dark smelling of salt. He put the box back on the shelf above his sleeping mat, and lay down, and to his great surprise he slept like a child.

In the morning there was salt dried white in the creases of his palms. He sat on the edge of the mat and looked at it a while before he washed it off.

Next · Part Two · Chapter TwoSokha

Part Two · Chapter Two

Sokha

The houses of the wet countries did not reach for the sky. They spread across the ground and made themselves into lakes. Where the desert house had a mouth, the water house had a skin: miles of it, laid flat under the rain, so that the whole country could be listened to at once. The dancers of those houses were not decoration. They were the operators, and their dances were the posture practice, done standing up.

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 2

15 min read

The northern water was filled in the month of the first rains, on a day the astrologers had chosen with great care, and the king came to see it.

Vann saw him from the sluice. He was very old and very thin, and he was carried on a gilded litter under nine white parasols, and when the litter was set down on the southern dike he didn’t get out of it. He sat in the shade with his hands folded and his eyes half closed, the way old men watch something they have waited a long time for, and ten thousand people stood in the heat on the dikes and in the empty bed of the reservoir and watched him watch it.

Madhava was beside himself. He had been up since before dawn checking the gate-sills with a plumb line. He had checked them four times.

“When the conch sounds,” he said to Vann for the eleventh time, “you open the first door. One hand’s width. Not more. Then the second, when the drums change. Then the third. Slowly. The water should come in like a guest, not like a flood. That’s what the leaf says.”

“I know what the leaf says.”

“I know you know.” Madhava wiped his face with his sleeve. “Humor an old man.”

Below them, in the dry bed of the reservoir, on a platform of new sandstone built out around the island shrine, the dancers were waiting.

There were forty of them. Vann had seen the royal dancers before, at a distance, at festivals, and he had liked them the way everyone liked them, the way you like fireworks. Today he was close enough to see their faces. They were very young, most of them, girls of twelve and fifteen, in gold crowns and tight silk bodices and heavy anklets, with their faces whitened and their eyes lined, standing motionless in the sun in two long rows. At the end of the rows stood a tiny old woman in plain white with her grey hair cropped short, holding a staff.

As he watched, two attendants brought a big clay jar to the platform and set it down, and one by one the dancers came to it and dipped their hands in and washed them. Front and back. Between the fingers. Once, and again, and a third time. The old woman watched every one.

Vann felt the hair go up on his arms.

“What are they doing?” he said.

“Purifying,” said Madhava. “For the god.”

“Three times?”

“It’s always three times. Every dancer in the city. They do it before every performance. You’ve seen it.” Madhava looked at him curiously. “What’s the matter?”

“Nothing,” said Vann.

The conch sounded.

He opened the first door a hand’s width.

The water came in from the canal below the gate, brown and quick with the first rain, and spread out across the bottom of the reservoir in a thin sheet, glittering. A sigh went up from the crowd. On the platform, the drums began, and the dancers began to move.

He had never really looked at it before. He looked at it now, with one hand on the great timber lever of the second door, because there was nothing else to do until the drums changed.

It was very slow. That surprised him. From a distance, at festivals, it had seemed all flicker and glitter. Up close it was slow, and grave, and strange. The dancers did not leap or spin. They stood with their knees bent and their weight low and their bare feet flat and spread on the stone, and they moved their hands. Only their hands, at first. A palm turned down, pressing toward the ground. A palm turned up, open to the sun. Fingers spread. A long slow wave of the arm, like water. A hand lifted and turning in the air. Fingertips pressed together. And then both hands open and held perfectly still, as if listening.

Seven. He counted them without meaning to. Seven gestures, over and over, in different orders, flowing one into another.

The drums changed. He opened the second door.

And as the water came through, faster, louder, one dancer faltered.

It was nothing. Nobody else would have seen it. She was at the end of the front row, a little taller than the others, older too, perhaps twenty. Her hand had been lifting into the turning gesture, and as the second door opened and the water came roaring through the gate below him, her hand stopped in the air, just for a breath, as if she’d been struck. Her head turned, very slightly, toward the gate. Toward him.

Then she was moving again, perfectly in time, and nobody had seen.

He opened the third door when the drums told him, slowly, slowly, as the leaf said. The water came in like a guest. The old king, under his nine parasols, opened his eyes and lifted one hand, and the crowd roared, and the dancers sank to the stone with their palms flat on it, and it was done.

She came and found him that evening.

He was still on the dike, because the reservoir had to be watched through its first night; a new dike could slump if it took the water badly, and Madhava trusted no one else. The crowds were long gone. The sky was going violet and the water in the new reservoir lay shallow and still across the whole wide bed, catching the color, and the frogs had already found it.

He heard her bare feet on the laterite before he saw her. She’d washed off the white face and taken off the crown, and she was wearing a plain cotton wrap, and she looked younger and much less certain than she had on the platform.

“You’re the one who opened the gate,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You opened the second door too fast.”

He blinked at her. “I opened it exactly when the drums changed.”

“I know. It wasn’t the drums. It was the water.” She came and stood beside him and looked down at the gate, where the river water was still trickling through the half-open third door into the new lake. “When the second door opened it went sour. Just for a moment. The blue went” (she made a small gesture with her hand, a kind of crumpling) “muddy. Like someone singing a note and their voice breaking.”

Vann had been an engineer for fifteen years. He had heard a great many people tell him a great many things about water. He found that he didn’t know what to say to this.

“The blue,” he said.

“The water’s blue.” She said it as though it were obvious. “Not the color you see. The color it is. Running water’s always blue to me. Still water too, but deeper. When it’s right it’s like…” She stopped. “You think I’m mad.”

“No.”

“Yeay Mom says I mustn’t tell people. She says they’ll think I’m possessed, and then the temple will send me away.” She sat down on the edge of the dike with her feet hanging over the water and looked out at the new reservoir. “But you opened the gate, so you ought to know. It’s nobody’s fault. The leaf said a hand’s width at a time. The leaf doesn’t know the water.”

He sat down too, a little way from her.

“What’s your name?”

“Sokha. I dance for the temple of victory. Or I will, when it’s finished.” She glanced at him. “You’re Vann. Kosal’s son. From the western water.”

“How do you know that?”

“Everyone at the western water knew your father. He used to sit on the old steps at the dark of the moon. I went out there sometimes when I was little, with my grandmother. We’d see him there, on the steps, with his hands on the stone.” She looked down at her own hands in her lap. “My grandmother said he was listening. I asked her to what. She said, the same thing we are.”

They talked until the stars were out. He didn’t know afterward how it happened. He was not a man who talked easily, and she had been told all her life not to.

She told him about the colors.

She had always had them. As long as she could remember, sounds had colors, and some sounds had tastes, and water had a sound that was not the sound of splashing but something under it, deeper, that came up through her feet when she stood barefoot near it. Drums were red and brown. The conch was a kind of amber. Her teacher’s voice was green. The sound of the water at the gate today, when it was right, had been blue, a living, moving blue, and the sour moment had been like mud stirred into it.

“And there are names,” she said. “For the colors. They come with them. Yeay Mom taught us the names in a song when we were little, all the girls, but for the others it’s only a song. For me the names are the colors.” She hummed, under her breath, very low. “Nen. That’s the red-brown. The drums, and the ground, and the laterite under us.” She hummed higher. “Sa. Amber. The conch. The sun. Ri is green. Hu is the water. Blue.”

“And the others? You said seven.”

“There are seven in the song. Nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef, ol.” She sang it quickly, a skipping rhythm, the way children sing a counting game. “We sang it jumping over a rope. But I only really hear four. Mai sometimes, very faint, at dusk, like now. Violet. Tef almost never. Once in a storm I heard tef, all silver, and it frightened me. And ol never. Yeay Mom says her teacher’s teacher said that ol was all the others at once, the color past the edge of colors, and that the old dancers could see it. I’ve never seen it. I don’t think anyone has for a long time.”

“Can Yeay Mom?”

“Yeay Mom?” Sokha laughed, softly, and then stopped laughing. “Yeay Mom has never heard anything in her life. No colors. No names. Nothing. She told me once. She was so ashamed, telling me, I wished I hadn’t asked.” She was quiet a moment. “She’s the best teacher in the city. She knows every gesture, every step, every count, exactly as it was taught to her, and her teacher before her. She makes us wash three times in salt water before every dance, and drink a cup of it, and she counts while we dance, and she can tell from across a courtyard if a girl’s palm is a finger’s width wrong. She’s never once been wrong about it. She doesn’t know why any of it is. She just knows that it is.”

“Why do you drink salt water?”

“Because Yeay Mom says.” She shrugged. “So we’ll be the same as the ones who danced before us. That’s what she says. So we’ll be the same as the first dancer.”

Vann looked down at his own hands.

“My father said that,” he said. “When he washed. So that every man who stands here is the same.”

She turned and looked at him in the dark.

He showed her the stones the next evening.

He hadn’t meant to. But she came again, at dusk, to the dike of the northern water, carrying a parcel of rice and fish wrapped in a banana leaf, which she said was for the watchman since he obviously hadn’t eaten, and when they’d eaten it he found himself telling her about the box, and the palm-leaf strip, and the seven lines on the seven steps at the end of the western dike. And then he found himself fetching the box from where he’d left it with his things in the watchman’s hut, because he’d been carrying it with him everywhere since the dark moon, and he didn’t want to think about why.

She knelt over the box in the light of the little oil lamp and looked at the stones for a long time without touching them.

“That sign,” she said finally. She touched the three strokes and a hook with one finger. “Yeay Mom has that sign on the salt jar. Cut into the clay, under the lip. It’s on all the dancers’ jars. It’s on the floor of the old dancing hall at the western temple, where the first girl stands.”

“It means auspicious. Fortunate.”

“That’s what she says it means.” Sokha picked up the first stone and held it in her palm. “What do they do?”

“They’re heights. The heights to set a lake to. My father used them for the western water. Each one is a level.” He found himself explaining it as if to Madhava, because he didn’t know any other way to explain anything. “They’re cut to a rule. They aren’t evenly spaced. The spacing is like the holes on a flute. Each height is to the next as small whole numbers. Somebody cut them very carefully, a very long time ago.”

She was holding the first stone in one hand with her eyes closed.

“Nen,” she said quietly.

“It’s a stone. It doesn’t have…”

“Not the stone. The height.” She opened her eyes. “You said it’s a level. A height of the water. Heights have colors. When the water stands at this height,” she touched the notch, “it would be nen. I don’t know how I know. I just know. Like I know the drums are red.” She put it down and picked up the second. “Sa.” The third. “Ri.” The fourth. She held it a long time. “Hu. This one’s blue. This is the one where the water would be most like itself.”

“That’s the one my father used most,” said Vann. “In the rains. The fourth.”

She picked up the fifth, and the sixth, and frowned at each one, and put them down. “Mai, I think. And tef. But I can’t feel them properly.” The seventh she didn’t pick up at all. She only looked at it.

“Ol,” she said.

They sat for a while without saying anything. The frogs were very loud.

“I’ve heard it,” Sokha said at last. “The western water. Not here. This one doesn’t sing at all. It’s the right blue but it’s dead inside, like a painted lake. But the western water, sometimes, in the rains. If I stand on the old steps barefoot at night. Very far away, very faint, so faint I’m never sure. Like music in someone else’s house, through a wall. All the names together. Rising and falling, slow.” She looked at the box of stones. “I always thought it was the rain. Or me. Yeay Mom said not to tell anyone.” She looked at the seventh stone, which she still hadn’t touched. “Some nights I hope it’s me. If it’s only me, they can send me away and I can stop listening. If it’s real, then somebody has to keep standing on those steps, and there’s nobody but me.”

“Why doesn’t this one sing?” He gestured at the new reservoir, wide and still and perfect under the stars. “It’s built to the old measures. Exactly. Madhava checked everything four times. It’s the most perfect water in the kingdom.”

“I don’t know. I’m a dancer.” She looked out at it. “But it’s like when a new girl learns the dance from watching. She can get every gesture right. Every count. Yeay Mom can’t find anything wrong. And it’s still dead. You can see it from across the courtyard. Something isn’t going through.” She hesitated. “Maybe your lake learned it from watching.”

He thought of Madhava saying, If the measure is exact, the virtue is in it. He thought of the king under his parasols opening his eyes and lifting his hand, and ten thousand people cheering a perfect lake that didn’t sing.

“Maybe it did,” he said.

She danced for him once, before the rains ended. Not the whole dance. A part of it. She said there was a part at the end that Yeay Mom made them practice more than anything and that no one ever saw, because it was danced only at dusk, in the temple, with the doors shut.

They were on the old steps at the end of the western dike, at the place where his father used to sit. She washed her hands three times in his father’s bronze bowl, and drank a mouthful of the salted water and made a face, and stood barefoot on the lowest dry step with her feet spread and her knees bent and her weight low, and she began.

It was only a few gestures, and they were very slow, and Vann, who measured heights for a living, saw that her hands did not stop just anywhere. They stopped at stations, the same stations the hands of the old friezes stopped at, and each station was a place on her own body. The stations weren’t evenly spaced. They were spaced the way the heights on his father’s stones were spaced, by some rule that wasn’t a ruler’s, and he found he was measuring the gaps against his own fingers without meaning to.
