Palms pressed down toward the earth, low, at the root of the spine, her soles spread flat on the stone: nen. Sun-hand open at the lower belly: sa. Fingers spread at the hollow under the ribs where the dancers said fire lived: ri, green in her mind though the stone was grey. A slow wave across the heart: hu, the river. Hand lifting and turning at the throat: mai. Fingertips together at the brow, between the eyes: tef.

Then her fingers spread again, ri, and the hand began to rise. It rose partway, and stopped. It didn’t reach anything. It stayed in the air, open, just short of arrival.

Then both hands opened above the crown of her head and went still.

She held the stillness for a long time. Long enough that Vann began to count his heartbeats. He’d counted twenty before she let her hands fall.

“That’s the end,” she said. “That’s the end of every dance. The last part’s the stillness. Yeay Mom counts it. If a girl moves before the count, she has to do the whole dance again.”

“What does it mean?”

“Yeay Mom says nobody knows. Her teacher said it was the oldest part, older than the temples, and you mustn’t change a single finger.” Sokha sat down on the step beside him and put her feet in the water. “She told me once that her teacher’s teacher said it was a question. What you say at a door, at night, when you’ve been waiting a long time.” She looked at her own open hands. “And the stillness at the end is where the answer goes. That’s why you can’t move. If you move, there’s nowhere for it to land.”

“Has anyone ever answered?”

“No,” said Sokha. “That’s why we keep dancing it.”

The western water lay all around them in the dark, sixteen thousand cubits of it, flat and unmoving. Far out on its island the four towers of the old temple stood square to the four directions against the stars. Vann sat with his feet in the lake beside the strangest person he had ever met, and listened as hard as he had ever listened to anything.

Frogs. The seep through the laterite. Her breathing.

“Is it singing?” he said. “Now?”

She was quiet for a long time, with her palms flat on the wet step.

“Very far,” she said. “I think. I’m never sure.” And then, as if it were the same answer, and he heard his father in it so clearly that his throat closed: “Maybe next month.”

Next · Part Two · Chapter ThreeThe Drought

Part Two · Chapter Three

The Drought

A famine can be counted. A silence cannot.

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 3

15 min read

The rains came late the next year, and then they came thin, and then in the month when they should have been heaviest, they stopped.

At first nobody worried. Rains were late sometimes. The old people said so; the astrologers said so; the farmers in the eastern districts transplanted their rice from the seedbeds into the paddies as they always did, in the month they always did, because the seedlings would die if they waited. The sky over the city went white with heat and stayed white. Clouds came up in the afternoons, enormous, purple-bellied, and thunder walked around the edges of the plain all night, and in the morning there was no rain.

Vann watched the western water go down.

He went out every evening to the old carved post beside the sluice and read it. In the first dry month the water fell a finger. In the second, a hand. By the middle of the third it had dropped below the line that matched his father’s fifth stone and was creeping down toward the fourth. Out on its island, the old temple of the west stood higher out of its reflection every week, and the steps at the end of the dike, where he and Sokha sat on the dark of the moon, came up out of the lake one by one, dry and pale, with their seven worn lines showing.

The northern water, the king’s new one, was nearly empty. It had never been full. It had been dug to the old measures and filled from a river that had stopped running, and now it was a wide shallow plate of cracked mud with a puddle in the middle around the island, where the four stone spouts stood dry with their mouths open. The eastern water, the old one, was worse; it had been silting for a hundred years and nobody had cleared it, and it was a marsh full of herons.

The western water was the only one left.

“You’ll have to open it,” Sokha said.

They were sitting on the steps. It was the dark of the moon again, the third time since the rains failed, and they had washed and wet their feet and sat with their palms on the stone for half the night, as they had every dark moon for more than a year now. She heard nothing. She’d heard nothing since the rains stopped. The water was too low, she said, and the air too dry, and the whole country felt to her like a drum with a split skin.

“Not yet,” said Vann.

“Soon.”

“Not yet.”

She looked at him in the dark. He could feel her looking.

“I went to my mother’s village last week,” she said. “In the east. Past the old water. The paddies are cracking. The seedlings are yellow at the tips. My uncle’s been carrying water from the well in jars, one jar at a time, out to the plants nearest the house. My cousins are carrying it. The little ones.” She stopped. “They’ll lose the whole crop if they don’t get water in the next month. All of them. All the eastern villages. You know that.”

“I know.”

“Then you’ll have to open it.”

“I know,” said Vann. “I know that. I’m the one who’ll have to do it.” He took his hands off the stone. “Do you know what happens when I open it? Not a hand’s width. All the way. Every door, for a month. It’ll fall below the fourth line in ten days. Below the first in a month. In two months there won’t be a western water. There’ll be a field of mud, sixteen thousand cubits long, with a temple sticking up out of it. And it won’t come back for years. Not until there are three good rains in a row.” He looked out at the dark lake. “And the steps will be dry. All seven lines.”

“Yes,” said Sokha.

“And you’ve never heard it properly. Not once. Not all of it.”

“No.”

“You said once, if it stood at the right line on the right night, you thought you might.”

“I said I thought.” She was quiet for a while. “I don’t know, Vann. I’ve never known. Maybe it’s the rain. Maybe it’s me. Maybe it’s my grandmother’s stories and I hear what I want.” She put her hand on his arm. “But I know about my cousins carrying water in jars. I don’t have to wonder about that.”

He didn’t answer.

“You can’t keep a lake full so a dancer can listen to it,” she said gently. “Not while children carry water to rice one jar at a time. You know you can’t.”

“I know,” he said.

He did know. He knew it the way he knew that water went downhill. That was the worst of it. She was right, and he was going to do it, and the only person in the whole country who might once have been able to tell anyone what the western water was for was sitting beside him telling him to let it go.

The council met in the hall of the royal water office, a long low building of timber and red tile beside the palace, in the white heat of noon.

The king was not there. He was very ill that season, people said; he lay in the palace with his physicians and was not to be troubled. In his place sat his minister of the granaries, the mratan Narendra, a heavy, grey, careful man from the eastern districts who had been born in one of the villages that were now drying out, and everybody knew it.

Madhava was there, with his slates and his ink-stained fingers. The royal astrologer was there, in saffron, with his charts. The king’s chaplain was there, an old Brahmin in white, whose family had come from across the western sea seven generations before and who still recited the rain rites in the old tongue. And Vann was there, at the end, because he was the engineer of the western water now, his father’s post, and the western water was the only thing left to talk about.

“Tell me the rice,” said Narendra.

His granary clerk read it. Thirty-one thousand households in the eastern and southern districts dependent on the rains and the western canals. Seedlings transplanted, as usual, in the month of transplanting. Seven parts in ten now dry. If water reached them within twenty days, most could be saved. If it reached them within forty, perhaps half. After that, nothing. Last year’s granaries already low, because of the king’s works, because ten thousand men digging reservoirs must be fed. If the eastern crop failed, there would not be enough rice in the kingdom to carry the eastern districts to the next harvest.

“So,” said Narendra. “Children will die. Not a few. Many. Unless the water reaches them.” He turned his heavy grey head. “Acharya?”

Madhava stood. He was precise and quick, and he was entirely right, and Vann loved him and wanted to throw something at him.

“The western water stands at a level sufficient to supply the eastern canals for thirty-four days at full release,” he said, reading from his slate. “I’ve measured it four times. If every door is opened tomorrow, water will reach the furthest eastern villages in nine days. The canals are clear. I’ve had them cleared.” He sat down.

“Astrologer?”

The astrologer began to unroll his charts. Narendra stopped him with two fingers.

“Will it rain in twenty days?”

“Saturn is in the house of drought, lord. At the next new moon but one, perhaps…”

“Perhaps.” Narendra turned to the old chaplain, who had already drawn breath to describe the rain rites. “And nothing has been neglected. I know. I’ve heard the hymns every morning for two months; I live next to the temple.” He said it without irony. He was a man who did not, as far as Vann could tell, have any irony in him. “Engineer. You’re the keeper of the western water. Is there any reason the acharya’s figures are wrong?”

“No, lord. They’re right.”

“Is there any reason not to open it?”

Vann stood. He had thought for three days about what he would say. He had thought he would talk about the stones, and the seven lines on the steps, and the palm-leaf strip with its the houses are different, the song is one. He had thought he might even say Sokha’s name.

He looked at Narendra’s face, and at the clerk with the figures, thirty-one thousand households, and found that he couldn’t.

“No, lord,” he said. “There’s no reason I can count.”

Narendra looked at him for a long moment.

“That’s an odd way to put it,” he said. “Is there a reason you can’t count?”

Nobody had ever asked him that. Vann stood in the white heat of the hall and felt every man in it looking at him, the astrologer and the chaplain and the clerk and Madhava, puzzled and a little worried, the way you look at a sensible man who has suddenly said something strange.

“My family has kept the western water for eleven generations,” he said finally. “There are old heights. Levels. My father set the lake to them on the dark of the moon, every month of his life, and so did his father. Nobody knows why. They’re cut to a rule, lord, a very exact one, I’ve measured it. When I open the doors, the lake will fall below all of them, and it won’t come back for years. And it might be that something is lost that no one knows how to count.” He heard how it sounded. He kept going anyway. “I’m not asking you to hold it. I wouldn’t. I’ve seen the eastern fields. I’m only asking for one thing.”

“Which is?”

“One night.”

It was Sokha who had given him the idea, though she didn’t know it.

He’d been sitting on the steps a week before, after she had gone home, looking at the carved post in the starlight and thinking about the lake going down. And he’d realized, slowly, the way you notice that the sky has changed color, that he had been thinking about it wrong.

He had been thinking of it as a choice between holding the lake and releasing it. Holding it at a line, or letting it go. But the lake wasn’t going to stand still in either case. It was going to fall. Whatever he did, every day, it was going to fall past one line and then the next and then the next, all the way down to the mud. It was going to pass through every one of his father’s heights on its way to nothing.

All he had to do was arrange to pass one of them on the right night.

“The lake stands now a little above the fourth height,” he told Narendra. He’d brought his slate; he held it out, and the minister’s clerk took it to him. “The acharya’s right. If I open every door tomorrow, it’ll pass the fourth height in about four days. But the dark of the moon is in nine. So I’d ask to open the doors partway for the first eight days instead of all the way. Not closed. Partway. The water still runs; the eastern villages still get water from the second day, only less of it at first. On the ninth day I bring the lake exactly to the fourth line, at dusk, and I close the doors for one night. From dusk to dawn. Then I open everything. All the way. Every door, for as long as there’s water.”

Narendra studied the slate in silence. He was, Vann had already guessed, a man who could read figures as easily as letters.

“How much water do the villages lose?” he said finally.

“I’ve worked it,” said Vann. “Over the nine days, about a fifth of what they’d have had at full release. Then nothing more. After the one night, they get everything.”

“A fifth. For nine days.” The minister turned to his clerk. “How many households is that?”

The clerk worked it on his own slate. It took a long time. Nobody spoke.

“Perhaps four hundred, lord,” the clerk said finally. “Four hundred households whose fields reach water some days later than they would have. Of those, perhaps a third might lose part of their crop who otherwise wouldn’t.” He hesitated. “It’s very hard to say. The rains might come. The furthest fields might be lost anyway.”

“Perhaps a hundred and thirty households losing part of a crop,” said Narendra slowly, “for one night of a lake standing at a line that nobody knows the reason for.” He looked up at Vann. “That’s the arithmetic?”

“That’s the arithmetic,” said Vann.

“And what happens on the night? If I give it to you?”

“I don’t know, lord,” said Vann. “Probably nothing. My father sat at that line for forty-one years and as far as I know nothing ever happened. I’ve sat at it for a year and nothing has happened to me.” He took a breath. “But I know someone who thinks she might hear something. If it’s right. And I believe her.”

“Hear what?”

“I don’t know that either.”

The minister sat very still. Madhava was staring at Vann openly now, his mouth slightly open. The chaplain had drawn himself up as if Vann had said something faintly obscene in the old tongue.

“You’re asking me,” said Narendra, “to take rice from a hundred and thirty households in the villages I was born in, in a drought, so that a woman you believe in can listen to a lake.”

“Yes, lord.”

Narendra rubbed his eyes with the heels of both hands, like a farmer at the end of a day.

“I’ll tell you what I think,” he said at last. “I think you’re a sensible man, and the acharya says you’re the best engineer he’s trained, and I think you’ve just said something that isn’t sensible, and you know it isn’t, and you said it anyway in front of all of us. Men don’t usually do that without a reason.” He put the slate down. “I also think that if I give you this, and it comes to nothing, and some child in my mother’s village goes hungry this winter because her family’s field got its water four days late, I’ll know exactly why. And I’ll carry it.” He looked at Vann without any anger at all. “Do you understand that? It isn’t your arithmetic. It’s mine.”

“Yes, lord,” said Vann. His mouth was dry. “I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be sorry. Be right.” Narendra turned to his clerk. “Write it. The engineer of the western water opens the doors partway from tomorrow, by his own figures, for eight days. On the ninth night, he closes them. On the tenth morning he opens them full.” He looked back at Vann. “One night. Then everything. If the rain comes before then, God be thanked. If it doesn’t, I don’t want to hear about levels again.”

“No, lord.”

“And engineer.” The minister’s face did something that was very nearly a smile. “If something happens. On the night. I’d like to know what it was. Not because it would change anything. I’d just like to know what I paid for.”

He told Sokha that evening, on the dike, and she didn’t say anything at all for a long time.

“A hundred and thirty families,” she said finally.

“Perhaps. It might be fewer. The clerk said it was very hard to know.”

“You asked for that. For me.”

“Not for you.” He found it hard to explain. “For whatever it is. For whatever my father was waiting for. For the lines on the steps.” He looked at her. “If you’d said no, I wouldn’t have asked.”

“I didn’t say anything.”

“No,” Vann said. “But you didn’t say no.”

She looked down at her hands.

“What if I don’t hear anything?” she said. “What if I sit there on the right line on the right night and it’s only frogs? What if it was the rain, all those times, and my grandmother’s stories? Then you’ll have taken rice from a hundred and thirty families for a dancer who imagines colors.”

“Then I’ll have been wrong,” said Vann. “And I’ll tell Narendra so. He asked to know.”

“You’d tell him?”

“He paid for it. He has a right to know what he got.” He hesitated. “And I won’t think you imagine colors. Even then.”

“Why not?” She was almost angry. “You can’t hear them. You’ve never heard anything. Not once, all those nights. How can you believe me?”

He thought about that while the frogs went quiet and started again. The lake lay all around them, lower than he’d ever seen it, the seven steps standing up dry and pale at the end of the dike. Far out on the island, the four towers stood square to the four directions against the white night sky.

“Because the stones are cut to a rule,” he said slowly. “Because my father washed three times and your teacher washes three times and neither of them knows why. Because the sign on my stones is the sign on your salt jar. Because you knew which height my father used most before I told you.” He looked at her. “And because when the second door opened too fast, the day we filled the northern water, I saw your hand stop. Before the water reached the platform. Before you could have seen it. You turned toward the gate. You heard it go sour before anyone could have known.” He shook his head. “I can’t hear any of it, Sokha. I never will. But I measure things. And everything I’ve measured says you’re telling the truth.”

She was looking at him in the dark. He couldn’t see her face.

“Yeay Mom will make me drink the salt,” she said finally. Her voice wasn’t steady. “If I tell her I’m listening at the dark moon. She’ll want to do it properly. All of it. The washing and the cup and the counting. She won’t know why. But she’ll want it right.”

“Good,” said Vann. “Let her.”

Then Sokha put her hand, palm up, open, the gesture for sa, on the dike between them. After a while he understood that it was meant for him, and put his own hand in it.

That night he went to Achar Som again, with the palm-leaf strip, because he couldn’t sleep, and because there was one line Som had said he didn’t understand and Vann wanted to hear it again.

The old reader was drunker than usual, and sadder, and pleased to see him. He held the strip close to the lamp.

“In the west, in the dry country,” he read, “the houses are built tall and drink from the sky. Here we build wide and drink from the ground. The houses are different. The song is one.” He squinted. “I’ve been thinking about that since you came. I found another stone in the library, in the same script. Just a few lines. Some of the same words.” He tapped the strip. “This part, after. I read it wrong before. It doesn’t say houses. It says something more like ears. The dry ears have one mouth, at the top, of gold. The wet ears have four, one at each of the four sides, and between them the water, which is a skin.” He put the strip down. “A skin. That’s the word. Like the skin of a drum.”

Vann thought of the four towers on the island, square to the four directions. The four stone spouts at the king’s new water, dry, with their mouths open. The lake itself, flat and wide under the sky, sixteen thousand cubits of it, a skin.

“The dry ears,” he said. “In the west. Do you know where?”

“In the dry country. That’s all it says.” Som poured himself more wine. “A very long way. Older than anyone. Probably sand now.” He looked at Vann over the cup. “Does it matter?”

“I don’t know,” said Vann. “Something is the same. In the west and here. Somebody knew it was the same.” He picked up the strip. “And now the lake’s going to be mud.”

“Then go and listen to it,” said Achar Som, “while it’s still a lake.”

Next · Part Two · Chapter FourOne Night of Music

Part Two · Chapter Four

One Night of Music

There is a kind of hearing open to those who cannot hear. It is how a blind man knows the color of the sky.

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 4

15 min read

For eight days he lived on the dike.

He slept in the watchman’s hut by the sluice when he slept at all, and ate what Sokha brought him, and spent the rest of the time with his hands on the levers and his eyes on the old carved post. He opened the doors partway on the first morning, as he had promised Narendra, and the water went out into the eastern canal brown and slow, and he walked the canal himself for half a day to see it reach the first village. He saw the farmers come out of their houses to look at it. He saw an old woman kneel down in the mud of the ditch and put her hands in it.

Then he went back to the post and began to work.

It was harder than anything he had ever done with water. The lake had to fall, every day, enough to feed the canal. But it had to fall to a rule: not too fast, so that it would pass the fourth line before the dark moon; not too slow, so that the eastern villages got less than he had promised. And the lake was enormous and had a will of its own. When the wind blew from the east it piled the water up against the western dike and the post read high; when it dropped, the water slid back and the post read low. In the hottest part of the day the surface fell by a straw’s thickness from nothing but the sun drinking it. He sat up through two nights working out how much, with Madhava’s old rule for evaporation and a bowl of water left out in the sun, and adjusted the doors by a finger’s width at dawn.

Madhava came out on the fifth day and stood watching him until the shadow of the post had moved a hand’s width along the dike.

“You’re working it to a fingernail,” the acharya said finally. “For eight days. Against the wind and the sun. For one night.”

“Yes.”

“Nobody in the kingdom could do this but you. Do you know that? I couldn’t.” Madhava looked out at the lake. “I don’t understand what you’re doing, Vann. I want you to know that. I think it’s very likely nonsense. But I’ve never seen anyone do anything so well.” He paused. “Your father would have liked to see it.”

“My father did it every month,” said Vann. “For forty-one years. I never once came out to watch him.”

On the ninth day, at dusk, the water stood at the fourth line on the carved post, exactly, to the width of a fingernail. He held the fourth stone up beside the post one last time in the red light, and looked at the notch and the line together, and closed every door.

The sluice went quiet. The canal below it slowed and stilled. For the first time in nine days the western water stood perfectly still, sixteen thousand cubits of it, flat as a polished floor under the last of the light.

He found that his hands were shaking.

Yeay Mom came out on the dike at dark with two of her oldest girls carrying the jar.

She was smaller than Vann had thought, close to. Her head came barely to his chest. Her face was creased all over like old silk, and her eyes were very bright, and she looked at him once, up and down, the way he’d seen her look at the dancers on the platform, as if checking that his palms were the right width.

“So you’re the engineer,” she said. “Sokha says you can’t hear anything.”

“No, grandmother. Nothing.”

“Good. Neither can I.” She turned to the girls. “Set it down. There. On the flat stone.” The jar had the sign cut under its lip, three strokes and a hook. “Bowl.” They gave her a bowl, and she filled it from the jar, and Vann could smell the salt from where he stood. “Sokha. Hands.”

Sokha knelt and held out her hands.

He had seen it done on the platform, among forty dancers, at a distance. It was different up close. The old woman took each of Sokha’s hands in her own and washed it slowly in the salt water, front and back and between each finger, the way you would wash a child, and set it down, and took it up again, and washed it again. Three times. Then she pressed her thumb into the center of each of Sokha’s palms, one and then the other.

“Feet,” she said. “In the lake.”

Sokha stepped off the dike onto the top step and put her feet in the water.

“Cup.”

One of the girls poured a cup from the jar, and Yeay Mom held it while Sokha drank it, all of it, and watched until it was gone. Sokha made the face. Yeay Mom didn’t smile.

“Now him,” she said.

Vann looked at her. “Grandmother, I can’t.”

“I know you can’t. Neither can I. Hands.” She held out her own hands, palm up, sa, and waited.

So he knelt on the dike, and the tiny old woman washed his hands three times in the salt water, and pressed her thumb into his palms, and gave him the cup, and he drank it, and it was bitter and burned his throat, and she watched him until the last of it was gone.

“Why do you do it for me?” he said. “If I can’t hear.”

“Because you’re going,” said Yeay Mom. “And if you’re going, you go the same as her. You go the same as the first one. That’s the only way I know.” She looked out at the dark lake. “I’ve done this nine thousand times. Every dancer. Every night. I have never once known why.” She turned to Sokha. “Go. I’ll count the stillness from here.”

They took his father’s little boat out across the western water to the island.

He rowed. Sokha sat in the bow with her wet hands in her lap and her feet in the bilge-water, very still, saying nothing. There was no moon. The stars were so bright on the flat lake that they rowed between two skies, one above and one below, and the oars broke the lower one into silver every stroke and it healed behind them. Far behind, on the dike, a single small lamp was burning where Yeay Mom sat.

The island temple rose out of the dark ahead of them. Four towers, one at each side, square to the four directions. The water came right up to the base of the old stone walls; it had been higher once, much higher. They tied the boat at the eastern steps and climbed up onto the platform barefoot.

“Not the middle yet,” Sokha whispered. “The sides first. Yeay Mom’s song says the first dancer goes to each of the four doors before she goes to the god.”

So they went to the four towers.

At each one she did the same thing. She stood on the threshold stone, which was wet from the lake, with her feet spread and her knees bent and her weight low, the dancer’s stance, and laid her palms flat against the sandstone of the doorframe, and was still. He stood behind her in the dark and waited.

At the eastern tower she said, very quietly, “Sa. There’s more sa here.” At the southern, “Hu. And nen, underneath, heavy.” At the western, a long silence, and then, “It’s the same. But it’s leaning.” At the northern: “Ri.” She took her hands away from the stone and stood with her eyes closed. “They’re all hearing the same thing. Each door hears it a little differently, like four people standing round a drum. But it’s one sound.” She turned to him in the dark. “It’s like having ears, Vann. Four ears. You could tell where something was. If it came from one side, that door would hear it first.”

“Does it?”

“No. That’s the strange part. Whatever it is, it comes from no side. All four hear it at once.” She was trembling. “It’s coming from everywhere.”

“Is it loud?”

“No. Not yet. It’s through the stone. It’s like hearing it through a wall.” She took his hand. “The god. Come.”

The shrine in the center of the island was a low square room with no roof, open to the sky, and its floor was a pool.

The lake came into it through a channel cut under the eastern wall, so that the pool stood at exactly the height of the lake outside; at the fourth line, tonight, to a fingernail. And in the pool, lying on his side on a bed of stone just under the surface, was the god.

Vann had seen him before, in daylight, from the doorway, the way everyone had. He was bronze, enormous, four times the length of a man, lying on his right side with his head propped on one hand and his eyes half closed, as if he were dreaming the world. The water came up over his shoulder and his hip and lapped at his face. He was very old. Nobody knew who had cast him. There was a hole in his belly, at the navel, and on some nights, the old people said, water came out of it.

Sokha stepped down into the pool. The water came to her knees. She waded to the god’s side and stood there a moment, with her feet planted on the stone bed and the bronze shoulder in front of her, gleaming faintly in the starlight.

Then she laid both her wet palms on the bronze.

He heard her breath go out of her.

It went out of her all at once, like someone hit in the stomach, and then nothing, for so long that he took a step toward the pool. Then he heard her draw a breath again, a long shaking breath, and another.

“Sokha.”

“Wait,” she whispered. “Wait.”

He waited.

“There’s something,” she said. “On my hands. Like ants. Walking. And in my mouth, like a coin. Like biting a coin.” Her voice was very small. “And the colors. Vann. All of them. Nen. Sa. Ri. Hu. It’s all of them, it’s so bright.” She stopped. “Mai. Mai is here. Violet. Like dusk. And…”

She went silent again. He saw her head go back, as if she were looking up, though her eyes were closed.

“Tef,” she said. “Silver. Silver-white, all over everything, like the storm. But it isn’t frightening. It isn’t frightening at all.”

Then nothing, for a long time.

When she spoke again her voice had changed. It was not small anymore. It was steady and full and a little hoarse, the voice of someone standing in a place they had been told about all their life.

“Ol,” said Sokha.

And she began to cry.

She cried for a long time. She didn’t take her hands from the bronze. She stood in the pool to her knees with her palms on the god and her face turned up to the stars and wept, not quietly, the way you weep at the end of a very long journey, and he stood on the edge of the pool in the dark and did not know what to do with himself and did not move.

“It’s music,” she said finally, when she could. “Under everything. It’s what I heard on the steps, through the wall. It’s so slow. It’s all seven at once, rising and falling, like breathing, like the whole country breathing. It’s so big, Vann. It’s bigger than the lake. It’s bigger than…” She couldn’t finish. “And on top of it, there’s a pattern. Quicker. Groups. They come and stop, and come again, and stop. Over and over. Like somebody saying something.”

He found he was kneeling at the edge of the pool. He didn’t remember kneeling.

“Count it,” he said. “I’ll tie it.” He could not hear the bronze. He could only knot.

She was quiet. Then she began.

He had brought his father’s measuring cord. He’d thought about it for nine days, how he’d remember anything she said in the dark when he couldn’t see to write. In the end he’d decided to do what his father would have done. He held the cord across his knees, and as she counted he tied knots in it, by touch: one knot for each name, and for each name a different place along the cubit, so that a knot at the first finger’s width was nen, and at the second sa, and so on up to the seventh. A double knot for the end of a group. A long empty stretch of cord for each silence.

“Seven groups,” Sokha whispered. “Then it starts again. Every group uses all seven names. But in a different order. And the order of the groups is always the same.” She took a breath. “First. Nen. Sa. Ri. Tef.” He tied. “Then the rest of it. Hu, mai, ol.” He tied. “Stop.” A double knot. A silence. “Second. Sa. Hu. Nen.”

He tied all night.

She counted it through four times, from the beginning, all seven groups, so that he could check his knots against her, and he checked them, the way Madhava had taught him to check everything, three times, and found one error and corrected it. By the fourth time through, her voice had gone rough and quiet and she was swaying a little on her feet in the pool, and his fingers had learned the cord so well that he could tie a name without thinking.

“Is it the same every time?” he said. “Exactly?”

“Exactly. Every group. Every silence.” She was quiet. “It doesn’t change. It’s beautiful and it doesn’t change. Like…” She stopped. “Like the dance. The way Yeay Mom does it. Perfect. Every count.”

“Who’s sending it?”

“Nobody.” Her voice was certain. “That’s the thing. That’s what I can’t get past. It doesn’t come from anywhere. The four doors all heard it at once. It’s not a voice in another house. It’s not someone. It’s coming up out of everything. Out of the ground and the water and the sky. It’s the country singing, Vann. Or the world. And the groups are part of the song. They’re the part that sounds like words.” She took a shaking breath. “I don’t know what they’re saying. I don’t think they’re saying anything to us. I think they’ve been saying it since before there were people.”

After a long while, he got up and stepped down into the pool beside her.

The water was cool and came to his knees. He waded to the god, and stood beside her with his feet on the same stone bed, and laid his own wet palms on the bronze, next to hers.

It was cold. Cold and smooth and slick with lake water. He closed his eyes and breathed slowly the way his father had breathed on the steps, and waited, as honestly as he had ever waited for anything.

Perhaps there was something. A faint crawling in the skin of his fingertips, so faint that he couldn’t have said whether it was there at all or only his blood. Perhaps there was a taste, at the very back of his tongue, of metal. Perhaps.

Nothing else. No colors. No names. No music. Only the cold of the bronze and the warmth of her hand beside his, and the frogs, very far away, on the dikes.

He stood there a long time anyway. Then he took his right hand off the god and put it over her left, so that her palm was on the bronze and his palm was on the back of her hand.

“Is it still there?” he said.

“Yes.”

“All of it?”

“All of it.”

“Then I believe you,” said Vann.

He didn’t say it to comfort her. He said it the way he’d have said the lake was at the fourth line: because he’d measured it, and it was true. He could not hear one note of what she heard. He would never hear it. He knew that, standing in the pool with his hand on hers, more surely than he had ever known anything; the knowledge was a kind of grief he hadn’t known was waiting for him. But he had watched her for a year. He had measured the stones. He had felt her hand stop in the air at the gate. And he believed her the way you believe a friend who tells you what is on the other side of a wall you can’t see over: completely, and with your whole weight, because there is no other way to know.

It was the closest he would ever come. He understood that, too. He found that it was very close.

Toward dawn, she took her hands from the god and stepped back from it, and stood alone in the middle of the pool with her feet spread and her knees bent, in the dancer’s stance.

“I want to ask it,” she said.

“Ask what?”

“The end of the dance. The question.” She laughed, a little, shaking. “Yeay Mom always says it’s what you say at a door. I’ve been standing at the door my whole life. I want to say it here. Once.”

So she danced the last part, there in the pool, in the dark, for the god and the lake and the music he couldn’t hear. And this time she sang it too, softly, the names with the gestures.

Hu and sa together, the wave and the open palm braided round each other at her heart. Mai, the hand lifted and turning at her throat. Tef, the fingertips pressed at her brow. Nen, both palms going down past the root of her spine toward the water and pressing there. Then ri, the fingers spread, the hand rising, and the note rising with it, partway, and stopping, not arriving.

Then both her hands open above her crown, and still. She sang nothing for the seventh. The stillness was where ol went.

She held the stillness. Vann counted it with her, his heartbeats, the way Yeay Mom would be counting it on the dike. Twenty. Thirty. He had never seen her hold it so long. Fifty.

Then she let her hands fall, very slowly, and put them back on the bronze.

“Anything?” he said.

She listened a long time.

“No,” she said. “It’s the same. The groups are the same. Exactly the same.” She leaned her forehead against the god’s shoulder. “It didn’t hear me. Or it heard and it doesn’t answer. Or I asked wrong.” She was quiet. “Or it’s not time.”

“Maybe next month,” said Vann, and she laughed, and cried, and he put his arms around her in the pool.

They rowed back at first light. The lake was grey and still. Behind them, the four towers stood up dark against a sky the color of sa, and she sat in the bow and watched them the whole way, and he rowed with the knotted cord coiled in the bottom of the boat between his feet.

Yeay Mom was still sitting on the flat stone by the jar, exactly where they’d left her, with her lamp burned out beside her. She had been there all night. She stood up when they came up the steps, slowly, an old woman’s movements, and looked at Sokha’s face.

Sokha knelt in front of her on the dike, the way a dancer kneels to her teacher at the end of a performance. “Grandmother,” she said. “I heard it. All seven. Ol. The music under everything. All of it.”

The old woman didn’t say anything. Out on the lake a heron lifted off the shallows and settled again further down.

“So it’s real,” she said finally.

“You didn’t know?”

“No.” Yeay Mom looked down at her own hands, small and creased and old, that had washed nine thousand dancers’ hands three times in salt water and never once felt a thing. “No, child. I never knew.” Then she put one hand, palm down, on Sokha’s bowed head, and pressed there, gently, as if to steady something. “I never needed to.”

Behind them on the sluice, the sun came up over the eastern dike. Vann looked at it, and at the carved post, and at the knotted cord in his hands. Then he went down to the levers, because he’d promised Narendra, and opened every door.

Next · Part Two · Chapter FiveThe Choice

Part Two · Chapter Five

The Choice

THE HALL OF DANCERS: …a pillared hall in the eastern part of the temple of victory, completed in the reign of the seventh Jayavarman. Above its doorways runs a frieze of dancing figures, much photographed. Scholars have noted that the dancers’ poses do not follow any known sequence of the classical dance, and that their raised hands and feet fall at heights in an unusually regular ratio, which has been attributed to the carvers’ pattern grid…

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 5

15 min read

It rained the next afternoon.

Not much. A single storm came up out of the south, the kind that had been walking around the edges of the plain for three months without ever coming in, and this one came in. It came across the city in a grey wall with the thunder inside it, and for the time it takes to cook rice the sky opened, and people ran out into the streets and stood in it with their faces up and their mouths open, laughing. Then it went away north, and the sun came out, and the roofs steamed.

Vann measured it. He’d left a bowl out on the dike for that purpose. A hand’s depth, in the time it takes to cook rice. Across the whole plain, that was a great deal of water. Not enough. But a great deal.

By evening everyone in the city knew why it had rained.

The chaplain claimed it for the rain rites and the astrologer for his conjunction. The farmers in the eastern villages said it was the water from the western canal, because when the ground was wet the clouds remembered it. And in the dancers’ quarter behind the royal temple, the girls whispered to each other that Sokha had danced the question at the god on the island on the dark of the moon, and the god had answered.

“Did it?” Vann asked her.

They were on the dike in the evening. The lake had fallen a long way in a single day, with every door open, and the steps at the end stood up out of the water to the third line.

“No,” said Sokha. “You were there. Nothing answered. The groups were the same.” She looked at the wet red laterite of the dike, steaming a little in the last sun. “Yeay Mom slapped one of the little ones for saying it. She said, We don’t dance for rain. We dance because it’s the dance.”

“Then why did it rain?”

“I don’t know, Vann. Because it was going to.” She was quiet. “Maybe the lake leans on the sky a little. Yeay Mom’s teacher used to say the old dancers could feel the weather coming a month away, and the water leaned toward it. But a lean isn’t a push.” She picked up a pebble and threw it into the lake. “Everyone wants it to be because of them. The priests, the astrologer, the girls. Me too. I wanted it to be me so badly last night, when it came. And it wasn’t. I was standing right there. I’d know.”

He looked at her. Of all the people in the city who had claimed the rain, she was the only one who had been on the island with her hands on the god, and the only one saying it wasn’t her.

“That’s why I believe you,” he said.

Narendra sent for him on the third day.

The rain had filled the eastern canals for a while, and the minister’s order came down with it: half the doors on the western water could be closed again, for now; the eastern fields were wet enough to wait. But Vann knew the arithmetic, and so did Narendra, and when Vann came into the long hall of the water office the minister was sitting alone at the low table with the granary clerk’s slates spread out in front of him, and his broad face was very tired.

“The rain bought us twenty days,” Narendra said. “Perhaps twenty-five. Then the late fields need water again, and the eastern canals will be dry, and there will be nothing in the western water but the bottom.” He looked up. “You understand what I mean by the bottom.”

“The deep gate,” said Vann.

“The deep gate.”

The western water had three sets of doors. The upper doors, which he’d been working, fed the canals while the lake stood high. Below them were the second doors. And below those, at the very foot of the dike, beneath even the lowest of the seven lines on the old carved post, was the deep gate: one great timber door set in a laterite culvert, so low that it was under water in every year of Vann’s life, so heavy that it took twelve men to work it. It had been opened twice in living memory, both times in famine. When it was opened, the lake went out of the bottom like water from a cracked jar, all of it, everything below the lowest line, until the bed was dry and the old island temple stood alone on a hill of mud.

“In twenty days,” said Narendra, “I’ll ask you to open it. I wanted to tell you now, so it’s not a surprise.” He set the slates aside. “And I said I’d like to know what I paid for. On the night. Will you tell me?”

So Vann told him. He told it plainly, the way he’d have told the level of a dike: the washing, and the four towers, and the four doors hearing the same thing at once, and the god in the pool, and Sokha’s hands on the bronze, and the seven names, and ol, and the music. The seven groups. The knotted cord. The question at the end, and nothing answering. He told him he had laid his own hands on the bronze and felt, perhaps, ants, and tasted, perhaps, metal, and heard nothing.

Narendra listened without moving.

“I can’t count any of that,” he said when Vann had finished.

“No, lord.”

“But you believe her.”

“Yes.”

The minister said nothing. Somewhere down the hall a clerk dropped a slate, and swore, and picked it up.

“I was born in a village east of the old water,” he said at last. “My grandmother used to wash my hands three times before we ate. In salt. Every meal. I asked her why once and she said, so that you’ll be the same as your grandfather. I thought it was the stupidest thing I had ever heard.” He looked down at his own heavy hands on the table. “I do it still. Every meal. I’ve never told anyone that.” He looked up. “I believe you a little, engineer, because you believe her. I don’t know what that’s worth. It doesn’t change anything. In twenty days I’ll ask you to open the deep gate, and you’ll do it.”

“Yes, lord.”

“Good.” Narendra pulled the slates back toward him. “Then I’d go and do whatever it is you’re going to do with those twenty days. You have the look of a man who’s thought of something.”

He had thought of something. He’d thought of it on the night of the storm, lying awake in the watchman’s hut with the knotted cord across his chest.

The cord was the most precious thing he owned. It was also, he knew, the most fragile. Palm fiber rotted. Knots slipped. In ten years, in the wet season, in a hut by a lake, it would be a stiff black string that no one could read but him; and after him, no one at all. The stones would last longer. But the stones, he knew now, were only half of what his ancestors had carried. They told you the heights. They didn’t tell you what you’d hear at them, or what to do when you got there, or that four doors could listen at once, or that there was a pattern of seven groups that came from nowhere and never changed. His family had kept the stones for eleven generations and forgotten everything else. The dancers had kept the dance and forgotten everything else. Each had kept their piece perfectly, and neither knew that the other existed.

And in twenty days, the lake would be mud.

He had lain awake, and thought about what lasts.

Leaves rot. Cords rot. Lakes go to mud and stay mud for years, and the men who knew how to set them die, and their sons become clerks. Rites go on but forget themselves. Kings change, and the new king has new astrologers. Even stones get lost: a box falls in a river, a house burns, a grandson sells them for a meal.

But nobody pulls down a dancer.

He’d seen the carvers at work at the king’s new temple of victory, the one beside the northern water. There were three hundred of them, working in yards full of sandstone dust, cutting gods and kings and armies and the churning of the sea of milk, and above every doorway in the temple, rows of dancing women, thousands of them, each one a little different. The king wanted the temple full of dancers. When the temple was finished, people would come to it for a thousand years and look at them, and nobody would look closely enough to see what they were holding.

The master carver of the eastern halls was a man named Pich, sixty years old, as brown and dry as a seed, with grey stone dust ground permanently into the creases of his hands and a temper that his apprentices spoke of in whispers. Vann found him in his yard behind the half-built Hall of Dancers, squatting over a block of sandstone with a charcoal stick, drawing.

He listened to Vann without once stopping drawing, until the dancer under his charcoal had a face. Then he put the charcoal down.

“You want me to carve your dancers,” he said. “Instead of mine.”

“Not instead. In the same place. Above the eastern doors. You need dancers there anyway. I’d only ask that they stand a certain way.”

“A certain way.” Pich looked at him with deep suspicion. “You’re the water engineer. Kosal’s son. What do you know about dancers?”

“Nothing,” said Vann. “But I know someone who does.”

He’d brought Sokha. She was standing in the gate of the yard in her plain wrap, holding herself the way dancers do even when they’re doing nothing. Pich looked at her for a long moment, and his face changed. Vann saw it change. It was the look of a craftsman who has seen good stone.

“Show me,” the carver said.

It took them three days to work out how, sitting on the floor of Pich’s yard in the dust with the stones and the cord and the carver’s charcoal.

Vann had found it on the night of the storm, lying awake, but he hadn’t been sure until he watched Sokha dance it in the yard for Pich. Every gesture in the old dance had its height. The palm pressed down toward the ground, nen, was held lowest, at the root of the spine, reaching for the soles. The palm turned up, sa, at the lower belly. Fingers spread, ri, at the hollow under the ribs. The slow wave, hu, across the heart. The hand lifted and turning, mai, at the throat; the fingertips pressed together, tef, at the brow; and the open still hands of the last pose, ol, just above the crown. He’d measured her. He’d measured three other dancers from Yeay Mom’s school, too, without telling them why, and then measured the gestures against each dancer’s height, and worked the ratios on his slate.

They were the stones.

The heights at which the old dance held each gesture, measured against the body of the dancer, were in the same ratios as the notches on his family’s seven stones, measured against the stones’ bases. The same flute-spacing. The same small whole numbers. Somebody, very long ago, had built the listening levels into the dancers’ bodies, so that a dancer, standing in the right place, would hold her hands at the heights the water should stand at. The stones were the levels for the lake. The dance was the levels for the person.

“It’s one thing,” he told Sokha, in the yard, when he’d finished the figures, and showed her the slate. “Do you see? The lake and you. It was always one thing.”

She turned the slate to the light and looked at it, though she couldn’t read the figures.

“The houses are different,” she said quietly. “The song is one.”

So the frieze would be seven dancers to a doorway, one for each name. Each dancer would hold her gesture at its height, exactly, to the old rule: Vann made Pich a measuring rod of seasoned teak with seven notches on it, scaled from the stones to the height of a carved figure, and the carver swore at him for an afternoon and then used it. Above the first eastern door, the dancers would stand in the order of the first of the seven groups Sokha had counted at the god: nen, sa, ri, tef, and the rest. Above the second door, the second group. And so on across seven doors, the seven groups, in the order they came. The knotted cord, turned into women of stone.

And at the very end of the hall, alone above the last doorway, one more dancer. She would stand at a door in the last pose of the dance: both hands open and held still above her head, after the question. Waiting.

“Why her?” asked Pich.

“Because that’s how it ends,” said Sokha. “That’s the part where the answer goes.”

Sokha posed for all of them.

She stood in the carving yard every afternoon for eighteen days, in the heat and the dust, holding each gesture at its height for as long as Pich needed to draw her. Hours at a time. She never complained. The apprentices began bringing her water without being asked and fanning her with palm leaves. Pich drew her, and drew her again, and swore, and drew her again, and then went to the stone.

He worked faster than Vann had believed anyone could work. He had his best four apprentices on the frieze, cutting the rough figures while he followed behind them with the fine chisels, the faces and the fingers and the folds of the silk. He worked by lamplight after the others had gone home. On the twelfth day Vann found him at midnight, alone, cutting the line of a dancer’s raised hand, and holding the teak rod up against it, and cutting again.

“You don’t have to do this,” Vann said. “I can’t pay you what it’s worth.”

“You haven’t paid me anything.” Pich didn’t look up. “I’ve carved four thousand women in this temple. All the same. Pretty. Dead. Nobody looks at them twice.” He blew the dust from the stone hand. “These are the first ones that are for something. Even if I don’t know what. I’ll take that over payment.” He looked up. “Your girl. She says these are for someone. Who?”

“Someone a long way off,” said Vann. “Who’ll know how to read them.”

“How long?”

“I don’t know. A long time. Long enough that the lake’s gone and the city’s gone and nobody remembers the dance.”

The carver thought about that. Then he went back to the stone.

Narendra’s order came on the twentieth day. The deep gate, at dawn.

The frieze was not quite finished. The last dancer, the one alone at the end above the last door, had her body and her silk and her face, but not her hands. That night, after Pich and the apprentices had gone, Vann and Sokha went into the Hall of Dancers with a lamp, and Vann climbed the scaffold with Pich’s fine chisels in a cloth.

He wasn’t a carver. He was an engineer. But he could cut a line to a fingernail, and he’d watched Pich for eighteen days. He cut the last dancer’s two hands himself, open, still, held above her head at the seventh height, exactly, by the teak rod. It took him until the middle of the night. When it was done he sat back on the scaffold and looked at them by the lamp, and they were not as good as Pich’s, and they were right.

Then, at the dancer’s bare foot, where it met the frame of the doorway, very small, where no one would ever look, he cut three short strokes and a hook.

“Auspicious,” said Sokha from below, softly, holding the lamp.

“That’s what they say it means,” said Vann.

At dawn he opened the deep gate.

It took twelve men on the great timber bar and Vann at the head of them, calling the pull. The door was old and swollen and the culvert was choked with three hundred years of silt, and at first it wouldn’t move at all, and then it moved a finger, and then all at once it went, and the water came out of the bottom of the western lake with a roar that he felt in his chest, brown and thick and full of mud, and went down the culvert into the eastern canal in a single heavy rope. The men cheered. Down the canal, far off, he heard the first village cheer too.

Sokha was sitting on the old steps at the end of the dike, alone, with her feet in the water and her palms flat on the wet stone.

He went and sat beside her when the gate was running and he could leave it. He didn’t say anything. He watched the lake go down.

It went fast now. He could see it go, the water sliding down the face of the steps, past the fourth line, past the third. The old carved post by the sluice standing up out of the water higher and higher, black and dripping. Far out in the middle of the lake, the island temple rising out of its reflection, the four towers lifting clear, the low walls of the shrine where the bronze god lay emerging dark and wet into the morning.

“Tef’s gone,” Sokha said quietly. She had her eyes closed. “I couldn’t really hear it anyway. Through the steps. But it’s gone.” A while later: “Mai.” A while later: “Hu.” Her voice was very calm. He had never heard her so calm. “It’s only nen now. Very faint. The ground. The laterite.” The water went down past the first line, the lowest, and the last step stood clear and pale in the sun with its seven worn marks. “Gone.”

She took her hands from the stone.

“I’m sorry,” said Vann.

“Don’t be.” She looked out at the falling lake, the mud showing at the edges now, the herons already coming down onto it. “My cousins will eat this winter. I’d make the same choice every time. You know that. So would you.” She leaned against him. “And it isn’t gone. Not really. The music’s still there. It’s always there. It was there before the lake and it’ll be there after. It’s only us who go deaf.”

“And the frieze?”

“The frieze is the stones and the dance and the cord, all in one place, in a wall where nobody will ever take it down.” She was quiet a moment. “And nobody will ever know what it means. They’ll walk past it for a thousand years and say how pretty.”

“Unless someone comes who can read it.”

“Unless someone comes,” said Sokha. She put her open hand, palm up, in his.

A month later, when the late rice in the eastern villages had come up green and the last of the western water was a shining puddle around the island, Narendra came to see the Hall of Dancers.

Vann went with him. They stood together under the eastern doors in the afternoon light, the heavy grey minister and the engineer, and looked up at the seven rows of seven dancers, each holding her gesture at her height, and at the last one alone at the end, above the last door, with her two open hands held still.

Narendra looked at them for a long time.

“They’re beautiful,” he said at last.

“Yes, lord.”

“Is that what I paid for?”

Vann thought about it honestly. “Part of it.”

“And the other part?”

“The other part was one night,” said Vann. “I can’t show you that. Only her.”

The minister nodded slowly. He stood a little longer under the last dancer, looking up at her open hands. Then, very quietly, as if he didn’t want anyone to see, he lifted his own right hand and laid it, palm down, flat against the cool stone of the doorframe.

He held it there a moment. Then he took it away and walked on.

Next · Part Two · Chapter SixThe Fading

Part Two · Chapter Six

The Fading

This is not a failure of the Plan. It is the shape of the Plan. What survives is the wall.

Concordance of Fragments, Angkor recension, Fragment 6

14 min read

The old king died in the twenty-eighth year after the drought, in the season of the first rains, and the city wore white for a month.

Vann was sixty-three. He was the chief engineer of the king’s water now, Madhava’s old post. Madhava had died long ago, at his slates, with ink on his fingers, still checking a figure. Narendra had died. Achar Som had died, of the palm wine, as everyone had always known he would, and left Vann a basket of old leaves that nobody else wanted. Pich had died the year after the Hall of Dancers was dedicated, and his apprentices had carved his face, very small, on the frame of a doorway, where he’d have hated it.

The western water had come back. It had taken five years and three good rains, but it had come back, and Vann had cleared the culvert of the deep gate and rehung the doors and stood beside the old carved post on the first full night and watched the lake come up past the first line, and the second, and the third. He’d set it to his father’s stones every dark moon since. Every single one. In twenty-three years he had missed four, all of them in sickness.

He’d heard nothing. Not once. He had not expected to.

Sokha had.

She’d stopped dancing at thirty, as the temple dancers did, and married him, which the temple dancers mostly didn’t, and when Yeay Mom grew too old to teach she had taken over the school. She had been a hard teacher and a loved one. She made the girls wash three times in salt water before every dance and drink a cup of it. She counted the stillness. She could tell from across a courtyard if a girl’s palm was a finger’s width wrong.

And every dark moon, for twenty-three years, she had gone out with Vann to the western water when it stood at a line, and washed, and sat on the old steps, or, when he could get the lake to the fourth line exactly and the night was clear and still, rowed out with him to the island and stood in the pool with her palms on the bronze god.

It had never been like the first night. She’d known it wouldn’t be. The first night had been a drought night, the air dry and the whole country listening, and the lake set by eight days of his life to a fingernail. On most nights afterward she heard the names, and on good nights some of the music, and on the best nights, twice, ol, thin as a thread. He’d knotted it all. He had a box of cords now, twenty-three years of them, every dark moon a cord, in the chest at the foot of their sleeping mat beside the box of stones.

And he had watched the arithmetic.

He hadn’t wanted to. He was a man who measured things; he couldn’t help it. In the first years, the cords were long and full. Around her fortieth year they began to be shorter. She stopped hearing the music first. That was the first thing to go, she said. Before ol, before tef. You still hear the names, so at first you don’t notice. You think the world has gone a little quieter. Then ol went, and never came back. Then tef, which she had barely heard anyway. Then mai, in her forty-sixth year, on a night when he had brought the lake to the line without a flaw and she stood with her hands on the god for half the night and said, finally, very quietly, I can’t find it.

On the last dark moon before the king died, she’d sat on the old steps with her palms flat on the wet stone until the frogs had stopped and started again.

“Nen,” she said at last. “I think. Very faint. Or it’s my pulse.”

She’d taken her hands away and looked at them in the starlight.

“I don’t know anymore,” she said. “I can’t tell if it’s there and I can’t hear it, or if it isn’t there. Is that strange? After all of it. To not know which.” She’d laughed, a little. “I think that’s how it goes. I think that’s how it went for everyone, all the way back. You just stop being able to tell.”

She asked him a month after the king died.

They were in the courtyard of their house in the evening, after the girls of the school had gone home. She’d been sitting very still, with a cup of tea gone cold beside her, looking at nothing. He had learned to leave her alone at those times. Then she said, without turning her head:

“Take me to the Hall of Dancers.”

“Now?”

“Tomorrow. Early. Before the pilgrims.” She turned and looked at him. “And bring the cord. The first one. From the night.”

He brought it.

He’d kept it in its own cloth, separate from the others, in a lacquered box Pich’s widow had given him. It was twenty-eight years old and it had gone dark and stiff, and some of the knots near the end had begun to fray, but it was whole. He carried it in both hands, the way you’d carry something alive, and they walked together through the grey morning to the temple of victory beside the northern water, which still didn’t sing, and in through the great eastern gate, and along the paved way past the guardian kings, into the Hall of Dancers.

It was empty at that hour. Pigeons. The light coming in sideways through the doorways, low and gold. Above the doors, the seven rows of dancers stood in the soft stone exactly as Pich had left them, each holding her gesture at her height, and at the far end, alone above the last door, the last dancer with her two open hands held still above her head. Twenty-eight years of rain had softened the edges very slightly. Some of the dancers’ faces had begun to go green with moss. Pilgrims had rubbed the feet of the nearest ones smooth for luck.

Sokha walked slowly down the length of the hall, looking up. He walked beside her. At the end, under the last dancer, she stopped.

“Tell me what it says,” she said.

He didn’t understand at first.

“You know what it says. You posed for every one of them.” He looked up at the frieze. “You can see the gestures. Nen, palm down. Sa, palm up. You can read the names off them better than I can.”

“I can read the names.” She was still looking up. “Nen, sa, ri, tef. I can see them. I know them. They’re words. Like reading a list of the names of rivers in a country you’ve never been to.” Her voice was very steady. “That’s what they are to me now, Vann. Words. I look at the first dancer and I know she’s nen, and I know nen is red, and the ground, and iron, and one. I know all of that. I can tell you all of it. And there’s nothing. There’s no red. I can’t remember what red was like when it was nen.” She turned to him. “And I can’t remember what it said. That night. At the god. I remember that it was the most beautiful thing that ever happened to me, and that I cried, and that it was enormous. But I can’t remember it. I can’t hear it, even inside. It’s like trying to remember a dream after you’ve been awake all day.”

He stood very still.

“You were there,” said Sokha. “You knotted it. Every name. You said you checked it three times. You’re the one who carved the last hands.” She looked down at the box in his arms. “So tell me. Please. What did it say?”

He opened the box. He took the cord out of its cloth, carefully, and held it up in the gold light, so that she could see the knots.

And he tried.

“The first group,” he said. “Nen. That’s here, the knot at the first finger’s width. Then sa, at the second. Ri, at the third. Tef, at the sixth.” He moved his thumb along the cord. “Then hu. Mai. Ol. Then the double knot, that’s the end of the group. Then this long stretch is the silence. Then the second group. Sa. Hu. Nen…”

“Yes,” she said. “Those are the names. What does it say?”

He stopped.

He looked at the cord in his hands. He looked up at the dancers above the doors, seven by seven, holding the same names at the same heights (root, belly, fire under the ribs, heart, throat, brow, and the open stillness just above the crown) in the same order, exactly, to a fingernail, because he had made sure of it. He had made sure of every single one. He knew where every name went. He knew how long every silence was, in heartbeats, and the ratio of every height to every other, and he could have drawn the whole frieze from memory on a slate with his eyes closed.

He did not know what one of them meant.

He had never known. He understood that now, standing in the Hall of Dancers in the gold morning, with the cord in his hands. On the night at the god, he had knotted what she told him, and believed her, and the believing had been so complete that he’d never noticed there was nothing inside it. It had been like carrying a sealed jar for someone and never once wondering what was in it, because you trusted her. He had trusted her. And she had been the one who knew. And now she didn’t, and the jar was still sealed, and he was holding it.

“I don’t know,” Vann said.

She looked at him.

“I don’t know what it says, Sokha. I never did.” His voice wasn’t steady. He let it not be. “I knew where you said everything was. I wrote it down. I measured it. I made them carve it exactly, every height, every count. I can tell you where every name is in that wall for the rest of my life.” He looked up at the last dancer, at the hands he’d cut himself by lamplight. “But I only ever knew where. You knew what. I thought that if I kept the where perfectly, the what would stay with it. I thought that was what keeping meant.” He shook his head. “It’s a wall full of names. It’s beautiful. I don’t know what it says.”

They stood together under the last dancer for a long time without speaking.

He thought she would cry. She didn’t. After a while she sat down on the low stone sill of the last doorway, slowly, the way her knees made her sit now, and patted the stone beside her, and he sat too.

“So that’s what it’s like,” she said. “For Yeay Mom. For all of them. For you, all those nights.” She looked at the cord in his lap. “I always felt so sorry for you. On the steps. Sitting there with your hands on the stone, hearing frogs. I used to think, he’s so faithful, and he’ll never know.” She almost laughed. “And now I’m the one who’ll never know. And you’re the one who still knows where everything is.”

“It isn’t the same.”

“No,” she agreed. “It’s worse for me. You never lost it.” She put her hand on his. “But it’s not so different, Vann. You hold the where. I held the what, for a while, and I let it go. Neither of us has both. Maybe nobody ever does. Maybe that’s what the wall is for.”

“For what?”

“For holding both. Because people can’t.” She looked up at the dancers. “The stones knew where. The dance knew how to stand. I knew what, for a while. And you put them all together in a wall, where they can’t forget each other.” She was quiet a moment. “Somebody will come and read it. Someday. Somebody who hears, and who knows a little about water, and about dancing, and about counting, all at once. Not one of us. Someone who can stand in all of the rooms at once.”

“How do you know?”

“I don’t,” said Sokha. “That’s why I’m sitting here.”

After a while she said, “Tell me the groups. Knot them. I want them in the cord before the hearing goes.”

He looked at her.

“Not what it says. I know you can’t.” She closed her eyes. “Just count it. The way you know it. The heights. The numbers. Out loud. I want to hear it once more, even if it’s only numbers.”

So he did.

He sat on the sill of the last doorway in the Hall of Dancers, with the cord across his knees, and counted it for her in the only way he could. Not in the names. In his own tongue, the tongue of slates and sighting rods. First door, first dancer: the first height, at the root. Second dancer: the second height, at the belly, as five is to four. Third dancer: the third height, under the ribs… He went along the cord with his thumb, knot by knot. He counted every silence in heartbeats. He gave her every ratio. He counted all seven doors, all seven groups, forty-nine dancers, the way he’d have read out the levels of a dike to Narendra, plainly, carefully, without any music in it at all.

And at the end, the last dancer. The seventh height, just above the crown. Both hands open. Still. Then the stillness, the length of fifty heartbeats, the way you held it at the god.

He stopped.

Beside him, Sokha sat with her eyes closed and her hands open in her lap, palms up. She didn’t say anything. A pigeon crossed the hall from one doorway to the other.

“Was it like that?” he said finally.

“I don’t know,” she said. “I can’t remember.” She opened her eyes. They were wet, but she was smiling. “But it was very beautiful, the way you counted it. I didn’t know numbers could sound like that.”

He gave the stones to her school that winter.

He thought about it all autumn. Their son, Rith, was a clerk in the royal granaries, Narendra’s old office, and a good one, and when Vann had shown him the stones once he had turned them over politely and said, They’re lovely, father, in exactly the voice Vann had once used to his own father. Rith washed his hands three times before meals in salt water because his mother had taught him to. He didn’t know why. He didn’t ask.

So Vann wrapped the seven stones in his father’s old cloth, and put them in the box, and put the box beside the salt jar in the washing room of the dancers’ school, under the sign cut into the jar’s lip, three strokes and a hook. He wrote on a strip of new palm-leaf, in plain Khmer, These are the heights at which the water listens. Keep them with the jar. They belong together. He put the cord from the first night in with them. The other cords he burned, on the dike of the western water, the way they had burned his father, and watched the smoke go out over the lake.

Sokha’s best girl that year was a quiet child of eleven called Chenda, who stood a little apart from the others and sometimes turned her head toward the courtyard fountain in the middle of a lesson, as if someone had spoken. Sokha had watched her for a month before she said anything. Then she’d asked the child, privately, whether water had a color.

“Blue,” Chenda said at once, and then looked frightened. “Isn’t it?”

“Yes,” said Sokha. “It is. Don’t tell anyone.”

Three names, she told Vann that night. Perhaps four. Not seven. But more than anyone else in the city.

“It doesn’t go away,” she said. “Does it? It gets less and less. But it doesn’t go away.”

“No,” said Vann. “It gets less.”

She died in her fifty-eighth year, in the dry season, at home, with the school girls singing the skipping song in the courtyard because she’d asked them to. He was beside her. At the end she held up her hand, a little, palm up, open, and he put his own hand in it, and held it until it was cold.

Then he went out to the western water, though it was not the dark of the moon, and set it to the fourth line, to a fingernail, because he could do that better than anyone; and washed his hands three times; and sat on the old steps with his feet in the lake and his palms on the stone until the stars moved a long way.

Frogs. The seep through the laterite.

He sat there anyway.

Eight hundred years later, the Hall of Dancers has no roof.

The fig trees came through it a long time ago, after the city was abandoned, and split the pillars and lifted the paving, and then the restorers came and cut the trees back and propped what was left. Now there is a wooden walkway, and a rope, and a sign in four languages. Hall of Dancers. Late 12th century. Reign of Jayavarman VII. The western water is still there, mostly. The bronze god was found in pieces in the mud of the island shrine in the last century and taken to a museum, where he lies under glass with his head on his hand, dreaming.

It is hot. A tour group comes along the walkway at ten in the morning, forty of them, in sun hats, with a guide holding up a small orange flag on a stick. They stop under the eastern doors.

“The famous dancers,” says the guide, in English, because that is the language of this group. “Apsaras. The celestial dancers of the gods. You see the poses are very graceful. Interesting thing: scholars say the poses do not match any known sequence of the classical dance. Maybe the carver made it up. Maybe the dance changed. Nobody knows.” She waves the flag. “Ten minutes for photographs. Please don’t touch.”

They take photographs.

They take a great many. Of the dancers above the first door, and the second, all seven doors. Of each other, standing under the dancers, smiling, with their arms around each other’s shoulders. A man with a long lens photographs the hands of each dancer one by one, carefully, because he likes hands. A teenager lies down on the walkway to get the whole frieze in one shot against the sky. An old woman, at the back, who has come a very long way and has bad knees, takes one picture, of the last dancer alone above the last door, with her two open hands held still above her head, and then lowers her camera and just looks at her for a while.

By evening there will be eleven hundred new photographs of the Hall of Dancers in the world. By the end of the year, a hundred thousand. They will go into telephones, and from telephones into machines, and from machines into other machines, all over the Earth, copied and recopied, kept perfectly, in a great many places at once, and nobody will look at them closely, because there is nothing to see. Only dancers.

The guide raises her orange flag. The group moves on along the walkway toward the next hall, talking about lunch.

The last dancer stays where she is, at the end, above the door. Her hands are open. She is holding the stillness, the length of fifty heartbeats. She has held it for eight hundred years.

Next · Part Three · Chapter OneThe Translator

Part ThreeThe Alchemists

Part Three · Chapter One

The Translator

SINAN IBN MARWAN AL-HARRANI: …translator, of the Sabian community of Harran, in the service of the sons of Musa at Baghdad. He is remembered, where he is remembered at all, for a single colophon: I have copied what I did not understand exactly as it was, because it was not mine to understand. The Concordance regards this sentence as the purest statement of the Plan ever written by someone who did not know there was one…

Concordance of Fragments, Baghdad recension, Fragment 1

18 min read

The Indian washed his hands before he would let anyone touch the book.

Sinan had seen a great many scholars arrive at the house of the sons of Musa with a great many books. Greeks from the cities of the Romans with Galen under their arms. Persians with astronomical tables. Syrian monks with Aristotle in Syriac, which Sinan’s father had spent his life turning into Arabic, and which Sinan had spent his own turning into better Arabic. Every one of them had been in a hurry to put his book down on the table and see what it would fetch. Not one of them had asked for a basin.

The Indian asked for a basin. He was very old, and very thin, with a white cloth wound round his head and a cord across his chest, and he’d come up the Tigris from Basra on a date boat with a single bundle wrapped in oilcloth. When the servant brought the basin, the old man took a twist of paper from his sleeve and shook salt into the water and stirred it with one finger. Then he washed his hands in it. Front and back, between each finger, slowly. Once. Again. A third time.

Then he dried them and unwrapped the bundle.

“Why three?” Sinan asked him. He asked it in Persian, because that was the language they had found they shared.

The old man looked up at him with eyes that were milky at the edges. “So that I go the same as the first one,” he said.

“The first one?”

“The first one who touched it.” He laid his dry hands flat on the bundle. “My grandmother taught me so. I don’t know who the first one was. But I go the same.”

His name was Kanakadatta, and he was a physician of the Brahmins from Ujjain, and he was dying.

He said so on the first day, plainly, the way physicians say such things. He had a growth in his belly that he had treated in himself for two years with every remedy he knew, and it had not cared. He had perhaps four months. He had come to Baghdad because the whole world said that in Baghdad there was a house where the sons of Musa paid good silver to have every book on earth turned into Arabic, and that Arabic was the language that would last.

“In my country,” he said, “nobody reads this anymore. Not even the physicians. They say it’s too old. They say it’s a book of the grandfathers and has nothing in it a modern man needs.” He untied the last cords of the oilcloth. “My sons are good physicians. They have no time for it. I am the last one in my family who knows what the words say. Not what they mean. Only what they say.” He laid the bundle open. “In your language, the whole world will read it. That’s what they tell me in Basra.”

Sinan looked at what lay on the table.

It was a stack of palm leaves, long and narrow, bound between two boards of dark wood worn smooth by handling. The leaves were brown with age. The writing on them was in the old script of the Indians, the one Sinan had seen on the astronomical tables, very small and very neat. But here and there, in the margins and sometimes between the lines, there was another writing, older, that he had never seen anywhere: square, careful, almost like pictures. And on the first leaf, at the head of the text, alone, there was a small mark. Three short strokes and a hook.

“What’s this?” He touched it.

“Fortune,” said Kanakadatta. “It’s on everything old. It means auspicious. Every water-tank in my grandmother’s village had it cut into the steps.” He shrugged. “It’s only a mark.”

“And this writing? In the margins?”

The old man was quiet a moment.

“I can’t read that,” he said. “Nobody can. My grandmother said her grandmother could. She said it was the first writing. The writing of the book before it was a book.” He touched one of the square signs, gently, the way you’d touch a sleeping child. “Copy it anyway. Please. Even if it means nothing. Copy it the way it is.”

They worked in the long room above the courtyard, where the sons of Musa kept their translators, in the mornings before the heat.

The method was the usual one when a book came in a language no one at the house could read. Kanakadatta read the Sanskrit aloud and put it into Persian, slowly, phrase by phrase, and Sinan listened and asked questions and wrote it down in Arabic, on the good new paper from the mill by the Basra gate.
