Then they went back over each passage together, three and four times, until the old man was satisfied that the Arabic said what the Sanskrit said. Sinan was famous at the house for this. He was not a brilliant man, and he knew it. He was not a philosopher like the Kindi, or a mathematician like the eldest son of Musa. He was a translator. He had one rule, which his father had beaten into him as a boy, and he had never broken it. A translator adds nothing and leaves nothing out.

The book was called, as near as he could make it, The Book of the Seven Waters.

It began like a hundred other books Sinan had seen. There were seven metals: lead, tin, iron, gold, copper, quicksilver, silver. There were seven wandering stars: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, the Moon. Each metal belonged to a star, as everyone knew. Lead was Saturn’s, and gold was the Sun’s, and so on. The physicians of every nation knew this. The Sabians of Harran, Sinan’s own people, had built seven temples to the seven stars in the old days, each temple of its own shape and its own color, each with its own metal, and his grandfather could still recite which was which.

Then the book went on, and Sinan began to frown.

There were seven colors. Red like rust, for iron and Mars. Amber for gold and the Sun. Green, the green of copper left in the rain, for Venus. Blue, the blue of deep water, for quicksilver, which the book called the water that is a metal. Violet, for tin and Jupiter, the color of the sky at dusk. White, silver-white, for the Moon. And for Saturn and lead, a color that the book said was beyond the others, the color that is all of them when they agree.

“That’s not a color,” said Sinan.

“No,” Kanakadatta agreed. “My grandmother said the same. She said it was the color you see when you hear all the others at once — at the crown of the head, she said, where the thousand-petaled lotus is, though she was a physician’s widow and not a temple dancer, and she laughed at herself for saying it.”

“Hear?”

“That’s the word.” The old man tapped the leaf. “Hear. Every color has a sound. It says so here. Seven colors, seven sounds. Like the seven notes of a string.” He frowned at the leaf. “And then it gives the sounds. They’re not words. Only syllables. Nen. Sa. Ri. Hu. Mai. Tef. Ol.”

He hesitated, as if deciding whether a translator of Arabic deserved the rest.

“She had another map for them,” he said. “Not in this book. In her hands. The red you feel in the soles and at the root of the spine. The sun-warmth low in the belly, under the navel. She said amber; the priests of her childhood said yellow, and they argued about it the way men argue about anything they cannot weigh. The green of growing things at the fire under the ribs. Water in the chest, not where the water-seat is supposed to be. Breath at the throat. The coin-taste between the eyes. And the whole at the crown, when the six agree.” He shrugged, frail. “Medicine. Or superstition. We washed. We kept the book. Nobody in my family has felt the seats clearly in five generations. I tell you because the leaf’s order matches her hands better than it matches your stars.”

Sinan wrote them down in Arabic letters, carefully, as near as he could make the sounds.

“And the order,” he said slowly, looking at what he had written. “The order is wrong.”

“Wrong?”

“The stars. Every astronomer from Babylon to your Ujjain gives the seven stars in the same order. By how far off they are, or by how fast they move. Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon. Your book has them that way when it lists them. But when it gives the colors and the sounds, it gives them in another order.” He drew a line under it. “Mars first. Iron. Red. Then the Sun. Then Venus, then Mercury, then Jupiter, then the Moon. And Saturn last, as the color that’s all of them. That isn’t any order of the heavens I know. It isn’t by distance or by speed or by brightness.”

Kanakadatta looked at him for a long moment.

“Then what is it by?” he said.

“I don’t know.” Sinan looked at the seven syllables. “It’s as though someone had an order of their own, for some other reason, and fitted the stars to it afterward.”

He decided, in the third week, that it was an allegory.

It was the only explanation that made sense. The book was about the seven metals and the seven stars, but it was not really about them, any more than the Song of Songs was really about a girl and her lover. It was about the soul. That was clear from the way it went on. The seven metals were seven states of the soul, from the heaviest to the brightest. The seven colors were the seven stages of its purification. The soul began as iron and earth, and rose through gold and copper and the blue water and the violet air and the white of the moon, until it became the color that is all colors, and was one with the whole.

The Sabians of Harran had taught something very like this for a thousand years. Sinan had learned it as a boy. He recognized it the way you recognize your mother’s voice in a crowd. He told Kanakadatta so, with some relief, and the old man listened politely and said that perhaps it was so.

The trouble was the second half of the book.

The second half of the book did not read like an allegory. It read like a carpenter’s instructions.

Take a vessel of unglazed clay, so many fingers high, so many wide. Line its two sides with two plates of red metal, not touching. Between them pour the water that is a metal, until it stands at the height of the fourth mark. Set beside it, close, but not touching, the stone that draws iron. Then the vessel will move its own water, without fire and without hand, whenever the breath of the sky passes between the plates.

Take the grey stone that is heavy and shines like lead, and set it in a cup of clay, and touch it with a needle of red metal no thicker than a hair, lightly, at one point only. Search the stone with the needle until you find the place where the breath passes one way and not the other. Mark the place.

Take the clear stone that is like ice, and press it in a vise of wood, so that it is squeezed; and it will answer.

Take the black stone from the island of the south, which draws ash to itself when it is warmed, and warm it with a small flame, never a great one; and it will answer also.

Take a rod of red metal clad in the metal of the sun and raise it as high as you can raise it, toward the sky. Take a plate of red metal and set it in water under the ground. Join them by the vessel and the stones. Then let the one who listens wash three times in salt and wet the feet and drink the cup and sit in darkness, and put the two hands on the two plates. Then the seven will be heard, each in its color.

Sinan put down his pen.

“That isn’t an allegory,” he said.

“No?”

“An allegory doesn’t need the thickness of the needle.” He found he was half angry, as if someone had cheated him. “Why does the soul need a hair-fine needle of copper? Why does the soul need the fourth mark on a clay pot? Look.” He pointed. “The vessel is to be so many fingers high. Here. And the plates so many apart. And then later, the same number again, here, for the distance from the vessel to the stone that draws iron. The same number. That’s not poetry. It’s a measurement. Someone measured something and wrote it down.”

Kanakadatta looked at the leaf.

“My grandmother said it was medicine,” he said. “Our physicians use the water that is a metal for medicine. A great deal. We purify it and bind it and give it for fevers. She said the book was how the old physicians made their medicines strong. That’s why my family kept it.” He paused. “But she also said that it was for listening. And that her grandmother had listened. When she was small. In the tank at the temple, with her hands on the copper steps.” He looked up at Sinan with his milky eyes. “I never understood that part. I thought she was old.”

“Did she hear anything? Your grandmother?”

“No. She said only her grandmother did.” He turned the leaf over, carefully. “Nobody in my family has heard anything in five generations. We kept the book. We washed our hands three times. That’s all.”

The sons of Musa came to see the work in the fifth week.

Two of the three brothers came, rich beyond counting and curious beyond counting, on a hot afternoon with servants carrying cold sherbet. Muhammad, the eldest, watched the stars. Ahmad, the second, built machines. Between them they wanted the whole world, in two halves.

Sinan read them what he had.

Muhammad listened to the first half with polite boredom until he got to the colors and the stars. Then he sat up. “The order,” he said. “The stars are out of order in the color list.”

“Yes, master. I noticed.”

“Copy that part for me separately. I want to see if it fits any of the old Babylonian schemes.” He waved a hand. “The rest is the usual Sabian business about the soul. No offense, Sinan.”

“None, master.”

Ahmad didn’t care about the stars at all. He’d been sitting with his elbows on his knees and his chin on his fists all through the first half, and when Sinan reached the vessel with the two copper plates and the quicksilver that would move its own water without fire or hand, he jumped up.

“Read that again.”

Sinan read it again.

“It moves the water itself?” Ahmad was pacing. “Without a wheel? Without a weight, or a float, or a siphon? Only with the stone that draws iron beside it?” He turned to his brothers. “Do you know what that would be? I’ve spent ten years on vessels that fill and empty themselves. Lamps that trim themselves. Fountains that change their shape every hour. Every one of them needs a float or a valve. If quicksilver can be made to move by a lodestone, without anything at all moving—” He spun round on Sinan. “Copy me the vessel. All of it. Every measurement. I want to build it.”

“Master, the vessel is only one part of what the book describes. It goes with the needle, and the stones, and the rod, and—”

“I don’t need the rest. The rest is listening and sitting in the dark. I need the vessel.” He was already at the door. “And the needle, if it’s a valve of some kind. The one-way stone. Copy me the needle too.”

Muhammad watched him go with the indulgence of an elder brother. “He will build it,” he said, “and it will not move, and he will put it in a book.” He looked at Sinan. “And I will find your colors in Babylon, and that will be the end of it.”

So in the end there were two books made out of one.

There was the book of the stars and colors, for Muhammad, who wrote a short treatise on whether the order of the colors might be a lost Chaldean scheme, and decided that it wasn’t, and put it away. There was the book of the vessel and the needle, for Ahmad, who built the vessel exactly to the measurements and set a lodestone beside it and watched it for a month and saw the quicksilver shiver once or twice, in a thunderstorm, and never again. He put a description of it in the great book of devices he was writing with his brothers, among the self-trimming lamps and the trick jugs, as a curiosity.

Both brothers were right about their parts. Muhammad knew more about the stars than anyone in Baghdad. Ahmad understood water and valves better than any man alive. Each took the piece he understood and left the rest, and the rest was exactly the part that would have told him what the piece was for.

And Sinan, who understood none of it, was the only man in the city who had read the whole.

Kanakadatta grew weaker in the heat of the summer.

He still came to the long room in the mornings, but he came on a litter now, and lay on cushions by the window, and read the Sanskrit to Sinan with his eyes closed, from memory, because he knew it by heart. Sinan learned to wait through the long pauses. Sometimes the old man slept in the middle of a sentence and woke an hour later and went on from the next word.

“I’ve made two books for the sons of Musa,” Sinan told him one morning. “From yours. Each of them wanted only one part.”

“I heard.” Kanakadatta didn’t open his eyes. “Is that bad?”

“I don’t know.” Sinan put down his pen. “It feels bad. It feels as though I took a pot and broke it in two and gave each half to a man who wanted a cup.” He hesitated. “I don’t even know what the pot was for. I said it was an allegory. I don’t think it is. I think somebody built something once and wrote down how. And now Ahmad has a vessel that doesn’t move and Muhammad has a list of colors that isn’t in any order he knows. And neither of them will ever ask the other.”

The old man was quiet for a long time.

“My grandmother said a thing,” he said at last. “When I was a boy, and she taught me the washing. I asked her, as you asked me, why three. And she said, So you’ll be the same as the first one. And I said, But we don’t hear anything. What’s the use of being the same? And she said—” he opened his eyes and looked at the ceiling “—she said, We aren’t keeping it for us. We’re keeping it for whoever comes. And when they come, they’ll need it whole.”

“Whoever comes?”

“That’s all she said.” Kanakadatta turned his head on the cushion and looked at Sinan. “Make one more book. Please. Not for the sons of Musa. For no one. Make it whole. Every part. The stars and the colors and the vessel and the needle and the medicine and the listening and the sitting in the dark. And the old writing in the margins, that no one can read. And the mark at the beginning.” He closed his eyes again. “And the last leaf.”

“The last leaf?”

They hadn’t come to it yet. Sinan went to the bundle and turned the leaves over to the end.

The last leaf was nearly blank. There was no Sanskrit on it at all. There was only a single line of the old square writing, very small, in the middle. And above each square sign, in faded colors, someone long ago had painted a small round spot. Seven spots. Blue and amber, side by side, touching, as if they were one thing. Then violet. Then silver-white. Then rust-red. Then green, and next to the green a little stroke, rising, that stopped short. And then a seventh place, where there was a square sign of the old writing but no color at all. Only the bare brown leaf.

“What is it?” said Sinan.

“My grandmother said it was a question,” said Kanakadatta. “The oldest thing in the book. Older than the book. She said it was what you say at a door at night when you’ve been waiting a long time.” He smiled faintly. “And the last color is empty because it’s the answer. Whoever comes is supposed to put it in.”

“What does it ask?”

“I don’t know,” said the old man. “I never knew. Copy it anyway. Copy the colors.”

He died eleven days later, on the cushions by the window, in the middle of the morning. He had been reading a passage about the clear stone pressed in a vise, with his eyes closed, and he stopped, and Sinan waited, as he always waited, and the next word didn’t come.

Sinan finished the book alone.

It took him the rest of the summer and most of the autumn. He worked from the leaves now, slowly, with the help of a Persian who had been in Sind and knew a little of the old script, and with his own memory of every word Kanakadatta had spoken. When he came to a word he didn’t know, he wrote it in the Indian letters and left it. When he came to the square signs in the margins, he copied each one exactly, stroke for stroke, though he had no idea what any of them meant, and drew a small box round each so a later copyist would know they were not Arabic. He copied the mark at the head of the book, three strokes and a hook, in the margin of the first page.

When he came to the last leaf, he bought colors from the illuminators in the street of the booksellers, the best ones, and mixed them himself. Blue and amber, side by side, touching. Violet. Silver-white, which he made with powdered tin, because the illuminators said that would last. Rust-red. Green, and beside it a small stroke, rising, stopping short. And a seventh place, with the square sign copied above it, and no color.

He looked at it a long time when it was done. He thought it was the strangest thing he had ever written. He thought it was not his to understand.

Then he wrote his colophon at the end, as translators did, in his best hand.

This is the Book of the Seven Waters, put into the Arabic tongue from the speech of the Indians by Sinan ibn Marwan of Harran, from the reading of the physician Kanakadatta of Ujjain, may God have mercy on him, who died before it was finished. I believe this book to be an allegory of the soul. But it contains measures that an allegory does not need, and signs I cannot read, and a question I cannot answer. I have added nothing and left nothing out. I have copied what I did not understand exactly as it was, because it was not mine to understand. Let whoever comes after me do the same.

He bound it in plain leather and gave it to the librarian of the house of the sons of Musa, who wrote it in the register under Alchemy and medicine, because that seemed the closest, and put it on a shelf.

Before he went home that evening, Sinan stopped at the basin in the courtyard. He didn’t know why. He stood there for a while in the last of the light. Then he took a pinch of salt from the kitchen and stirred it into the water with one finger, and washed his hands. Front and back. Between each finger. Once, and again, and a third time.

So that he would go the same as the first one. Whoever that had been.

Next · Part Three · Chapter TwoHrubý

Part Three · Chapter Two

Hrubý

The Latin version of the Book of the Seven Waters was made at Toledo in the twelfth century by a translator who did not sign his name. He kept Sinan’s colophon, and added one line of his own beneath it: “I have done the same.” Four hundred years later, at the court of the Emperor Rudolf in Prague, a man who could not have done otherwise read the book as it was written. This is rarer than it sounds. Most readers of most books are looking for something else.

Concordance of Fragments, Prague recension, Fragment 2

16 min read

The gold was very good, which was how Tobiáš Hrubý knew it was a fraud.

He held the little bead up to the window of the assay room with his tongs and turned it in the grey February light. It was the size of a lentil. It had come out of a crucible an hour before, in front of four witnesses and a secretary of the Imperial chamber, at the end of a demonstration by a man from Saxony who called himself Doctor Aurelius and said he could turn lead into gold with a red powder of his own making. The lead had gone into the crucible. The powder had gone in. The fire had roared. And there at the bottom when it cooled was a bead of perfectly good gold.

“Well?” said the secretary.

“It’s gold,” said Hrubý. “Very fine. Twenty-two carats, I’d say. I’ll test it properly, but I’d say twenty-two.”

Doctor Aurelius smiled the smile of a man who has been paid before.

“May I see the crucible?” said Hrubý.

The smile flickered. Hrubý picked up the crucible with the tongs. It was an ordinary clay one, the kind every alchemist in Bohemia bought by the dozen from the potters in the Old Town. He turned it over, and tapped it on the edge of the bench, and the bottom came away cleanly in his tongs, because it had been a false bottom, a thin disk of clay sealed in with wax, which had melted, and under it there was a little hollow where a lentil of gold had waited the whole time.

He held it up to the secretary without saying anything.

The secretary sighed. Doctor Aurelius said something in Saxon German that Hrubý didn’t catch and didn’t need to. Two of the castle guard came in and took him out. It was the third this winter.

“I don’t know how you do it,” said the secretary, writing in his book.

“I look,” said Hrubý. “Everyone else is looking at the gold. I look at the crucible.” He put the tongs down. “It’s not hard. You only have to want to know what happened more than you want it to have been gold.”

He was thirty-eight that year, and an assayer of the Imperial mint, which was a small, honest, badly paid post that nobody envied. He’d been born in Kutná Hora, in the silver country, the son of a miner, and he’d gone down the shafts with his father at nine and learned to tell the ores by their weight in his hand: the heavy shining grey of galena that the silver came out of, the red of cinnabar, the black of the lodestone that leapt to his father’s pick. At fifteen, because he was quick with figures, he’d been taken out of the shafts and put to the assay furnace. At twenty-five he’d come to Prague, because everyone came to Prague. The Emperor was there.

The Emperor Rudolf was strange, everyone said, and sad, and he didn’t like to be looked at. He lived up in the castle above the river with his collections, and he collected everything. Clocks. Stuffed birds from the Indies. Unicorn horns and a cup carved from a single crystal by the Miseroni brothers below the walls. Astronomers and alchemists and men who claimed to speak with angels. The Englishman Kelley had promised the Emperor gold and been made a baron for it, then thrown into a tower when the gold didn’t come; he had died there the summer before last, trying, people said, to climb out of a window. The court was full of men like Doctor Aurelius. Hrubý’s job was to look at their crucibles.

He didn’t think of himself as an alchemist. He’d read all the books, of course; an assayer had to. He knew the seven metals and the seven planets, Saturn’s lead and Jupiter’s tin and the rest, and the stages of the Great Work and the colors the matter was supposed to pass through, black and white and red. He thought most of it was nonsense, and the rest was chemistry written by men who were afraid of being understood.

But he liked the old books, the way he liked the lute, which he played badly, and clocks, which he took apart on Sundays. He liked anything made carefully by someone who’d wanted to know what happened.

That was how he found the Book of the Seven Waters.

It was in the Emperor’s library, in the long room under the roof of the castle where the books on alchemy were kept, on a shelf with a hundred others. The Emperor’s librarian had let him in to look for an old treatise on assaying silver, and he hadn’t found it, and on the way out his eye had caught a binding that was older than the others, plain brown leather gone soft with age.

Liber Septem Aquarum, said the label. Translatus de arabico. Alchymia et medicina.

He took it down because of the word aquarum. Waters. Alchemical books were usually about fire.

He read the first page standing up. Then he sat down on the librarian’s stool by the window and read for three hours, until the light went, and the librarian came and coughed at him.

It was a Latin translation, made at Toledo, of an Arabic book made in Baghdad from the tongue of the Indians. It began with the seven metals and the seven planets, as they all did. Then it gave seven colors and seven sounds, nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef, ol, in an order that wasn’t any order of the heavens. And then, in the second half, it stopped being a book of alchemy at all and turned into a list of parts.

A vessel of unglazed clay, so many fingers high. Two plates of red metal, which was copper, not touching. Quicksilver between them, to the height of the fourth mark. The stone that draws iron, beside it. The grey stone that shines like lead, which was galena, touched with a needle of copper no thicker than a hair, searched until you found the place where the breath passes one way and not the other. A clear stone like ice, pressed in a vise, which was rock crystal. A black stone from an island in the south that drew ash when it was warmed. A rod of copper clad in gold, raised high. A plate of copper in water under the ground. And then: wash three times in salt, wet the feet, drink the cup, sit in darkness, put the two hands on the two plates. Then the seven will be heard, each in its color.

At the end was the colophon of a man called Sinan of Harran: I believe this book to be an allegory of the soul. But it contains measures that an allegory does not need. And beneath it, in a different Latin hand: I have done the same.

On the very last leaf, seven painted spots of color: blue and amber, touching; violet; silver; red; green, with a little rising stroke that stopped short; and a seventh place, bare, with a square sign above it and no color at all. There were other square signs in boxes all through the margins. And at the head of the first page, a mark that Hrubý had seen a hundred times on old boundary stones in the silver country, which the miners at Kutná Hora cut into the mouths of new shafts for luck. Three strokes and a hook.

He sat with the book in his lap in the dark long after the librarian had given up coughing.

Measures that an allegory does not need. He thought that was the most honest sentence he had ever read in a book of alchemy. The man from Harran had looked at the crucible.

He built it in the cellar of the house where he lodged, in the Malá Strana below the castle, over the spring and the early summer.

He told nobody what it was for, because he didn’t know. When his landlady’s son Jan, who was fourteen and followed him about like a dog, asked what he was making, Hrubý said, Something out of a book, and the boy said, Gold? and Hrubý said, I don’t think so, and Jan lost interest for almost a whole day.

The vessel was easy. Any potter could make it. He had it thrown to the exact fingers the book gave, and the plates cut from good Mansfeld copper, and he measured the gap between them a dozen times with a pair of dividers. The quicksilver came from the Imperial stores; it came up from the mines at Idrija in sealed iron flasks, and the mint used it for gilding, and nobody counted it very closely. He poured it between the plates until it stood at the fourth of the marks he’d scratched inside the clay, and it lay there heavy and bright, trembling slightly every time a cart went by in the street.

The lodestone he borrowed from the Emperor’s collection, through a friend of a friend of the keeper of the Kunstkammer, who owed him for not looking too closely at a crucible once. It was a fist-sized black stone in a silver mount, and it pulled the iron nails out of the cellar beam when he held it near.

The galena he had already. He had a box of it from Kutná Hora, from his father’s last shaft. He set a piece the size of a walnut in a little cup of clay and spent three weeks of evenings touching it with a needle of copper wire that he’d drawn himself, thinner than a hair, at one point and then another and then another, the way the book said. Search the stone until you find the place. He didn’t know what he was searching for. He searched anyway. He was an assayer. If a book said search, he searched.

The crystal he bought from the Miseroni workshop, a clean offcut of rock crystal they’d have thrown away, and set it in a little vise of oak. The black stone from the island of the south he found, after a month of asking, in a dealer’s shop in the Old Town, in a tray of curiosities from the Portuguese Indies: dull, long, ridged, like a burnt stick. He warmed it at a candle and held it over the ash on the hearth, and the ash leapt up to it, and the dealer crossed himself and charged him double.

The rod was the hardest. He had a copper rod made by a coppersmith in the Old Town, as long as two men, and he took it to a goldsmith and had it gilded, which cost him two months’ pay, and his landlady thought he’d gone mad. Then one night, with Jan holding the ladder and whispering prayers, he climbed onto the roof of the house and lashed it to the chimney, pointing up at the sky, as high as he could raise it. A wire of copper ran down from it inside the chimney to the cellar. The other plate he lowered on a long copper wire down the well in the yard behind the house, into the black water at the bottom, where it was always cold.

Then he joined everything by the vessel and the stones, as the book said, with more wire, and a great deal of thought, and some guessing, and on an evening in June he stood in the cellar and looked at it, and it looked like nothing at all. A clay pot full of quicksilver, a black stone, a grey stone with a needle in it, a crystal in a vise, a burnt stick by a candle, and wires going up a chimney and down a well. An Imperial assayer had spent a third of his year’s pay on it.

“What does it do?” said Jan.

“I don’t know,” said Hrubý. “Let’s find out.”

It did nothing.

He did everything the book said, exactly, because he had decided that if he was going to do it at all he was going to do it the way it was written and not the way he thought it ought to be. He washed his hands three times in salt water, front and back and between each finger, feeling ridiculous. He wet his feet in a basin. He drank a cup of salt water and nearly threw it back up. He blew out the candle, except for the small one under the black stone, and sat in the dark, and put his two wet hands on the two copper plates of the vessel.

Cold metal. The faint tremor of the quicksilver when a cart went by. That was all.

He did it every night for a month. Jan watched the first three nights and then went back to bed. Hrubý adjusted things. He moved the lodestone a finger’s width nearer the vessel, and then a finger’s width away. He moved the needle on the galena. He tightened the vise on the crystal and loosened it again. He wrote it all down in a notebook, each night, in his plain assayer’s hand: the weather, the hour, the position of everything, what he did, what he felt. Every page said the same thing at the bottom. Nothing.

He was not disappointed. That surprised him. He had expected nothing. He had built it to see what happened, and nothing was what happened, and that was an answer too. He would have given it up in another month, and written in the notebook the Book of the Seven Waters is an allegory, as the man from Harran said, and gone back to looking at crucibles.

Then came the night of the twenty-third of July.

It had been hot for a week, heavy and still, the air over the city thick and yellow, and that day a storm had been building in the west all afternoon. You could see it from the bridge: a black wall over the White Mountain, with the lightning inside it, very far. In the evening the air in the streets was so close that dogs lay panting in doorways and the swallows flew low over the river. The hair on Hrubý’s arms stood up when he walked home. When he touched the iron latch of the house door, a small blue spark jumped to his finger and stung.

He went down to the cellar the way he always did. He washed three times. He wet his feet. He drank the cup. He lit the small candle under the black stone and blew out the big one and sat down.

Then, before he could put his hands on the plates, a gust of wind came down the chimney from the coming storm and blew out the small candle too.

He sat in the absolute dark, swearing softly. The candle had been under the black stone for a quarter of an hour; the stone was warm, he could feel the warmth of it a hand’s width away. He didn’t want to grope about for the tinderbox with wet hands. He thought, I’ll just put my hands on, once, and then I’ll light it again.

He leaned forward in the dark, and his knee knocked the oak vise, hard, and the crystal in it gave a small creak as the jaws bit down on it. He heard the quicksilver shiver in the vessel. He put out both his wet hands, and found the two copper plates, and laid his palms on them.

And the world opened.

He would try for the rest of his life to write down what happened next, and fail every time.

It began with his hands. A crawling in the skin of his palms, as if ants were walking very carefully across them. Then a taste on his tongue, sharp and clean, like biting a coin. He thought, it’s the storm, and nearly took his hands away, and didn’t.

Then, in the perfect blackness in front of his eyes, a light.

It wasn’t there. He knew it wasn’t there. It was the kind of light you see when you press your eyes. But it had a color, a deep warm rust-red, like the iron ore his father used to split open in the shaft. And it flickered in time with the crawling in his palms. And as he sat there, rigid, not breathing, it changed. It went to amber. To green, the green of old copper roofs in the rain. To blue. Violet. A silver-white so bright it was almost no color at all. And with each color, he heard something: not with his ears, but somewhere behind them, in the middle of his head, a tone. Low for the red. Higher for the amber. Higher still. Seven of them. Each a color and a sound at once, and each a number, one, two, three, and each a taste, iron and honey and crushed green stems and salt and the clean taste after rain and the coin, and his hands, his hands were moving on their own, a palm pressing down, a palm turning up—

Nen, he thought. Sa. Ri. The syllables in the book. They weren’t syllables. They were these.

Then all seven came together at once. They fought each other for a moment like cats in a sack. And then they settled, the way water settles, and there in front of him in the dark was a color that wasn’t a color, the color that is all of them when they agree, and he heard himself make a sound he had never made in his life.

It was not about gold. The seven metals weren’t seven stages of the soul. They were seven ways that something moved through the copper and the quicksilver and the stones, through the ground under the house and the sky over it and the storm in the west. The ground and the water and the air, singing, very slowly, in seven voices, so slowly that only a body perfectly still and perfectly wet and perfectly in the dark could hear it. And on top of the singing, quicker, a pattern. Groups. Coming and stopping. Coming again.

The book was not an allegory and not a recipe. It was an ear. Someone had written down how to build an ear.

And then, with a grief so sudden that it doubled him over with his hands still on the plates: everyone in every house in the city was asleep inside this, and had never heard it, and their grandfathers hadn’t either. It had been here the whole time.

He didn’t know how long it lasted. Afterward he thought perhaps the time it takes to say a long prayer. Perhaps less.

Then the storm arrived.

The lightning struck somewhere very close, across the street, on the church tower, with a crack that he felt in his teeth. In the vessel under his hands the quicksilver leapt. He felt it leap, all at once, up the side of the clay toward the wire that went to the roof, and touch something, and close. And the colors went out like a candle in a wind, all seven at once, and the crawling in his hands was gone, and the coin was gone, and the music was gone, and there was only the dark and the smell of burnt copper and the rain coming down outside, suddenly, enormously, on the roof and in the yard and down the well.

Jan found him there in the morning, on the cellar floor, with his back against the wall and his wet hands in his lap, laughing and weeping at once.

The boy ran for his mother, and she came down with a candle and a jug of beer and found the Imperial assayer on the floor of her cellar beside his strange machine with his face wet and shining, and asked him what in the name of all the saints had happened.

“It sang,” said Hrubý.

“What sang?”

“All of it.” He looked up at her. “The ground. The sky. The water in the well. It sang to me. It’s been singing the whole time. All our lives. Did you know that?”

She looked at him for a long moment, and then at the machine, the clay pot and the stones and the wires going up the chimney, and then she put the beer down beside him and took her son by the shoulder and went back upstairs without saying anything, and he heard her close the door very quietly at the top of the stairs.

He sat there a while longer.

Then he got up, slowly, and lit the candle, and looked at the apparatus. The needle on the galena had fused to the stone in a small black bead. The crystal in the vise had a crack running through it. The quicksilver lay in the bottom of the vessel, still and bright, as if nothing had happened. He touched the plates with his dry hand. Cold metal.

He sat down at the bench and opened the notebook to that night’s page. He wrote the date. He wrote the weather. He wrote the hour, and the position of the lodestone and the needle and the vise, and that the candle had blown out, and that his knee had struck the vise. All of it, plainly, the way an assayer writes.

Then he came to the line at the bottom where every other page said Nothing, and he held the pen over it for a long time.

He wrote: Everything.

Next · Part Three · Chapter ThreeThe Long Failure

Part Three · Chapter Three

The Long Failure

The notebooks of Tobiáš Hrubý run to nine volumes. The first is written in the plain hand of an assayer: weights, hours, weather, positions. By the fifth he is writing of green lions and a king drowned in a bath. Later readers took this as a descent into madness. It was the opposite. It was an honest man discovering that the plain words had no room in them for what he had to say, and reaching for the only other words there were.

Concordance of Fragments, Prague recension, Fragment 3

15 min read

He rebuilt it in a week.

A new needle of copper wire, thinner than a hair. A new crystal from the Miseroni offcut box. The same galena, turned over, so the needle could search a fresh face. The lodestone, the black stone, the vessel and the quicksilver at the fourth mark, all exactly where they’d been. He measured every distance with his dividers against the notebook, because he’d written everything down, every position, that night and every night before it. He was an assayer. He had the figures.

On the first night he washed three times and wet his feet and drank the cup and sat in the dark and put his hands on the plates.

Nothing.

“Of course nothing,” he told Jan in the morning. “There’s no storm. That night there was a storm in the west. The air was full of it. The latch sparked when I touched it. I’ll wait for a storm.”

He waited for a storm.

It came in the middle of August, a big one, rolling in over the White Mountain in the afternoon exactly as before, the sky going black and yellow, the swallows low. He went down to the cellar an hour before it broke. He did everything. He let the small candle burn under the black stone for a quarter of an hour, and then blew it out himself, because it had been out that night. He put his knee against the oak vise and pressed it, the way his knee had struck it, so the crystal creaked. He laid his wet hands on the plates.

The crawling came.

He nearly cried out. It was faint, so faint he wasn’t sure, a prickle in his palms like the first moment of pins and needles. The coin was on his tongue, perhaps. He sat rigid, waiting for the red.

It didn’t come. The prickle grew a little, and faded, and grew. Then the lightning struck somewhere far off across the river, and the quicksilver jumped in the vessel, and the prickle was gone. He sat there until the rain stopped. There was nothing more.

In the notebook he wrote: Ants, perhaps. Coin, perhaps. No color. No sound. Storm farther than before; the strike across the river, not across the street. Distance matters?

That was the first year.

He spent it the way an honest man spends a year on a problem: one thing at a time. He moved the rod higher, on a second pole lashed to the first, so that it stood above every roof in the street. He moved the well plate deeper, and then shallower, and then put it in the river instead of the well, on a long wire down through the yard and over the wall, and the neighbors complained. He tried the lodestone on the left side of the vessel and the right. He tried more quicksilver and less. He searched the galena with the needle for hours at a time, every face of it, and found that in some places, when he passed a little current from a pair of plates in brine through the needle and the stone, it went one way and not the other, exactly as the book said. He marked the places. He tried each of them on the nights of storms.

He tried warming the black stone more, and less. He tried pressing the crystal harder, and then not at all. He sat in the dark at every hour of the night. He tried it with his feet wet and his feet dry, to be sure, and with the cup and without, and with three washings and with one. He didn’t believe the washings mattered. He tested them anyway. A man who was going to test the needle had to test the salt.

The crawling came eleven times that year. Always on storm nights. Always faint. Twice the coin came with it, sharp and clean, and once, for the space of a single breath, in the black in front of his eyes, he thought there was a smudge of rust-red, and he made a sound, and it was gone.

That was all.

At the end of the year he sat in the cellar on the anniversary night, the twenty-third of July, with every piece in its place, and the sky perfectly clear, and the stars out over Prague, and he did not even put his hands on the plates. He sat and looked at the apparatus in the light of the candle and did the arithmetic.

One night in four hundred. One night out of every night he’d sat there. One night, and he didn’t know why.

“Perhaps it wasn’t the machine,” Jan said. The boy had come down with a candle; he was fifteen now, and taller, and he sat on the bottom step and watched Hrubý the way you watch a sick man. “Perhaps it was you. Perhaps you were tired. My mother says you hadn’t slept.”

“Perhaps.” Hrubý didn’t look round. “That’s a very good thought, Jan. Write it in the book.”

“You think I’m wrong.”

“I think you might be right. That’s worse.” He rubbed his face with both hands. “If it was me, then I can’t test it. I can’t put myself in a vise and press. I can’t search myself with a needle.” He laughed a little. “I can test everything in this cellar except the one part that might matter.”

The second year he began to do it the same way every time.

He told himself it was method. If he couldn’t find which part mattered, then he would at least hold every part the same, so that when it happened again he would know what had been true. So he blew out the candle under the black stone at the same moment every night, by the bell of the church across the street. He wore the same linen shirt he had worn that night, which his landlady washed for him every week and which became thin at the elbows. He sat on the same stool. He pressed the vise with the same knee, the left one, with the same force, which he practiced until he could do it without thinking. He washed three times, because that night he had washed three times. He drank the cup to the bottom.

And somewhere in that year, without his noticing, it stopped being method.

He noticed it on a night in the spring when he had a fever. Jan’s mother had begged him to stay in bed. He’d got up anyway and gone down to the cellar shaking, because it was a night with a storm in the west, and he had to be there, he had to sit there the right way. He washed three times with hands that were burning. He drank the cup and was sick on the floor. He wiped it up and drank another cup, because the cup had to be drunk, and sat on the stool in the thin linen shirt and blew out the candle on the bell, and pressed the vise with his left knee, and laid his hands on the plates.

Nothing came. Of course nothing came. Halfway through the night he found himself sitting in the dark, sick and shivering, with his hands on cold copper, saying the seven syllables under his breath over and over, nen sa ri hu mai tef ol, as if they were a prayer that would make it happen.

He stopped. He sat very still.

He thought of Doctor Aurelius from Saxony, who’d put his lentil of gold under a false bottom and believed, Hrubý was quite sure now, that if he did it often enough and well enough in front of enough witnesses, one day there would be gold in the crucible without the false bottom. He thought of the priests in the cathedral up on the hill, saying the same words in the same order every morning because their fathers had. He thought of himself, a year ago, saying to the secretary, you only have to want to know what happened more than you want it to have been gold.

He was looking at the gold. He’d stopped looking at the crucible. He had become a man who wore the right shirt.

He took his hands off the plates and sat in the dark and wept, quietly, so as not to wake the house. Then, because he couldn’t think of anything else to do, he went back to bed.

He went down again the next night. The shirt. The bell. The knee. He knew exactly what he was doing now, and he did it anyway.

In the third year, plain words failed him.

He’d been writing the notebook every night in the same assayer’s hand. Weather, hour, positions, what he did, what he felt. Nothing, at the bottom, most nights. But at the back of the notebook, on the pages he never showed Jan, he had been trying to write down the night of the twenty-third of July. What had happened. What he had understood. And every time he tried, it came out wrong.

The colors had sounds. True, and nothing; it sounded like a man describing a fever. The red was iron and one and my hand going down. True. Nothing. Every plain sentence he wrote was accurate, and it was like describing music by giving the weight of the lute.

So, slowly, without deciding to, he started writing it the way the old books wrote.

He’d always despised that language. The red lion and the green lion. The king and queen in the bath. The peacock’s tail. He’d thought it was the language of men who wanted to sound wise and be paid for it. Now, in the back of his notebook, he found that it was the only language he knew that had room in it for a color that was also a sound that was also a metal that was also a feeling in the hand.

By the fifth year the back of the notebook was full of lions and kings and baths and stars, and the front of it was full of weights and hours and Nothing, and he couldn’t have said which half was more truthful.

The Jesuit’s letter came to court in the winter of the fourth year.

It was read aloud at a dinner of the Emperor’s physicians, which Hrubý attended because one of the physicians owed him money. A priest of the Society of Jesus had gone to China, to the far end of the world, and lived among the Chinese and learned their language and read their old histories, and he’d written home about the wonders he’d found. The letter had come by way of Lisbon and Rome. The physicians passed it round with great amusement. The Chinese, it said, had an ancient historian who wrote that their First Emperor, the one who built the great wall, had been buried under a mountain in a tomb made like the world: the heavens above, picked out in pearls, and the earth below, with all its rivers. And the rivers were made of quicksilver. They flowed. By machines, the historian said, the quicksilver was made to flow in the rivers and the seas, and to circulate.

“Imagine the cost,” said one of the physicians, and the others laughed.

Hrubý didn’t laugh. He sat very still at the end of the table with his cup in his hand.

The heavens above and the earth below. Quicksilver between them, moving. He thought: They built one. At the far end of the earth, someone had built the vessel on his cellar bench the size of a mountain.

He went home and wrote it in the back of the notebook. In the east there was a king who made the silver water into rivers under a sky of pearls, so that the whole world would sing in his grave. Then he sat looking at the sentence and thought: I’ve become one of them. A year ago he’d have asked how much quicksilver, and from which mine, and how they’d kept it from poisoning the workmen. Now he heard about a tomb on the other side of the world and knew, at once and without any measurement, what it was for.

The worst of it was that he was sure he was right.

In the fifth year he met the Emperor’s new mathematician on the stone bridge over the river.

The old Danish astronomer Tycho, the one with the silver nose, had died the autumn before, and the Emperor had given his post to his assistant, a thin, nervous, bright-eyed Swabian named Kepler, who had bad eyesight and worse luck and talked very fast. Hrubý had seen him about the castle. He’d heard that Kepler believed the planets moved in a music. That the distances between them were set by the same ratios as the notes of a scale, and that God had made the heavens as a harmony, and that the mathematician meant to prove it.

They stood together at the parapet of the bridge in the cold, watching the ice on the river, and Hrubý told him.

He hadn’t meant to. He hadn’t told anyone in five years except Jan. But Kepler asked him what an assayer was doing in the Emperor’s library so often, and something in the man’s quick, hungry, half-blind face made Hrubý tell him the truth. The book. The vessel. The night of the twenty-third of July. The seven colors that were sounds.

Kepler listened without interrupting, which Hrubý had been told was rare.

“Seven,” he said at last. “Seven tones. And you say the book gives measures? Ratios?”

“The heights in the vessel. The distances. They’re not even. They go up like the holes on a flute. The first wide and then closer.”

“Give them to me.” The mathematician had a little notebook out already, and a pencil of lead. “Exactly. Do you have them?”

Hrubý gave them. He knew them by heart. Kepler wrote fast, muttering, and did something with the figures, and stopped and looked at them, and Hrubý saw his face go very still.

“They’re harmonic,” Kepler said quietly. “Small whole numbers. As four to three. As three to two.” He looked up. “Do you know what this is? This is the music. These are the ratios. If I can find these in the distances of the planets, in their speeds—”

“It isn’t the planets,” said Hrubý.

“What?”

“It isn’t up there.” He pointed at the sky, grey over the castle. “Not the stars. Not the far sky. It’s here. It was in the ground and the water and the air. The storm. It came up through my hands from the well under the house. It’s the near sky, the one between us and the clouds. Not the far one.”

Kepler looked at him, and then at his figures, and then at the sky, and Hrubý saw the thing happen in his face that had happened, centuries before, in the faces of the sons of Musa: the moment when a man who is very good at one thing takes the piece that fits it and puts down the rest.

“Perhaps,” said Kepler kindly. “Perhaps it’s in both. As above, so below, they say. But the heavens are where I can measure.” He tapped the notebook. “These ratios I can take to the heavens. Your well I can’t.” He hesitated, then said, with real curiosity: “You heard it? With your ears?”

“Not with my ears.”

“I’ve never heard it at all,” said Kepler. “I only hear it with my mind. In numbers. I think it’s the most beautiful thing in creation, and I’ve never heard one note.” He put the notebook away. “Then you’re luckier than I am, assayer. Or madder.”

“Both,” said Hrubý.

Kepler laughed, and clapped him on the shoulder, and went away across the bridge toward the castle with Hrubý’s seven ratios in his pocket, to look for them among the planets. Hrubý heard, many years later, that he’d found them.

Once, in the sixth year, the Emperor’s keeper let him into the Kunstkammer at night to see a glass prism that had come from Venice.

It was a long triangle of perfect glass, set in a silver frame on a velvet stand, and when you held it in a beam of sunlight from a slot in the shutter it threw a band of colors onto the white wall. The keeper had shown it to a dozen courtiers. They argued about how many colors there were. Most said five. Some said six. One, a Dutch painter, insisted on four, with the rest being mixtures.

Hrubý stood in front of the wall for a long time.

“Seven,” he said.

“Seven?” said the keeper. “Where?”

Hrubý didn’t point at the wall. Without meaning to, he touched himself instead: low, at the base of the spine; the belly; the hollow under the ribs; the heart; the throat; the brow. Then the crown of his head, where nothing on the wall was.

“There,” he said, and the keeper laughed, uncertainly, and put the prism away.

Then he went home and didn’t sleep, and in the morning he could not have told anyone whether he had found something or only drawn a picture of what he needed to be true.

In the eighth year, Jan, who was twenty-two now and an assayer’s assistant at the mint because Hrubý had trained him, came down to the cellar on the night of the twenty-third of July and sat on the bottom step, as he’d sat when he was fourteen.

Hrubý was on the stool in the thin linen shirt. The candle under the black stone was burning. He had his left knee against the vise. He was waiting for the bell.

“Master,” said Jan quietly. “Can I ask you something?”

“Always.”

“Do you still think it’ll happen?”

Hrubý looked at the apparatus. The vessel. The quicksilver at the fourth mark. The galena with its needle, the twentieth needle. The crystal, the fourth crystal. The black stone. The wires going up the chimney to the gilded rod and down the well to the cold water. Eight years of figures in the front of nine notebooks, and eight years of lions in the back.

“No,” he said. “I don’t think it’ll happen.”

“Then why do you come down?”

The bell started across the street. Hrubý leaned forward and blew out the candle, on the first stroke, exactly, the way he had every night for eight years. In the dark he pressed the vise with his left knee. He heard the crystal creak.

“Because I can’t stop,” he said. “Because I heard it once. Because if I don’t come down, then it’s over, and if I do come down, then there’s still one chance in four hundred, or one in four thousand, or none, and I can’t tell which, and the not knowing is the only thing I have left of it.” He put his wet hands on the cold plates. “I know what I am, Jan. I’m Doctor Aurelius. I’m my own false bottom. I’ve seen the gold once and I’ve spent eight years putting the lentil back in the crucible so I can watch it come out again.”

Jan was quiet in the dark for a long time.

“I believe you heard it,” he said at last.

Hrubý didn’t answer.

“I’ve never heard anything. I don’t think I ever will. I’ve sat where you sit, on the nights you weren’t here. I’ve done it all. Nothing.” The young man’s voice was steady. “But I saw your face that morning. I was fourteen, and I was frightened, and I’ve never forgotten it. Whatever happened to you, it was real. You’re the most honest man I know. You’d never have told anyone if it wasn’t.”

Hrubý sat with his hands on the copper.

“Thank you,” he said, finally, very quietly. “That’s more than I can say for myself.”

They sat together in the dark until the bell rang midnight. Nothing came.

In the morning, in the back of the ninth notebook, under the lions, he wrote: The one who heard it once is the poorest man alive, because he knows what he has lost, and the one who never heard is richer, because he still believes.

Then he turned to the front and wrote, in the assayer’s hand: Twenty-third July. Clear. All positions as before. Jan present. Nothing.

Next · Part Three · Chapter FourThe Emperor’s Gold

Part Three · Chapter Four

The Emperor’s Gold

Rudolf of Habsburg assembled the greatest collection of wonders in Europe: clocks that played music, stones that cured poison, a cup carved from a single crystal, the horn of a unicorn. Contemporaries called it a cabinet of the world. It was. It was the world as the specialists were beginning to leave it: every piece perfect, every piece in its own drawer, and nothing in the room that spoke.

Concordance of Fragments, Prague recension, Fragment 4

14 min read

The rumor took eight years to reach the castle, and when it got there it had turned into gold.

It began with Jan’s mother. She never told anyone, she said afterward, not really; she’d only told her sister, on the morning she found the assayer on the cellar floor, that he had seen something in his machine that made him weep like a child. Her sister told the baker’s wife. The baker’s wife told a woman who sold eggs at the castle kitchens. By the end of the first year everyone in the Malá Strana knew that the Imperial assayer had a machine in his cellar that had shown him heaven. By the third year it was that he had seen an angel in it. By the fifth, the angel had told him the secret of the Great Work. By the eighth, in the year the Emperor’s own family declared him unfit to rule, the story that came up the hill into the castle was that Tobiáš Hrubý of the mint had made gold from quicksilver in his cellar, one summer night in a thunderstorm, and was hiding it from the Emperor.

The Emperor needed gold very badly that year.

His brother Matthias had made himself master of Hungary, and the archdukes had met in Vienna and named Matthias head of the house in the Emperor’s place. The treasury was empty. The mint was striking coins with less silver in every batch; Hrubý had been assaying them and writing the figures in his book with a sick feeling, every week. There were soldiers to pay and debts to bankers in Augsburg and Genoa. And somewhere in the castle, a clerk of the Imperial Chamber heard the story about the assayer and the quicksilver, and looked up the stores of the mint, and found that over eight years a great deal of the Emperor’s quicksilver had gone down the hill into the Malá Strana and not come back.

The clerk’s name was Rotter, and he came to the cellar on a morning in October with four men of the castle guard and a secretary with a ledger.

He was not cruel. Hrubý had seen a great many men from the Chamber over the years, and Rotter was one of the better ones: tired, precise, polite, with ink on his cuffs and a cough. He stood at the foot of the cellar stairs and looked at the apparatus for a long time without speaking. The vessel with its quicksilver at the fourth mark. The lodestone in its silver mount. The galena and the needle. The crystal in its vise. The black stone. The wires going up the chimney and down the well.

“So this is it,” he said.

“This is it,” said Hrubý.

“It makes gold?”

“No.”

Rotter looked at him with the patience of a man who has heard no from a great many alchemists. “The story says that it made gold. In the summer of ninety-eight. In a storm.”

“The story is wrong.” Hrubý stood with his back to the bench. He’d known someone would come one day. He had thought about what he’d say for eight years, and now that the day had come he found that the only thing he had to say was the truth. “It doesn’t make anything. It’s a kind of ear. One night it let me hear something. I’ve been trying to hear it again ever since. I never have.”

“Hear what?”

He opened his mouth. Shut it.

“Music,” he said finally. “The ground. The sky. I don’t know how to tell you.”

The secretary with the ledger had stopped writing. Rotter looked at Hrubý for a long moment, and then he coughed into his sleeve, and when he looked up again something in his face had become, very slightly, sorry.

“Master Hrubý,” he said, “I’ve been sent to find out whether you’ve been making gold from the Emperor’s quicksilver and keeping it. I’ll be honest with you. I hoped you had. It would have been simpler. I’d have taken the gold and you’d have been made a baron, like the Englishman, until it ran out.” He gestured at the apparatus. “Instead you tell me it’s an ear, and that it heard music once.”

“Yes.”

“Then I’m afraid you’ve spent eight years taking the Emperor’s quicksilver to listen to music in your cellar.” Rotter said it gently. “You understand what that is, in the Chamber’s ledger.”

“Yes,” said Hrubý.

“Write it,” Rotter said to the secretary. “One vessel of clay, containing quicksilver, to be weighed. Two plates of copper. One rod of copper, gilded, on the roof. One lodestone in a silver mount, the Emperor’s property, from the Kunstkammer. Wire of copper, by weight. Stones, various.” He turned back. “These will go up to the castle. The Chamber will assess them. You’ll come too.”

They put him in the White Tower.

It was not a dungeon. It was a room at the top of a stair, with a window and a bed and a bucket, the same room, the guard told him cheerfully, where the Englishman Kelley had been kept for a while before they moved him to the castle where he died. Hrubý sat on the bed and looked out at the roofs of the castle in the rain.

He was not frightened. He’d expected to be. Instead he felt something closer to relief, and it took him two days to understand what it was. For the first time in eight years, on the night of a storm, he would not be going down to the cellar. He would not be blowing out the candle on the bell or pressing the vise with his left knee or drinking the cup. He couldn’t. They had taken it.

On the third night there was a storm. He lay on the bed in the White Tower and listened to the thunder walk over the city, and for the first time since the twenty-third of July in the year ninety-eight, he slept through it.

He woke in the morning and wept for an hour, because he had slept.

On the ninth night a guard came for him after midnight with a lamp and told him to get up and put on his coat.

They took him down the stair and across the courtyard in the rain and in through a small door he’d never seen, and up and along a passage hung with tapestries, and through a series of rooms, each darker and stranger than the last. There were clocks in them, dozens of clocks, ticking in the dark. Paintings he couldn’t see. Things on shelves with the lamplight sliding over them: a stuffed crocodile, a cup of horn, a globe of the heavens, a mirror of black stone. A smell of wax and camphor and old wood.

The Kunstkammer. He’d been in its outer rooms once, for the prism. He had never been this far in.

In the last room, on a long table under a single lamp, someone had laid out his apparatus.

They’d set it up wrong. The vessel was at one end of the table and the lodestone at the other. The galena was in its cup with the needle lying beside it. The crystal was out of its vise. The gilded rod, unlashed from his chimney, lay along the floor beside the table like a spear. It looked exactly like what it was: a collection of things on a table, each one labeled with a small card in the Chamber’s hand. Vas fictile cum argento vivo. Magnes. Lapis plumbeus.

And in a chair at the far end of the table, in the shadow beyond the lamp, there was a man in black.

Hrubý had seen the Emperor perhaps four times in his life, always at a distance, always on some feast day, a small figure at a window. He’d never seen him this close. Rudolf was fifty-four that year and looked older. He was heavy, with the great Habsburg jaw and a grey beard and eyes that were sunk and very tired and very intelligent. He wore no jewels. He sat with his hands folded on the head of a stick, looking at the apparatus.

The guard went down on one knee. Hrubý, after a moment, did the same.

“Get up,” said the Emperor. His voice was soft and slow. “Both of you. Leave us.” The guard hesitated, and went. “You’re the assayer.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“Rotter says you have been listening to music on my quicksilver for eight years.” The Emperor didn’t smile. “He says you told him it was an ear. That it heard the ground, and the sky.”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“I have a great many things that are supposed to do remarkable things.” Rudolf lifted one hand from the stick and moved it slightly, taking in the dark room, the shelves, the clocks ticking in the rooms beyond. “That cup will sweat if there is poison in the wine. That stone will cure the plague. That horn is from a unicorn. That mirror shows the angels, the Englishman’s friend said. I have astronomers who can tell me the motions of every planet, and alchemists who tell me every month that the gold is nearly ready. I have clocks that play music, and paintings so true that you’d think you could walk into them.” He was quiet for a moment. “And none of it speaks to me. Do you understand? I have the most perfect things in the world in these rooms, assayer. And I sit with them at night, and they’re silent.”

Hrubý didn’t know what to say.

“Rotter said you heard something,” said the Emperor. “Once.”

“Once, Majesty.”

“Then let me hear it.”

He set it up properly.

It took him an hour, by the light of the single lamp, while the Emperor watched without a word from his chair. He moved the lodestone back beside the vessel, a finger’s width from the clay, the way it had been. He put the crystal back in the vise and tightened it. He set the needle on the galena at the place he had marked, years ago, where the breath passed one way only. He set the black stone on its little stand and lit a candle under it. He couldn’t raise the rod. There was no chimney, and no well. He found a window that opened onto the rain and propped the rod out of it as high as it would go, on a chair, with the wire trailing back to the table. For the earth he asked the Emperor’s leave to send for a bucket of water from the castle well, and the Emperor rang a small bell, and after a while a frightened servant brought one, and Hrubý put the second plate in it.

It was wrong. He knew it was wrong. The rod was too low and the bucket was not the ground. There was no storm. He did it anyway.

When it was done he asked for a basin, and salt.

The Emperor watched him wash his hands. Front and back, between each finger. Three times. Then Hrubý wet his feet in the basin, and drank a cup of salt water, and made the face.

“Why do you do that?” the Emperor asked.

“The book says to, Majesty.”

“Does it matter?”

“I don’t know,” said Hrubý. “I’ve tested it. It doesn’t seem to. I do it anyway.”

The Emperor looked at the basin for a long moment. Then, slowly, he leaned his stick against the table and held out his own hands.

Hrubý stared at him.

“If I’m to listen,” said Rudolf, “I’ll do it as the book says.”

So the Imperial assayer of the mint, who had been nine days in the White Tower for the theft of quicksilver, knelt on the floor of the Kunstkammer and washed the hands of the Holy Roman Emperor three times in salt water by lamplight. The Emperor’s hands were soft and heavy and cold. He held them out like a child. Then, without knowing why, Hrubý pressed his thumb into the center of each palm, one and then the other, the way a potter tests clay. The book didn’t say to. His hands did it anyway. Then he gave the Emperor the cup, and the Emperor drank it all, and made no face at all.

Hrubý blew out the lamp, and the candle under the black stone, and pressed the vise with his knee.

“Now put your hands on the plates,” he said into the dark.

He heard the Emperor’s chair creak. He heard the rustle of black velvet. He heard, very faintly, the small sound of skin touching cold copper.

Then, for a long time, he heard nothing at all except the rain at the window and the clocks ticking in the rooms beyond, dozens of them, all slightly out of time with each other.

“Well?” said the Emperor at last, softly.

“I’m sorry, Majesty.”

“There’s nothing.”

“No.”

“Not even for you?”

Hrubý had his own hands on the edge of the table, not on the plates. He had not put them on the plates. He found that he couldn’t.

“No,” he said. “Not even for me. Not for eight years.”

The Emperor was quiet for a long time in the dark. When he spoke again his voice was different. It was not angry. It was something worse. It was recognition.

“Then it’s like everything else in my collection,” he said. “Perfect. And silent.”

He lit the lamp again with shaking hands. The Emperor was sitting with his wet palms still on the plates, looking down at them.

“Does it make gold?” Rudolf asked.

“No, Majesty.”

“A pity.” The Emperor took his hands away and dried them slowly on his black sleeve. “If it made gold, I could keep it. Do you understand? My brother has taken Hungary. He’ll have Austria and Moravia next, and then Bohemia, and the crown. I have nothing left that I’m allowed to keep, except what pays for itself.” He looked at the apparatus on the table, the vessel and the stones and the gilded rod propped in the window. “This doesn’t pay for itself. The Chamber has weighed it. There’s a good deal of quicksilver, which is mine. There’s copper, which is mine. There’s gold on that rod, which is mine, which I paid you for in wages without knowing it. They’ll want it for the mint. I can’t stop them. I can’t stop anyone anymore.”

“I understand, Majesty.”

“Do you?” The Emperor looked at him with those sunk, tired, clever eyes. “I don’t think you do. I think you think I’m taking it from you. I’m not. It was never yours, and it was never mine either. It belonged to someone who could hear it.” He picked up his stick. “There’s no one like that left. That’s why it’s being melted. Not because of my brother. Because no one can hear it.”

He stood, heavily, and went to the door. At the door he stopped.

“The music,” he said, without turning round. “When you heard it. Was it beautiful?”

“Yes, Majesty.”

“More beautiful than my clocks?”

“Yes.”

The Emperor stood in the doorway a moment longer, a heavy shape in black against the darker dark of the rooms beyond, where the clocks were ticking.

“Good,” he said. “Then something was.”

And he went out.

They let Hrubý out of the White Tower the next week, because the Emperor said so, and because there was nothing to charge him with that anyone wanted to write down. But there was one more thing the Chamber required, and Rotter came to the mint himself to tell him, with his cough and his tired, sorry face.

“They want it assayed before it’s melted,” he said. “So the ledger will be right. You’re the assayer.” He hesitated. “I’m sorry. I asked them to send someone else. There isn’t anyone else who can do it today.”

So Hrubý assayed it.

He did it at his own bench in the mint, in the grey November light, with his own tongs and scales and touchstone, while Rotter’s secretary sat beside him with the ledger. He did it the way he did everything, carefully, plainly, the way he’d been taught as a boy at the furnace in Kutná Hora. He scraped the gold from the rod onto a square of parchment and weighed it. He weighed the copper of the rod, and the copper of the two plates, and the wire. He poured the quicksilver out of the clay vessel, slowly, into one of the Imperial iron flasks from Idrija, and weighed the flask, and subtracted the flask. His hands didn’t shake. He was surprised by that.

The secretary wrote it all down.

Gold, from the rod: four ducats’ weight, less a grain. Copper of the rod and plates, by weight. Wire. Quicksilver: one flask and a third. Lodestone in silver mount: to be returned to the Kunstkammer.

“And these?” The secretary pointed at what was left on the bench. The galena in its little clay cup, with the twentieth needle fused to it in a bead. The cracked crystal. The black stone from the island of the south, dull, ridged, like a burnt stick. The empty clay vessel with the four marks scratched inside.

Hrubý looked at them.

“Stones,” he said. “Of no value.”

“Shall I throw them out?”

“No,” said Hrubý. “I’ll take them.”

The secretary looked at Rotter. Rotter, after a moment, nodded.

That afternoon the gold went into the mint’s crucible with the rest of that week’s gold, and the copper went into the copper, and Hrubý stood by the furnace and watched it go, because the assayer had to sign that it had been done. The gold from his rod ran into the bright mass of the Emperor’s gold and was lost in it at once. In a week it would be ducats with the Emperor’s face on them, paying soldiers who were waiting to change sides.

He signed the ledger.

He walked home down the hill in the dusk with the stones in a basket on his arm, and the nine notebooks under his other arm, because no one at the Chamber had wanted those. Words weren’t worth anything by weight.

In the cellar, the bench was bare. The well cover was off; someone had pulled up the plate and its wire and left them coiled on the ground, and then taken them anyway. The copper wire from the chimney was gone too. Only the empty iron brackets were left on the roof, where the gilded rod had pointed at the sky for eight years.

He set the basket down on the bench and sat on the stool. He didn’t light a candle. After a while he realized it was the hour of the bell, and that he’d heard the first stroke, and that his breath had gone in, as it did every night, to blow out a flame that wasn’t there.

He sat with that for a long time, in the dark, with his dry hands in his lap.

Next · Part Three · Chapter FiveThe Coding

Part Three · Chapter Five

The Coding

SEPTEM AQUAE: …a small book of seven woodcut emblems with Latin mottoes, printed at Prague in 1610 in an edition of about three hundred copies, attributed to “T. H., assayer.” It is one of more than four hundred alchemical emblem books printed in Europe between 1550 and 1700, and was for three centuries regarded as among the least interesting of them. That was the point. The best place to hide a true riddle is in a library full of false ones…

Concordance of Fragments, Prague recension, Fragment 5

15 min read

The laundress came to the mint in the first week of Advent, with snow on her shawl.

Hrubý didn’t know her. He looked up from the assay bench and saw a very old woman standing in the doorway of the long room, small and bent, in a brown wool dress and a white apron, with her hands folded in front of her. Her hands were red and swollen from water. Laundry hands. The castle laundresses all had them; you could tell them anywhere in the city.

“You’re the assayer,” she said. “Hrubý. The one who washed the Emperor’s hands.”

He put down his tongs very slowly.

“Who told you that?”

“Pavel. The boy who carried the bucket from the well that night. He’s my granddaughter’s husband’s brother.” She came a step into the room. Her eyes were pale blue and very clear in her lined face, and they were looking not at his face but at his hands. “He said you washed the Emperor’s hands in salt water. Three times. Front and back, between each finger. And then pressed your thumb into each palm.” She paused. “He said you told the Emperor the book said to. But the book didn’t say about the thumb, did it?”

Hrubý looked at her for a long moment. Then he said, very quietly, “No.”

“No,” the old woman agreed. “It never does.” She unfolded her red hands. “My name’s Marta. I’ve come to see if you’re one of us.”

He took her home with him, because he didn’t know what else to do, and because the mint was no place for this.

In the cellar she looked for a long time at the bare bench and the basket of stones and the coiled wire someone had forgotten to take from the bottom of the well. She touched nothing. Then she asked for a basin, and salt, and he brought them, and she washed her hands.

Front and back. Between each finger. Three times.

Then she took his hands, without asking, and washed them too. He let her. Her hands were rough and very warm. When she had finished she pressed her thumb into the center of each of his palms, one and then the other, the way a potter tests clay. He had done that to the Emperor, without knowing why. He watched her do it to him, and his throat closed.

“Who taught you?” he said.

“My grandmother. And her grandmother taught her.” Marta sat down on the cellar stool as if it were hers. “All the women of my line. We wash three times before we eat, and before we bake, and before we touch a newborn. We teach the little ones a counting song with seven words that don’t mean anything.” She half sang it, under her breath, in the cracked voice of a very old woman, a skipping rhythm: “Nena, sa, ri, hu, maj, tef, ol.” The ol came out round and long, like the end of a bell. “Once a month, on the dark of the moon, we stand in a dark room with our feet in a basin and our palms on the wall, and count. And we cut a little sign on our salt crocks. Three strokes and a hook.”

“Why?”

“For whoever comes,” said Marta.

He sat down on the bottom step. He had to.

“My grandmother’s grandmother came from the south,” she said. “Over the mountains, from a city built on the water, where she’d been a lace-maker. Before that, my grandmother said, her people came from the east, from across the sea. Before that, nobody knows. It goes back and back.” She looked at her red hands. “There aren’t many of us. A family here. A family there. A lace-maker in Venice. A widow in Kraków. Some of them don’t even know there are others. We keep the washing and the counting and the song and the sign. That’s all we keep. Nobody ever told us what it was for, except that it’s for whoever comes. And that when they come, they’ll need us to have kept it right.”

“Have you ever heard anything?” said Hrubý. “When you count. In the dark.”

“No,” said Marta. “Never. Not once in seventy-one years.” She said it simply, the way you’d say you had never been to Rome. “Nor my mother, nor hers. My grandmother said her grandmother heard something once, as a girl, in Venice, during a storm, with her hands on a bronze door. She was never sure. That’s the most anyone in my line has heard in two hundred years. A girl, once, in a storm, not sure.” She looked up at him with her clear blue eyes. “And then Pavel comes home from the castle and tells his brother that the assayer washed the Emperor’s hands three times and pressed the palms, and said there was a book, and that he had heard music in a machine once, in a storm.” Her voice didn’t change, but her red hands had tightened in her lap. “So I put on my good shawl and walked up to the mint. Because I am seventy-one years old, and I have done this every day of my life, and I have never once met anyone who heard.”

So he told her.

He told her the whole of it, in the dark of the cellar, with the snow falling outside in the yard. The book from Baghdad by way of Toledo. The man from Harran who had copied what he did not understand. The vessel, the stones, the rod, the well. The night of the twenty-third of July, and the candle blowing out, and his knee striking the vise. The ants and the coin. The seven colors that were sounds that were metals that were numbers that were tastes. The seven groups. Alive.

He didn’t try to say it in plain words. He had learned that plain words wouldn’t hold it. He told it the way he’d been writing it in the back of the notebooks, in lions and kings and baths and stars, and found that Marta, who had never read a book in her life, followed every word of it better than Kepler had.

When he had finished, she was crying.

She cried quietly, without covering her face, sitting very straight on the stool with her red hands in her lap and the tears running down into the lines of her cheeks. He didn’t know what to do. He sat on the step and waited.

“So it’s real,” she said at last.

“It was real. Once.”

“Once is real.” She wiped her face with the corner of her apron. “Two hundred years, my line has been washing its hands for a thing that might have been a girl’s fancy in a storm. And it was real.” She looked at him. “And then?”

“And then nothing. For ten years.” He told her about the long failure. The rebuilding, the storms, the shirt, the bell, the knee. The notebooks. The night he had realized what he’d become. Jan on the bottom step saying I believe you heard it. The Chamber, and Rotter, and the White Tower, and the Emperor’s hands. The assay. The gold from his rod running into the Emperor’s gold. The basket of stones of no value.

“They’ve taken it,” he said. “All of it. All the metal. There’s nothing left but stones and words.
