And I couldn’t make it work even when I had it.” He looked at the bare bench. “I’m a man who heard it once and spent ten years failing to hear it again. That’s all I am. I’m sorry. I’m not the one you were waiting for.”

Marta was quiet for a long time.

“No,” she said. “You’re not.” She said it kindly. “Whoever comes will hear it, and keep hearing it. You can’t. That’s not your fault. But you heard it once, and you know where everything goes. The rod and the well and the stones and the vessel and the washing.” She nodded at the nine notebooks on the shelf. “And you wrote it down.”

“In lions,” said Hrubý. “In kings and baths. Nobody will understand a word of it. The plain half says nothing on every page, and the other half is madness.”

“Good,” said Marta.

He looked at her.

“What happens to a thing that people understand?” she said. “You’ve just told me. They weigh it. They melt it for the gold on the rod. Your Emperor wanted it until he found it didn’t make gold, and then he couldn’t keep it. Everything that’s worth something gets taken. That’s the whole of the world, assayer.” She leaned forward on the stool. “But what’s a lion worth? What’s a king in a bath? Who would steal a riddle?”

He didn’t understand at first. She had to explain it to him three times, in her plain laundress’s way, and even then he argued.

“You want me to make a book of emblems,” he said. “Like the others. Like the hundred others on the Emperor’s shelves. Pictures and riddles.”

“Pictures,” said Marta. “Cut in wood and printed. Many of them, a few hundred, so they go everywhere. Not one book in one library that can be burned, or one machine in one cellar that can be melted. Pictures. Riddles in Latin under them, the way the gentlemen like. So they look like all the others.”

“But they’re nonsense. All the others. Ninety-nine in a hundred are charlatans writing riddles so they can sell powders. Anyone who reads mine will think it’s the same. They’ll put it on the shelf with the rest and never look at it twice.”

“Yes,” said Marta. “That’s what I’m asking.”

He stared at her.

“Whoever comes,” she said patiently, “won’t be a gentleman looking for gold. Those don’t come for this. They’ll be someone who looks at the crucible. Like you. Someone who reads your lions and says, wait. That’s not a lion. That’s a vessel. That’s how far the stone that loves iron sits from the clay.” She tapped his knee with one red finger. “Your pictures will be lost among a thousand others, and nobody will steal them, and nobody will melt them, and nobody will burn them, because nobody will think they’re worth anything. And the one who comes will find them. Because the one who comes is the kind who reads everything.”

He thought of Sinan of Harran, writing I believe this book to be an allegory, and copying every measure anyway. He thought of the unknown Toledan who’d written I have done the same. He thought of himself in the Emperor’s library at thirty-eight, taking down a book because of the word waters.

“It would take a year,” he said. “More. To draw them right. So that every measure is in them. Every part. In pictures that look like nonsense.” He found that his hands were moving on his knees, already, the way they moved when he was working something out at the bench. “The vessel would have to be the bath. The king and queen. The plates would be pillars. The lodestone a dragon, or a wolf. The galena, the needle in the grey stone: that would have to be something small. A bee, perhaps. Searching a flower.”

“You see?” said Marta. “You know already.”

He stopped.

“Why should I?” he said. And it came out of him harder than he’d meant it, ten years of it. “Tell me that. Why should I spend a year cutting riddles for someone who might come in a hundred years, or a thousand, or never? I heard it once. Once. And then I lost it, and I’ve spent ten years in this cellar like a dog waiting at a grave, and I’ll never hear it again. You’ve never heard it. Not once in seventy-one years. Your mother never heard it. You told me so.” He was standing now. He didn’t remember standing. “How can you do it? How can you wash your hands every day of your life and stand in the dark every month and teach the little ones that song, for something you have never felt? How can you serve a thing you’ve never felt?”

The old woman looked up at him from the stool, with her clear blue eyes and her red hands folded in her lap. She didn’t seem angry, or hurt. She seemed, if anything, sorry for him.

“The same way you do now,” she said.

He sat down again on the step.

He sat there a long time. The snow had stopped in the yard. Somewhere across the street the church bell began to ring the hour, and he heard his own breath go in, the way it did every night, to blow out a candle that wasn’t there, and he heard Marta hear it.

The same way you do now.

He had come down to this cellar four thousand nights without hearing anything. He had washed his hands and drunk the cup and pressed the vise for something he could no longer feel, and could not prove, and could barely remember, because once it had been true.

He was exactly what she was. He had been for ten years. He’d only thought that the one night made him different.

“Yes,” he said at last. “All right.”

Marta nodded, as if he’d agreed to carry a basket for her.

“Good,” she said. “Then I’ll bring you salt.”

It took him fourteen months.

He drew every emblem a dozen times before he was satisfied, at the bench where the apparatus had stood, with the stones of no value in front of him for reference. Jan came every evening after the mint closed and read the notebooks aloud to him, the plain half and the lion half, while he drew. Marta came on Sundays with salt and bread and sat on the stool and watched and said nothing, except once, about the bee, that it needed more legs.

When he was done there were seven.

The first was a king and a queen sitting naked in a bath between two pillars, with a wolf crouching beside the bath, its nose not quite touching the stone, and a stream of stars falling into the water from above. That was the vessel and the plates and the lodestone. The distance between the wolf’s nose and the bath, if you measured it against the height of the bath, was the distance in the book.

The second was a bee searching a grey flower for the one place where it could drink. That was the galena and the needle.

The third was a lion with its paw pressing down on a block of ice, and the ice weeping. The fourth was a black salamander lying in a small flame, with ash rising toward it out of the fire. The crystal under pressure; the black stone warmed.

The fifth was a tower with a golden spire reaching up into a storm cloud, and roots going down from its foundations into dark water. The rod and the well. The sky-mouth and the earth-mouth, though he did not know those words.

The sixth was a man sitting in a dark room, with a candle just put out beside him, smoking, and his feet in a basin, and his two hands laid on two pillars. Beside him, on a stool, a cup. In the corner of that one, very small, he drew an old woman with red hands. Nobody would ever know why.

And the seventh was a star.

It had seven points. At six of them he put the signs of six of the wandering stars, in the order the old book gave for the colors, not the order of the heavens: Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, the Moon. Since a woodcut had no color, the signs would have to carry the colors for him. Iron’s red. Gold’s amber. Copper’s green. Quicksilver’s blue. Tin’s violet. Silver’s white. Beneath each sign he cut a syllable of the singing-masters’ scale: ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.

The seventh point he left bare. No sign. No syllable. Where Saturn should have been, and the color beyond color, there was only the empty white of the paper.

Inside the star, he traced the last leaf of Sinan’s book, the way the colors went. A ribbon binding the signs of Mercury and the Sun together, as one. From them a line to Jupiter. From Jupiter to the Moon. From the Moon to Mars. From Mars to Venus. And from Venus a short line rising toward the empty seventh point, which stopped partway, and didn’t reach it. A question with no answer yet. He didn’t know what it asked. He had never known. He copied it the way it was.

Round the edge of the star, for a motto, he cut two words from Sinan’s colophon. Fac similiter. Do the same.

And at the very bottom, very small, in the corner where the block-cutter usually put his mark, three short strokes and a hook.

The book was printed in the spring of 1610 by a printer in the Old Town who printed a great many such books and asked no questions about any of them. Three hundred copies. Septem Aquae, on the title page. The Seven Waters, in Seven Figures, by T. H., Assayer. The Chamber did not notice. Nobody noticed. It sold slowly, to the kind of gentleman who bought such things, and was carried away in saddlebags and boxes to Vienna and Leipzig and Leiden and Kraków and Paris, and put on shelves with the others.

Marta died that summer, in the castle laundry, with her hands in the water. Hrubý heard about it a week later and walked up to the castle and stood in the laundry yard among the steaming tubs, and one of the young laundresses came over, a thin girl with red hands, and looked at him, and said, You’re the assayer, and then, very quietly, She said you’d come.

He gave the girl a copy of the book. She couldn’t read. She looked at the pictures for a long time, and at the sixth, at the tiny old woman with red hands in the corner, she put her finger on it and smiled.

He died himself in the winter of the next year, a few months before the Emperor, in his lodgings in the Malá Strana. Jan was with him. He left Jan the nine notebooks and the basket of stones and the copper wire that had been forgotten at the bottom of the well, and a letter that said only, Keep them with the salt. Whoever comes will need them whole. On the last night he asked Jan to wash his hands. Jan did. Three times, front and back, between each finger, and pressed his thumb into each palm, though no one had ever taught him that.

Seventy years later, in the royal library in Paris, a junior librarian in a powdered wig was working through a crate of books that had come to the king from the estate of a cardinal.

He was cataloguing them, as he catalogued everything, quickly and correctly and without much interest. He had a long day of it ahead. Missals. Histories. A treatise on fortification. Six books of alchemy, which he sighed at. Every cardinal who’d ever lived seemed to have collected alchemy.

He opened the last of them. A thin quarto, much worn. Septem Aquae. Pragae, 1610. Seven woodcuts. A king and queen in a bath. A bee. A lion on a block of ice. A salamander. A tower. A man in the dark with his hands on two pillars. And a seven-pointed star, with the signs of the planets at six of its points, and the seventh point blank, which he assumed was a printing error, and a little crooked line running inside it from point to point that didn’t quite arrive anywhere.

He turned it over, checked the binding, and wrote in the register in his neat hand: Septem Aquae. Emblemata alchymica. Prague, 1610. Seven figures. Of no particular interest.

Then he gave it a shelf mark, and slid it into its place on a long shelf in a long room among four hundred others like it, and closed the glass door of the case, and went on to the next crate.

In the dark behind the glass, between a book of green lions and a book of golden eggs, the star waited with its seventh point empty.

Interlude

The Universal Language

Paris, 1827

SUDRE, JEAN-FRANÇOIS: …music teacher of Albi and Paris, inventor of the langue musicale universelle later called Solresol, in which every word is built from the seven syllables of the scale and may therefore be spoken, sung, played, painted in seven colors, or signed with seven gestures of the hand. He believed to the end of his life that he had invented it. He was mistaken, though not in any way he could have discovered. A man can only invent what he has not already been given…

Concordance of Fragments, Paris recension, Fragment 1

11 min read

The salon was too hot, and Clara Ashby’s father had already fallen asleep.

He did it at every concert. He was a piano-maker from Clerkenwell who had come to Paris to look at the Érard workshops and buy French felts for his hammers, and he loved music more than anyone Clara knew, and the moment anyone began to play it he closed his eyes and was gone. She let him sleep. She was nineteen, and it was her first time out of England, and she’d made him bring her tonight because the card had said Une langue universelle, and she had wanted very badly to know what that could mean.

There were perhaps sixty people on the gilt chairs. Officers in blue with high collars. Two old men from the Institut with ribbons in their buttonholes. Ladies with fans. A row of schoolboys in black. At the front, on a low platform beside a pianoforte, stood a small, eager, dark-haired man in a brown coat, and a boy of about ten with a violin under his arm.

“Mesdames, messieurs,” said the small man. “My name is Sudre. I am a teacher of music. And tonight I am going to show you that music is not only something we listen to. It is something we can say.”

He began very simply.

“There are seven notes,” he said, and went to the pianoforte and played them, one after another, rising. “Do, ré, mi, fa, sol, la, si. Every child who has ever had a music lesson knows them. In every country in Europe they are the same seven. A Russian, an Italian, a Spaniard, an Englishman—” he bowed slightly toward Clara’s row, because her bonnet was so obviously English “—all of them know these seven sounds.” He turned back to the room. “So. What if the seven notes were not only notes? What if they were the letters of a language? A language that every person in the world already half knows, without knowing that they know it?”

He took a piece of chalk and wrote on a board.

Do. Ré. Mi. Fa. Sol. La. Si.

“Seven syllables,” he said. “With one syllable, I can make seven words. With two, forty-nine. With three, more than three hundred. With four, more than two thousand. With five, enough for every thought a man can have.” He wrote beneath. Mi-sol. “Good. Bon. Now I turn it around.” Sol-mi. “And it means the opposite. Bad. Mauvais. Do you see? In my language, the opposite of a word is the word reversed, as a melody can be turned upside down.” He smiled, delighted with it, a man who had been delighted with it for ten years and had never once tired. “And because every word is a melody, it can be sung.” He sang it. Mi, sol. Sol, mi. “It can be played.” He nodded to the boy, and the boy lifted the violin and played it, two notes and then the same two reversed. “It can be shown with the hand.” He held up his fingers in seven different shapes, rapidly. “It can be fired with a cannon, or blown on a bugle, for the army across a battlefield. And—” he lifted a cloth from a frame beside the board “—it can be painted.”

Under the cloth were seven squares of colored paper on a board, in a row.

Red. Orange. Yellow. Green. Blue. Indigo. Violet.

“Newton’s seven,” said Sudre proudly. “The seven colors of the rainbow, as the great Newton counted them in the prism, and matched them himself to the seven notes of the scale. Do is red. Ré, orange. Mi, yellow. Fa, green. Sol, blue. La, indigo. Si, violet. A ship at sea could speak to another ship with seven colored flags. A deaf man could read it. A painter could write a poem on a wall.” He tapped the board, warming to the cataloguer’s joy. “And I am arranging my dictionary so that each first syllable opens a house of meaning: do, the man and his mind and his food; re, the house and the family; mi, our actions and our faults; fa, the country and the sea; sol, the arts and the sciences; la, industry; si, the city and its government. You see? Not only colours. A whole world in seven doors.”

There was a murmur in the room, half admiration and half amusement. One of the old men from the Institut was writing in a little book. An officer near the front said something to his neighbor about the cannon and laughed.

Clara wasn’t laughing.

She was looking at the row of colors, and the hair was standing up along her arms.

She didn’t know why. She sat very still in her grey silk with her gloved hands in her lap and felt it, a prickling that started at her wrists and ran up the insides of her arms to her shoulders, as if she’d walked into the cold air in front of a thunderstorm. It wasn’t the room. The room was stifling.

It was the colors. Something about the colors was wrong.

That was absurd. They were Newton’s colors, in Newton’s order; she had seen them in her father’s copy of the Opticks a hundred times, the little circle with the seven slices and the seven notes written round it. Red was do. That was right. She felt that it was right, the way you feel the floor under your feet. Orange was ré, and that was nearly right, orange the color of honey held up to a window. But yellow for mi—

No, said something in her, very quietly. Green.

She frowned. She looked at the green square, which was fourth. Fa. And the something in her said, No. That’s blue. Blue is the fourth.

It was as though she were reading a familiar word printed with one letter slipped. Every color after the first two had moved one place along. And at the end, where si was violet—

She didn’t know what si should be. She looked at it and felt the prickling go up into her scalp, and there was nothing there at all. A space. As though the last square on the board should have been left empty.

“Now,” said Sudre, “I will show you that it is a real language, and not a parlor game. Mademoiselle—” he was looking at the ladies in the second row “—will someone give me a sentence? Anything. In French.”

A lady with a fan said, laughing, “Je vous aime.”

There was more laughter. Sudre bowed, unembarrassed, and thought for a moment, and wrote on the board a string of syllables, and handed the chalk to no one, and went to stand at the back of the platform with his back to the boy. “Now. My pupil has not seen what I wrote. I will sing it, and he will play what he hears, and then he will tell you what it means.”

He sang.

It was only a few notes. But as he sang them, one of the phrases fell, sliding, from the second note of the scale down to the first. Ré to do. Orange down to red.

Clara’s eyes filled with tears.

She had no idea why. She was not a girl who cried at things; her father said she had a dry eye. She sat in the hot salon with the tears running down her face under her bonnet while sixty Parisians smiled at a little man singing I love you in musical notes, and she felt as if something enormous and very old had gone down into the ground. The sun into the earth. She knew it as surely as she knew her own name. That’s grief. That falling note was grief. It had always been grief.

The boy played it back on the violin, and said brightly, “Je vous aime, monsieur,” and the room applauded.

Clara’s father woke with a start at the applause and looked at her face and said, alarmed, “Clara, my dear. Whatever’s the matter?”

“Nothing,” she said. “Nothing. It’s the heat.”

She went up to him afterward.

She had never done such a thing in her life. Her father was talking to a man about felts. The officers and the ladies had gone out to their carriages. The old men from the Institut were still sitting with their heads together over the little book. Sudre was by the pianoforte, packing the boy’s violin into its case, with the board of colored squares leaning against the wall behind him.

“Monsieur,” said Clara, in her careful schoolroom French. “Forgive me. May I ask you something?”

He turned, and saw her bonnet and her pink, damp face, and smiled at her with real pleasure, because people who came to ask questions were what he lived for.

“Of course, mademoiselle. You’re English? Your French is very good.”

“Thank you. It isn’t.” She pointed to the colors. “Where did they come from? The colors for the notes.”

“From Newton,” he said, puzzled. “I said so. His Opticks. He divided the spectrum into seven, to match the seven notes, because he believed, as Pythagoras did, that the same harmony governs light and sound. I took his order. Why?”

“Did you ever think they might be—” she didn’t know how to say it “—in a different order?”

Sudre looked at her more carefully.

“Different how?”

“Red for do. Yes. And orange for ré, or a color like honey. But then green for mi. Not yellow. And blue for fa. And then—” she stopped. She was going to sound mad. “I’m sorry. I don’t know why I think it. It’s only that when you sang the falling note, ré to do, I—” She couldn’t finish.

“You wept,” said Sudre gently. “I saw you. I thought it was the lady’s sentence.”

“It wasn’t the sentence. It was the falling. It was—” she reached for it “—it was like the sun going into the ground.”

The small man in the brown coat went very still.

After a moment he looked down at the violin case under his hand.

“May I tell you something, mademoiselle?” he said. “I tell everyone that I invented this language. And I did. I have spent ten years on it, and I have a dictionary of nine thousand words, and I have shown it to the army and to the Institut, and it is mine.” He hesitated. “But I’ll tell you how it began, which I don’t usually say, because people laugh. When I was a young man, before I came to Paris, I sat one evening by a river at Albi, playing the scale on my violin, idly, the way you do. Up and down. And all at once I had the feeling that the notes were saying something. Not like a melody. Like words, in a language I’d once known and had forgotten. It was so strong I stopped playing and sat there with the hair standing up on my arms.”

Clara felt her own arms prickle again under her sleeves.

“I thought it was an idea,” said Sudre. “A wonderful idea. I’ve spent my life on it. But sometimes, late at night, it feels less like an idea and more like—” he searched for the word “—a memory. As if I were trying to remember something, and getting it nearly right, and not quite.” He smiled at her, a little sadly. “Like your colors. Nearly right. Not quite.”

“Did you ever see it anywhere? Before? Written?”

He laughed. “No, no. Nothing like it exists. I’ve looked. I spent a whole winter in the Bibliothèque du Roi looking, to be sure no one had thought of it first.” He paused. “There was one thing. An old book of alchemy, from Prague. Pictures. Nonsense, mostly, kings in baths. But at the end there was a star, with seven points, and six of the points had the old signs of the planets on them, and under each one a syllable of the scale. Ut, ré, mi, fa, sol, la. The old way, with ut for do.” He shook his head. “And the seventh point was blank. Nothing. As if the printer had forgotten it.”

“What did you do?”

“I laughed,” said Sudre. “And I took my pencil and wrote si on the blank point, very lightly, so the librarian wouldn’t see. To finish the scale.” He looked at her, amused at himself. “It seemed a shame to leave it empty.”

Clara stood very still.

“Perhaps it was meant to be empty,” she said.

“Perhaps,” said Sudre, kindly, the way one says perhaps to a nineteen-year-old English girl with a damp face. “But a scale isn’t finished without its seventh note, mademoiselle. Everyone knows that.”

She bought his grammar from him before she left. A thin booklet, Langue musicale universelle, printed on cheap paper, with a folding chart of the seven colors in the back. He signed it for her: À Mademoiselle Ashby, qui a pleuré au bon endroit. To Miss Ashby, who wept at the right place.

She carried it home to London in her trunk, and kept it all her life.

She married a maker of scientific instruments named Hale, from Bristol, which surprised everyone, including her. She had a daughter, Anne, who grew up to marry a clerk of the new electric telegraph named Edward Pryce. She had a piano, too, that her father made her as a wedding present, with seven brass candle-sconces on its front because she asked for seven. She taught her daughter the scale on it, and the grammar of the universal language, which Anne thought was a lovely game and forgot. She taught it again, years later, to her granddaughter, a quick, solemn, frowning little girl named Eleanor, who did not think it was a game, and did not forget.

She never told anyone about the colors. Not her husband, not her daughter. Once, when Eleanor was very small and was banging the keys of the old piano, Clara heard her play two notes, ré and then do, sliding down, and felt the hair go up on her arms, and found that she had sat down suddenly on the piano stool and taken the child’s hand off the keys.

“What’s the matter, Grandmama?”

“Nothing, darling.” Clara held the small hand. “That’s a very sad thing you just played. Did you know?”

Eleanor looked at the keys, and at her grandmother, with her solemn frown.

“No,” she said. “Is it?”

“Yes,” said Clara. “It always has been.”

In the Bibliothèque du Roi, which would soon be called something else, and then something else again, a thin quarto of seven woodcuts went back to its shelf among four hundred others like it, behind glass. On its last plate, a seven-pointed star had six signs of the planets at six of its points, and a syllable of the scale under each, and at the seventh point, where the old assayer had left the white of the paper bare, there was now, very faint, in pencil, a single word.

Si.

It looked right. It finished the scale. Anyone would have done the same.

Next · Part Four · Chapter OneEarth Current

Part FourThe Electricians

Part Four · Chapter One

Earth Current

On the night of the first and second of September, 1859, following a great flare upon the Sun observed by Mr. Carrington at Redhill, the aurora was seen as far south as Cuba and Rome. Telegraph lines across Europe and America failed or ran wild. Operators received shocks; paper caught fire under the styluses. On the line between Boston and Portland, the batteries were disconnected entirely, and the operators worked for two hours on the current coming out of the sky and the ground alone. It is the first recorded occasion in the modern world on which people sent messages using nothing but the breath.

Concordance of Fragments, London recension, Fragment 1

15 min read

The sky over Didcot was red, and the needles were moving by themselves.

Eleanor Pryce was twelve years old, and she should have been in bed. She had been in bed. She’d been woken by the dog barking and her mother at the window saying Edward, Edward, come and look, in a voice Eleanor had never heard her use. She’d gone to her own window and pulled back the curtain and seen the whole northern half of the sky on fire, great slow curtains of crimson and pale green hanging over the railway cutting and the water-meadows and the roofs of the town, folding and unfolding, so bright that she could see the hands of the church clock across the lane. Ten minutes past midnight.

By a quarter past she was in her nightgown and her father’s old coat in the telegraph office at the junction, because her father had pulled on his boots and run there, and she’d run after him, and nobody had sent her home.

The office was a single room at the end of the station platform, with a stove and a high desk and the long bench where the instruments stood. Two of them: the double-needle machines of the Electric Telegraph Company, each a wooden case the size of a small clock with a dial on its face and two black needles on the dial, which swung left and right to spell out letters when someone at the far end of the line pressed the keys. The line to London. The line to Bristol. Eleanor had sat on the stool by the stove a hundred times after school, watching her father read the needles, and she could read them herself, a little, faster than he knew.

Tonight the needles were swinging and no one was sending.

They swung hard to the left and stuck there, trembling, pinned against the brass stops. Then they swung back across the dial and stuck to the right. Then they quivered in the middle. Then left again. The clerk on night duty, a young man named Fisk, was standing back from the bench with his hand wrapped in a handkerchief.

“It bit me,” he said to her father. “Mr. Pryce, it bit me. I put my hand on the key to call London and it threw a spark at me like a cat.”

Her father went to the bench. He was a careful man, a clerk, not an engineer, who wrote a beautiful copperplate hand and kept the station accounts to the farthing. He looked at the needles for a long time. Then he picked up a pencil and touched it, by the wood, to the brass terminal where the line from London came in. A blue spark jumped a full inch to the pencil’s end, with a snap. Fisk swore. Eleanor’s father put the pencil down.

“Is it lightning?” Eleanor said. “There isn’t a storm.”

“No,” said her father. “There isn’t a storm.”

They all looked out of the window at the sky.

Bristol called them at half past one.

It came through on the Bristol needles, between the wild swings, in short desperate bursts: Didcot. Didcot. Is yours mad. Her father keyed back, wincing, with a glove on: Mad. Batteries on, can’t send. Then nothing for a while, the needles banging against their stops. Then Bristol again, slowly, letter by letter, as if the man at the other end were spelling something he didn’t believe.

Take your batteries off.

Her father stared at it.

The batteries were in a wooden trough under the bench: rows of glass jars full of acid with zinc and copper plates in them, which made the current that drove the needles. Without them the telegraph was a dead thing. Everyone knew that. You might as well take the water out of a mill-race.

Take them off, said Bristol again. Ours are off. We have current without them. Try.

So her father got down on his knees in front of the trough with a lamp and unscrewed the brass clamps one by one, and lifted the wires away from the jars, until the batteries were sitting there connected to nothing at all. Then he got up and went to the key and pressed it.

On the dial, the needle swung.

It was weaker than usual. It wavered. It was riding on the other swinging, the wild one, the one that came from nowhere. But it swung when he pressed the key, and stopped when he let go. And after a moment the Bristol needle answered, faintly, unmistakably: We have you Didcot. No batteries. God knows what’s driving it.

Fisk sat down on the floor.

Eleanor stood by the stove in her father’s coat, looking at the needles. There was no battery. There was nothing in the room making the current. Her father was sending a message to Bristol, forty miles away, on something that came out of the red sky and down the wires, or up out of the ground through the earth-plate buried under the platform, and nobody in the room understood it.

She thought it was the most beautiful thing she had ever seen.

They worked the line that way for nearly two hours, sending the night’s few messages and long bursts of chatter between themselves and Bristol about what in heaven’s name was happening. Fisk went home with his burnt hand. Her father forgot Eleanor was there.

And Eleanor watched the needles in the gaps.

In the gaps, when nobody was sending, the needles kept moving. At first she thought it was just wild, the way a dog runs wild in a field. But she had been watching her father read these needles since she was six, and she had a quick eye, and after a while she began to see that it wasn’t wild at all.

It came in bursts. A set of swings, of different strengths: a little to the left, a lot to the left, a little to the right, very far to the right, and so on. Then a stillness. Then another set. Then a stillness. And when she started counting the strengths, the way you’d count how far a pendulum goes, she found that there seemed to be about seven different strengths it could swing to, from barely moving to pinned on the stop.

She found a pencil and the back of a telegraph form, and started writing it down. She numbered the strengths one to seven. She wrote each burst as a row of numbers. She drew a line for each stillness.

It was very hard. The needles were fast, and the strengths weren’t always clear, and twice the whole thing was swamped by a great surge that pinned both needles for a full minute. But by three in the morning, when the sky had faded to a dull rose and the needles were beginning to settle, she had two forms covered in her square, careful figures.

There were seven bursts. Then it started again. Every burst used all seven strengths, but in a different order. And the order of the bursts was always the same.

“Papa,” she said. “Look.”

Her father looked. He was grey with tiredness. He looked at the two forms for a long moment, and then at her, and put his hand on her head.

“You’ve been very good,” he said. “Very patient. It’s only the storm, my love. Disturbances. The aurora gets into the wires, and the wires go mad. It doesn’t mean anything.” He folded the forms and gave them back to her. “Keep them, if you like. Now come home. Your mother will think we’ve both been struck.”

She kept them.

Seventeen years later, on a winter night in 1876, she took them out of the tin box where she’d kept them all that time, and laid them beside a sheet of photographic paper on the testing-room bench at the Central Telegraph Office in London, and saw that they were the same.

She was twenty-nine. She was, as far as she or anyone knew, the only woman in England who was paid to do what she did, though she was paid as a clerk and not as what she was. When the Post Office took over the telegraph companies it had hired women by the hundred, because women were cheaper and had quicker fingers, and Eleanor had come in as one of the hundreds. Within a year she was in the testing room on the top floor, where the lines from every town in the kingdom came together, because she was the only person on the instrument floor who could find a fault on a line from a hundred miles away by measuring its resistance, and because the senior engineer had found that out and was a sensible man.

She worked nights. She preferred them. At night the traffic was light, and the testing room was quiet, and she could do what she had been doing for three years without anyone asking her why.

She was measuring the earth.

Every telegraph line in the kingdom was a single wire. Nobody bothered with two. The current went out along the wire from London to, say, Aberdeen, and came back through the ground itself, through the earth-plates buried at each end, through five hundred miles of soil and rock and water, as if the whole island were the second wire. Everyone knew this. Every engineer took it for granted, the way you take it for granted that the floor will hold you.

What Eleanor had found, that everyone else knew and thought was a nuisance, was that the ground was not quiet.

When you took the batteries off a long line at night and put a sensitive instrument across it, a current flowed anyway. Not much. A whisper. It came and went with the hours, and with the season, and very strongly with the aurora and the storms on the sun that the astronomers at Kew were beginning to count. The engineers called it earth current, and they hated it, because on bad nights it made the needles jabber and the messages garble, and they spent a great deal of ingenuity trying to get rid of it.

Eleanor spent hers trying to listen to it.

She had borrowed a mirror galvanometer from the instrument store: a tiny magnet hung by a silk thread inside a coil, with a scrap of mirror glued to the magnet, so that the faintest current through the coil would turn it a hair’s breadth, and a beam of lamplight bounced off the mirror would swing across a scale on the far wall like the hand of a giant clock. She’d rigged a clockwork drum covered in photographic paper beside the scale, so that the beam left a trace as it swung. Every night for three years, when she could get the line free, she’d taken the batteries off the long lines and laid the galvanometer across them and let the trace run until dawn.

Most nights it was nothing. A slow wandering line, rising and falling with the hours.

On storm nights it was not nothing.

On the night of the fourth of February, 1872, when the aurora had come down red over London and half the lines in England had failed, she’d had the Aberdeen line on the drum, and the trace had come off in the morning covered with a dense crabbed scrawl of peaks and troughs that the senior engineer had looked at and laughed at and called the devil’s handwriting. She’d taken it home and spent a month of evenings on it with a ruler and a magnifying glass. Under the scrawl there was a pattern: bursts at seven heights, and stillnesses between.

Tonight, in 1876, after three more years and four more storm nights, she had the Penzance line and the Aberdeen line both on the drum at once, five hundred miles apart in opposite directions, and the same pattern was on both of them. At the same moment. Burst for burst, to the second.

And when she took out the two old forms from the Didcot office, written in a twelve-year-old’s square pencil on the back of telegraph paper on the night of the great aurora in 1859, the bursts were the same as those.

Seventeen years. Three lines. The same seven groups.

She sat at the bench with the lamp hissing and the trace and the forms in front of her and felt the hair stand up on her arms.

She took it to the senior engineer, Mr. Ransome, the next morning.

He was a kind man, overworked, with ink on his cuffs and a beard like a hedge, and he looked at the traces for a long time with real interest. He’d always liked her. He’d fought to get her into the testing room.

“It’s very fine work,” he said. “Very fine. I don’t think anyone’s laid two long lines side by side like this before. You should write it up.”

“For whom?”

He thought about it. That, she would come to understand, was the whole of the trouble.

“Well,” he said. “Not for us, really. We’re telegraph men. Our business is getting the messages through. Earth current is a fault to us, Miss Pryce; you know that. We want it gone.” He tapped the traces. “If it’s to do with the aurora, that’s for the astronomers. Kew, or Greenwich. They have the magnetic instruments. And if it’s in the ground itself, I suppose it’s for the geologists. Or the Royal Society.” He frowned. “Though I’m not sure what section.”

“It’s in all of them,” said Eleanor. “That’s the point. It’s on the lines, so it’s ours. It comes with the aurora, so it’s the astronomers’. It’s in the ground, so it’s the geologists’. And it’s a pattern, Mr. Ransome. The same seven groups, for seventeen years, on lines five hundred miles apart. Whose is that?”

He looked at her with a kind of helpless sympathy.

“I don’t know,” he said honestly. “I think it falls between.”

She went to Kew, where the superintendent said, courteously, that his instruments recorded magnetic variation, not currents in telegraph lines, and that her pattern was very likely an artefact of her clockwork. At the Geological Society a man said that earth currents were electrochemical effects between dissimilar rocks and could be expected to vary. The Royal Society passed her paper to a Fellow who specialized in electricity, who wrote to say that he specialized in the electricity of the laboratory, not the earth, and passed it to another.

She began to keep a list, in the back of her trace-book, of the doors she had been politely shown out of. By the summer it had nine names on it, and every one of them was a good man.

In the spring of 1876 her grandmother was dying, and Eleanor went home to Bristol to sit with her.

Clara Hale was sixty-eight. She’d been ill all winter, and now she lay in the narrow front bedroom of the house in Clifton where she’d lived for forty years, with the window open on the garden and the old piano downstairs, the one with seven brass candle-sconces, which nobody had played properly since Eleanor went to London.

“Play me something,” she said on the second evening. “I can hear it through the floor. Leave the doors open.”

Eleanor sat at the piano in the dusk and didn’t know what to play. Then, because she’d been carrying the traces with her for a month and couldn’t think of anything else, she took them out of her bag and laid them on the music stand.

It was an idea she’d had a long time ago, as a child, and never tried. Seven strengths. Seven notes. Her grandmother had taught her the scale on this piano when she was four, and the universal language of the little French music-master, which her mother had called a game. Do for one. Re for two. Up to si for seven.

She played the first burst.

It sounded like nothing. Seven notes, in an odd order, plunking in the empty parlor. She played the second. The third. She felt foolish. She played all seven, from the Aberdeen trace, the way she’d numbered them, and sat with her hands on the keys in the silence afterward.

“Eleanor.”

Her grandmother’s voice came down through the open doors, thin and strange.

“Eleanor, play that again.”

She played it again. When she finished she heard her grandmother say something, very quietly, that she didn’t catch. She went upstairs.

Clara was sitting up in bed. Her face was wet. She was holding out her arms, and the fine white hairs on them were standing up in the lamplight.

“Where did you get that?” she said.

“It’s earth current, Grandmama. It’s from the telegraph lines. In storms. I’ve been recording it for—”

“It’s the language.” Her grandmother’s thin hand closed on Eleanor’s wrist with surprising strength. “Monsieur Sudre’s. The universal language. Every group is a word. Seven syllables. Do-re-mi-—” She shook her head. “No. Not his. Something like his. He had it wrong. He had it nearly right and wrong, I told him so, I was nineteen and I told him—” She was crying properly now. “The colors were wrong. And the end. The last one. It’s older than him, Eleanor. It’s older than anything. Where did you get it?”

“From the ground,” said Eleanor. “And the sky. Both at once.”

Clara lay back on the pillows and closed her eyes.

“Go down,” she said after a while. “Play it again. And again. As long as you can. I want the numbers in my bones before I sleep.”

So Eleanor went down, and sat at the piano in the dark with the doors open, and played her grandmother the earth current, over and over, from the traces, the way you’d read out a column of figures. She didn’t feel anything. She had never felt anything, at the bench or the piano or anywhere else; she was a counter, not a hearer, and she knew it. She played it the way her father had read the needles, plainly, accurately, every note in its place. Upstairs, through the floor, she could hear her grandmother crying, and then not crying, and then, very softly, singing along. Not in do-re-mi. In some other syllables that Eleanor had never heard, round and strange, like a nursery rhyme from another country.

She played until the candles in the seven sconces burned down.

Clara Hale died four days later. In the drawer of her bedside table Eleanor found a thin booklet of cheap paper, Langue musicale universelle, with a folding chart of seven colors in the back. On the flyleaf, in faded ink, in a small French hand: À Mademoiselle Ashby, qui a pleuré au bon endroit.

And on the chart, beside each of Newton’s seven colors, in her grandmother’s own pencil, someone had crossed out the color and written another. Do, red, left alone. Re, orange, crossed out, honey. Mi, yellow, crossed out, green. Fa, green, crossed out, blue. Sol, blue, crossed out, violet, like dusk. La, indigo, crossed out, silver, white.

And beside si, violet, she had crossed it out and written nothing at all. Only a small empty circle.

Eleanor sat on the edge of her grandmother’s bed for a long time with the booklet in her hands.

Then she went back to London, to the testing room at St. Martin’s-le-Grand, and the next storm night she took the batteries off the Aberdeen line and laid the galvanometer across it and let the drum turn until dawn, because she didn’t know how to stop.

Next · Part Four · Chapter TwoThe Cairo Survey

Part Four · Chapter Two

The Cairo Survey

In 1880 and 1881, a young surveyor measured the Great Pyramid with an accuracy never before attempted, and found its sides true to within a fraction of an inch over seven hundred and fifty feet. He noted, in passing, that the walls of the lower chamber were crusted with salt to a thickness of half an inch, and offered no explanation. Neither did anyone else. The age of measurement had arrived at Giza, and it measured everything, beautifully, except what the building was for.

Concordance of Fragments, Cairo recension, Fragment 2

16 min read

The copper hook was smaller than she’d expected. It lay on a square of green baize on Mr. Dixon’s dining table in Newcastle, between a cedar splinter and a granite ball the size of an orange, and it would have fitted in the palm of her hand with room to spare.

“There,” said Waynman Dixon, with the cheerful pride of a man who has shown his treasures to a great many visitors. “That’s the lot. Out of the northern shaft of the Queen’s Chamber, 1872. My brother’s man pushed a jointed rod up it, and these came out, or were knocked down from somewhere above. Nobody had seen the inside of those shafts since the builders. They’d been sealed at the chamber end with a skin of stone. We found the mouths by tapping.”

“May I?” said Eleanor.

He nodded, and she picked up the hook.

It was a crude thing. A short bent rod of copper, gone green and brown, with a hook at one end and a stub at the other where it had broken off something. She held it in her palm and thought, very clearly and coldly, copper. Copper, inside a shaft no wider than her two hands, that climbed into the body of the largest building in the world and led nowhere. Copper, in a sealed chamber whose walls were crusted half an inch thick with salt.

She had spent twelve years putting copper into salt water at the bottom of telegraph poles.

“What do you think it was for?” she asked.

“Oh, it’s a tool, I should think,” Dixon said. “Bit of a builder’s tool, dropped and forgotten. They’d have used rods to clear the shafts as they built them. Air shafts, you know. Or for the soul of the king to fly out of, the Egyptologists say.” He smiled. “Take your pick, Miss Pryce. I’m an engineer. I build bridges. I’m only glad I found the thing.”

She put the hook back down on the baize, very carefully. It was the summer of 1883. Six months later she was in Egypt.

She went as a clerk again. She’d come to accept it. The Eastern Telegraph Company was laying a new line from Alexandria to Cairo and on up the river, now that the British were in Egypt and meant to stay, and they needed someone to test it who could find a fault by measurement, and Mr. Ransome, who had gone over to the Company as a consulting engineer, wrote to them that the best person in England for the work was a woman of thirty-six with no letters after her name and a habit of laying galvanometers across long lines at night. They took her. They paid her half what they’d have paid a man and gave her a room in a hotel in Cairo with a ceiling fan worked by a boy on a string.

On her first free Sunday she hired a carriage and drove out to the pyramids.

She had seen engravings. Everyone had. The engravings were nothing. She came round the last bend in the road through the green fields with the dust behind her and there they were, three of them, on the edge of the desert, in the white morning, and the largest was so large that her eyes refused it. They kept trying to make it into a hill, because nothing that size could be made. Then she got down from the carriage and walked to its foot, and put her hand on a block of stone as high as her shoulder, and looked up, and up, and could not see the top for the glare.

The top was flat. She knew that from the engravings. A broken square platform, thirty feet across, where the point should have been.

Something was there, she thought. And they took it.

The survey that winter was being made by a man named Hadley, for the Royal Society, to check and extend the measurements of the young Mr. Petrie of two years before, whom Hadley admired more than anyone living. Ransome knew him. A letter was written. Hadley was a tall, stooped, scrupulous man of forty with a bald head burnt brown and a great love for exactness, and he was pleased to have, for a few weeks, the help of someone who knew how to read a fine instrument and wasn’t afraid of the dark.

“You understand,” he said, on the first morning, at the mouth of the entrance passage, “that we’re here to measure. Not to theorize. There’s been far too much theorizing about this building. Men measuring it with their Bibles, and finding the date of the end of the world in the length of the passages.” He said it with real distaste. “Mr. Petrie has shown how it should be done. You measure. You measure again. You write it down. You let the stone speak for itself.”

“Yes,” said Eleanor. “I understand.”

She did understand. She agreed with him completely. That, too, would turn out to be the trouble.

There were five of them in the party, most days. Hadley and Eleanor. A young Egyptologist from Oxford named Fenwick, who could read the old writing and was writing a book about the funerary religion of the Old Kingdom. A captain of the Royal Engineers named Moss, on loan from the army in Cairo, who was there to advise on the stability of the masonry and spent most of his time looking at joints. And the guard.

The guard’s name was Mahmoud. He was a tall, quiet man of fifty with a grey beard and a blue robe, and he had the keys to the iron gate that the Service des Antiquités had put across the entrance passage, and he went in with them every morning and came out with them every night. His father had guarded the plateau, he said, and his grandfather, and his grandfather’s grandfather, back to before the French came. They had always lived in the village at the foot of the plateau. They had always guarded it. Nobody had ever asked them to and nobody had ever told them to stop.

Eleanor noticed him on the second morning, before they went in.

He stopped at the well by the gate where the donkey-boys watered their animals. He took a twist of paper from his robe and shook something into a tin cup and filled it with water from the well and stirred it with one finger. Then he washed his hands in it. Front and back, between each finger. Once, again, a third time. He drank what was left. Then he went to the iron gate and unlocked it.

Nobody else noticed. Hadley was checking his theodolite. Fenwick was reading. Captain Moss was looking at a joint.

“What was that?” Eleanor asked him, later, in her bad Arabic, when they were alone at the gate.

Mahmoud looked at her a long moment.

“Salt,” he said. “My father did it. Before going in.” He shrugged. “So I do it.”

“Why salt?”

“I don’t know.” He locked the gate behind them. “So that I go in the same as he did.”

The lower chamber, the one the Arabs called the chamber of the Queen, was reached by a long low passage off the bottom of the great sloping gallery, so low she had to go most of it bent double, with a candle in one hand and a measuring rod in the other. Then it opened, all at once, and she could stand.

It was a room of white limestone, perhaps eighteen feet by seventeen, with a pointed roof of great slabs leaned together. In the east wall there was a tall niche, stepped, like a doorway made smaller and smaller as it went back into the stone. The floor was rough and unfinished, as Petrie had said. And the walls—

The walls were white with salt.

She’d read about it. Petrie had noted it, half an inch thick in places. Reading about it was nothing. She held up her candle and the light went across the walls and they glittered, every one of them, a crust of grey-white crystals grown up out of the stone itself, thick and rough and crumbling, so that the whole chamber looked as if it had been dipped in frost. She put out her hand and touched it. Coarse. Gritty. She touched her finger to her tongue, and it was bitter and sharp.

“Extraordinary, isn’t it?” said Hadley behind her. “Petrie thought it might be from the limestone itself. Natural salts sweating out. Though there’s a good deal more of it here than anywhere else in the building.” He was already setting up his tripod. “We’ll take the wall dimensions first. To the hundredth of an inch, if you’d be good enough to hold the rod.”

She held the rod.

In the north and south walls, a little above the height of her shoulder, were the two square holes that Dixon had found, where his brother’s man had chiseled through the thin skin of stone and found the shafts behind it. They were hand-span square, black, perfectly cut. She held her candle to the southern one. The shaft ran up into the stone at a steep angle, straight and true, the walls of it running together into a point of darkness far beyond the reach of the light. Dixon’s man had pushed his rods up the northern one. Nobody had gone very far up the southern.

She stood looking up it for a long time.

She found the grooves on the fifth day.

She would never have seen them if she hadn’t been holding the rod. Hadley was taking the height of the walls at intervals of a foot, all the way round, and she was moving the rod along the west wall a foot at a time, and at the height of her shoulder her fingers, sliding along the stone under the salt, went into a dip.

She stopped.

“A little to the left, Miss Pryce,” said Hadley.

“One moment.”

She scraped at the salt with her thumbnail. It came away in flakes, grey and white, and under it the limestone was smooth. Not smooth the way the rest of the wall was smooth, dressed with a chisel and rubbed. Smooth the way the step of an old church is smooth, the step everybody has walked up for six hundred years. Worn. Hollowed in a shallow curve, about as wide as her hand and as long as her forearm, running horizontally along the wall at the height of a person’s shoulder.

There was another one a foot to the right. A pair.

She took her pocketknife out and scraped more salt away, quickly, while Hadley called to her. At each end of each groove there was a small square hole in the stone, as wide as her little finger, filled flush with old grey mortar. She put the point of her knife into the mortar and it crumbled, and behind it the hole went back half an inch into the stone, and the inside of the hole was stained green.

Green. The particular dull blue-green she’d seen ten thousand times on the brass and copper terminals of telegraph instruments left too long in damp offices.

“Miss Pryce?”

“Mr. Hadley,” she said. Her voice sounded strange to her. “Would you come and look at this, please?”

They all came. Hadley with his lamp, and Fenwick from his notes, and Captain Moss from his joint. Mahmoud stood in the low doorway of the passage with his candle and watched.

She showed them the two grooves, side by side, worn at the height of the shoulder. The four pin holes, filled with mortar. The green stain inside them. She went round the chamber with her knife and found two more pairs, on the north wall and the south, each at the same height, each with its pin holes, each worn into the stone in the same shallow curve, as if by hands.

“Copper,” she said. “Those were bands of copper. Or something with copper in it. Set into the wall at the height of the shoulders, held with pins. Four pairs. They’ve been taken out. The holes were filled afterward.” She heard herself going on. “And the salt. The whole chamber is crusted with salt, more than anywhere else in the building. Salt water. Salt water and copper and stone. I’ve spent twelve years putting copper plates into salt water, gentlemen. That’s what a telegraph is. Those were contacts. Somebody put their hands on them.”

There was a silence.

Then Captain Moss, very kindly, said, “Contacts for what, Miss Pryce?”

“I don’t know.”

“An electric telegraph,” said Fenwick, and smiled. He wasn’t sneering; he was a pleasant young man, and he liked her. “In the Old Kingdom. Five thousand years ago. Miss Pryce, I know how it must look, to someone in your line of work. But you must see. The niche there—” he pointed to the stepped doorway in the east wall “—is almost certainly for a statue of the king’s ka. His spirit-double. These grooves are very interesting, I grant you. I’ve never seen them noted. But the obvious reading is ritual. Offerings, or perhaps a rail to hold hangings, textiles, during the funeral rites. Copper fittings would be quite usual. They’d have been taken by robbers. Robbers took every scrap of metal in Egypt.”

“And the wear? The shape of hands?”

“Priests,” said Fenwick. “Touching them during the rites. For centuries, perhaps. Ritual contact with a sacred object. It’s very well attested.” He was entirely reasonable. “Grooves worn by devotion. Quite moving, really.”

“And the salt?”

“Natural efflorescence from the limestone,” said Hadley. “I’ve no doubt. I’ll take samples.” He looked at the grooves with his lamp, closely, scrupulously, the way he looked at everything, and she saw that he was interested, genuinely, and that he was about to do the most honest thing he could think of. “We’ll measure them,” he said. “All of them. Exactly. Length, depth, profile, the position of every hole to the hundredth of an inch. Then the evidence will be there for whoever comes after. That’s the right way.”

“And then?”

“And then nothing, Miss Pryce,” he said gently. “We’re surveyors. We don’t need to know what they were for. We need to know where they are.”

Captain Moss had put his hand flat on the salt-crusted wall beside one of the grooves and was looking at the joint above it with a professional eye.

“Good solid work, that,” he said. “Not a hair out of true.”

So they measured them.

She did it herself. Hadley let her, because she’d found them, and because she was better at it than Fenwick and more patient than Moss. She spent eleven days in the Queen’s Chamber with a candle and a scale rule and a set of fine calipers and Hadley’s best brass protractor, and measured every groove and every pin hole and every inch of the salt, and wrote it all down in a notebook in her square careful hand. The profile of each groove, at intervals of a quarter-inch, so that you could have rebuilt the curve of the hands that wore it. The diameter and depth of each pin hole. The distance of each groove from the floor, and from each corner, and from the mouths of the two shafts. She took samples of the salt in glass tubes. She took scrapings of the green stain on a square of white paper and folded it carefully and labeled it.

She measured the shafts. As far as her rods would reach, which was not far. The angle of the southern one. The cut of its walls, which was perfect. The faint tide-line of white crystals along its floor, going up into the dark.

She did it perfectly. She knew it was perfect. It was the best work she had ever done. And on the last evening, when Hadley and the others had gone out to the carriage, she sat down alone in the chamber on the rough floor, with her notebook on her knee and her candle beside her, and understood that she had measured everything in the room, to the hundredth of an inch, and felt nothing at all.

She was a counter. She had always known that. She could see the pattern, and number it, and write it down, and she had never once in her life felt the hair on her arms rise except when someone else did. Her grandmother had heard the earth current through a piano and wept. Eleanor had played it to her, every note right, and felt nothing. And now she had come two thousand miles and found the very place where someone had once put their hands on the copper and heard whatever it was, and she had measured the grooves their hands had worn, perfectly, and it was like measuring the inside of an empty church to the hundredth of an inch and writing down, very accurately, that there was no one in it.

She looked at the grooves in the light of her candle.

Then, because there was no one to see, she got up, and went out to the well by the gate in the dusk, where Mahmoud was sitting with his keys. She asked him for the salt. He looked at her, and gave her the twist of paper without a word. She stirred it into his tin cup and washed her hands. Front and back, between each finger. Three times. She drank the rest, and it burned her throat.

She went back in. She blew out her candle. In the absolute dark she found the grooves on the west wall by touch, and set her wet palms in them, and stood there.

Cold stone. Grit of salt. The weight of the whole building over her head, and the silence.

Nothing.

She stood until her arms ached. Nothing came. She hadn’t expected anything to come. There was no copper in the grooves, and no cap on the top of the building, and nothing in the shafts, and she was who she was. She knew that the circuit was open. She knew it the way she knew the resistance of a line. You could stand at the bands of a stripped telegraph station with wet hands all night and nothing would come through, because there was nothing there to carry it, and it would be no use at all that your hands were wet.

She stood anyway.

When she came out, the stars were out over the desert, and Mahmoud was locking the gate. His son was with him, a small boy of six or seven who had come up from the village with his father’s supper, and was sitting on the wall by the well swinging his legs and singing to himself.

She stopped.

It was a counting song. Children all over the world had them. Seven words, in a skipping rhythm, over and over, the kind of rhyme you’d say jumping over a rope. But the words weren’t Arabic, or not any Arabic she had learned. Round, simple, strange.

Nen, sa, ri, hu, mai, tef, ol.

The ol at the end came out long and round, like the stroke of a bell.

“What’s he singing?” she said.

Mahmoud glanced at the boy. “A game,” he said. “For counting. My father taught me. It doesn’t mean anything.” He finished with the lock and put the keys away in his robe, and looked at her. Then he looked at her hands, which were still damp, and smelled, she supposed, of salt.

He didn’t say anything about it. But as he walked away down the slope toward the village in the starlight, with his son on his shoulders still singing, he turned back once and lifted one hand to her, palm up.

That night in her room in Cairo, under the ceiling fan, she wrote up the day in her notebook as she always did. The measurements. The samples. Hadley’s opinion of the salt, Fenwick’s of the grooves, Captain Moss’s of the joint, each set down as fairly as she could, because each was very likely true.

Then she wrote, at the bottom of the page, something that didn’t belong in a surveyor’s notebook.

Four pairs of contacts at shoulder height. Salt water. A shaft running up toward where the summit used to be. Copper in the shafts. A guard who washes three times in salt before going in, because his father did. A child who sings seven syllables and doesn’t know what they mean. My grandmother sang them too.

I have measured all of it perfectly. I have felt nothing. We have become very good, in this century, at measuring the place where something used to happen.

And then, underneath that, because she was a telegraph engineer and couldn’t help it:

If I am right, then the southern shaft of the lower chamber, if anyone ever follows it to its end, will be found closed. And there will be copper at the closure.

She underlined it twice. Then she put the notebook away in her trunk and lay awake for a long time, listening to the fan.

Part Four · Chapter Three

Augustus Thorne

The Plan assumed that the hearing would fade, and that it would return only when the world had become able to receive it: through a mind that could hold all the fragments at once, and that would know what it was holding. It did not provide for a third possibility. That a hearer might be born early, by chance, into a world that was not ready, and might gather the fragments himself, and understand them well enough to want them.

Concordance of Fragments, London recension, Fragment 3

16 min read

The card was very white and very thick, and the name was engraved in a copperplate so fine that Eleanor’s father would have envied it.

Mr. Augustus Thorne. The Thorne Electric Company. New York. Chicago. Butte, Montana.

On the back, in pencil, in a quick, slanting, confident hand: I have read your paper on periodic groups in earth current, 1885. I believe I can hear what you have been counting. Savoy Hotel, any evening this week. A.T.

She was fifty that year. She was the clerk-in-charge of the night testing room at St. Martin’s-le-Grand, which was the highest a woman could go and a good deal lower than she should have gone, and she had published exactly one paper in her life, twelve years before, in the Journal of the Society of Telegraph Engineers. It had been eight pages long. It had set out, with tables, the seven groups she had recorded on five long lines over thirteen storm nights, and suggested, very cautiously, in a single closing sentence, that the pattern might not be an artefact. She had been told by the editor, kindly, that it would be of interest to the members. As far as she knew, in twelve years, it had been of interest to no one.

She turned the card over three times.

Then she went home, and changed into her good grey dress, and took the Underground to Charing Cross.

The Savoy was eight years old and blazed with electric light. Every room. Every corridor. Lamps of cut glass on the walls, lamps on the tables, lamps in the lifts that carried you up and down without stairs. It was the first hotel in London lit entirely by electricity, and everyone who came through its doors looked up, the way people look up in a cathedral.

Mr. Thorne’s rooms were on the fourth floor, looking out over the river.

He opened the door himself. She’d expected a servant. She’d expected, from the card and the hotel and the three cities, a heavy man in a heavy watch-chain, the kind of American who came to London to buy patents and talk loudly in clubs. He was not that. He was perhaps forty-five, lean and tall and very quick, in a dark suit that fitted him the way a glove fits a hand, with a clean-shaven face burnt brown by some fierce sun, and black hair going grey at the sides, and eyes of a pale startling blue. He looked at her the way she had seen Hadley look at a block of stone: entirely, all at once, as if measuring her.

“Miss Pryce,” he said. “Thank you for coming. I wasn’t sure you would. Come in. Come in and see.”

The sitting room was full of books and papers and objects, laid out on every table, under the electric lamps, like an exhibition.

She saw it all at once and then one thing at a time. A large photograph, framed, of a row of carved stone dancers above a doorway, somewhere tropical, with fig roots coming down through the stone. A plaster cast of another dancer, life-size, propped against the wall; she would learn later that it came from a museum in Paris where a Frenchman had brought casts back from a ruined city in Cambodia. A thin old quarto open on a stand, with a woodcut of a seven-pointed star. A Latin manuscript in a glass case. A cheap paper booklet that she recognized with a jolt like missing a stair: Langue musicale universelle. Telegraph traces pinned to a board. And on the desk by the window, open, heavily annotated in pencil in that quick slanting hand, a copy of the Journal of the Society of Telegraph Engineers for 1885.

Her paper.

“You see,” said Augustus Thorne, behind her, quite softly. “I’ve been collecting you.”

He gave her sherry and sat her by the fire, and then he didn’t sit down himself. He walked. He walked the whole time he talked, from the window to the cast to the woodcut to the fire and back, and touched things as he passed them, lightly, the way a man touches the keys of a piano he owns.

“I was born in a mining camp in the Montana Territory,” he said. “In a tent. My father dug copper and drank. He died when I was nine, in a shaft. I went down the shafts myself at ten, because there wasn’t anything else.” He stopped by the window. “And at ten years old, in the summer, when the storms came over the mountains, I used to go out barefoot on the hill after the rain and lie face down on the wet ground, and listen.”

“To what?”

“To the hill.” He turned. “It sang to me, Miss Pryce. Not in sounds. In colors. Behind my eyes, when I shut them. Red mostly, deep red, low, in my chest, like the biggest organ pipe in the world. Sometimes a kind of gold above it. Sometimes, on the best nights, in the biggest storms, other colors above that, too thin to hold. And in some places on the hill the red was stronger. A lot stronger. As if the hill were louder there.” He smiled. “I didn’t know what that meant. I was ten. I kept it to myself for ten more years. Then at twenty I told a man I worked for, an assayer, that the hill was loudest in a certain gulch, and he laughed and sank a test shaft there to shut me up, and struck the richest vein of copper that had ever been found in North America.”

Eleanor put down her sherry.

“I’ve done it ever since,” said Thorne. “Every claim I ever bought, I bought by lying on it after rain. Copper sings louder than anything. I own a hill in Butte now that they call the richest hill on earth, and I found it with my face in the mud.” He gestured round the blazing room. “The copper went into wire, and the wire went into light. I’ve lit half of Chicago. I’m not a showman pretending to be an engineer, Miss Pryce. I’m an engineer who learned to be a showman, because engineers don’t get paid.” He came back to the fire. “And all of it was because, when I was ten, I could hear a hill.”

“Can you still?”

“Less.” He said it without self-pity. “Every year a little less. The high colors went first, in my twenties. I hear the red still, very strongly. The gold, mostly. Above that, only in storms.” He looked at her with his pale eyes. “And I’ve had headaches my whole life. Doctors in four countries. Bromides. Laudanum. A man in Vienna who wanted to put electricity through my skull. None of them knew what was wrong with me.” He picked up her paper from the desk. “Then, two years ago, a clerk in my New York office who reads everything brought me this. And I read it, and I saw your tables, and the strengths you’d numbered one to seven, and I sat down on the floor of my office, Miss Pryce, the way I’m told a man sat down on the floor of a telegraph office in Didcot once.”

She went cold. “How do you know about Didcot?”

“I’ve read everything you’ve ever written,” said Thorne simply. “Including the letters to the Royal Society that the secretary filed. I paid a clerk.” He set the paper down. “I’m sorry. I don’t do things by halves. I couldn’t afford to. Because I knew, the moment I read your tables, that you’d been counting what I’ve been hearing since I was ten. And nobody else in the world has ever done either.”

He showed her the collection.

He went through it the way Hadley had gone through the Queen’s Chamber, one thing at a time, exactly, and she saw at once that whatever else he was, he was not a fool. He had found the fragments. Not all of them, and not in order, but he had found them, across three continents and four thousand years, by himself, without anyone to tell him they were related, because when he looked at them he heard the same thing.

The Latin manuscript was a fifteenth-century copy of a book called Liber Septem Aquarum, translated from the Arabic: seven metals, seven planets, seven colors, seven sounds, and then a list of what were plainly parts. A vessel of quicksilver between copper plates, beside a lodestone. A galena crystal touched with a fine wire. Quartz in a vise. A rod raised high, a plate in water under the ground. Let the one who listens wash three times in salt. He had bought it from a monastery in Austria that needed a roof.

The old woodcut book was Septem Aquae, Prague, 1610. He turned the pages for her. A king and queen in a bath between pillars; she saw the vessel and the plates at once, and the lodestone in the wolf. A bee searching a grey flower; the galena. A tower with roots in water and its spire in a storm cloud. A man sitting in the dark with his hands on two pillars and his feet in a basin. And the star.

“Six planets,” said Thorne, “with a syllable of the old scale under each. Ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la. And the seventh point empty. I’ve seen another copy, in Paris, at the Bibliothèque Nationale, where some fool has pencilled si in the gap. This one was never touched. And look at the line inside.” He traced it with one finger. “It goes from point to point in an order that isn’t the scale. Mercury and the Sun bound together. Then Jupiter, the Moon, Mars, Venus. Then it stops short of the empty point.” He looked at her. “What do you make of it?”

“I don’t know,” said Eleanor. “It looks like a sentence.”

“So do I. I don’t know what it says either.” He closed the book gently. “Not yet.”

The photograph was of the frieze of the Hall of Dancers in Cambodia, taken by a French expedition twenty years before, and the cast was of a dancer from the same hall. “Their hands,” Thorne said. “Look at the heights. I had a man measure them from the casts in Paris. They’re not random. The heights of the hands, against the height of the figures, go up in ratios. Small whole numbers, like the holes on a flute.” He handed her a sheet of figures. “Like the strengths in your tables, Miss Pryce. The same ratios. Exactly. Carved in Cambodia in the twelfth century, under a heathen king, by people who’d never heard of Egypt or Prague.”

She looked at the figures for a long time. Her hands were not quite steady.

“And this,” he said, and picked up Sudre’s booklet, “you know already. Your grandmother had one. You mentioned it in your second letter to the Royal Society.” He smiled. “À Mademoiselle Ashby, qui a pleuré au bon endroit.”

Eleanor stood up.

“You’ve had someone in my grandmother’s house.”

“No. I’ve had someone in the Royal Society’s correspondence files, which is a good deal easier.” He held up his hands. “Miss Pryce. Please. Sit down. I’m not your enemy. I’m the only person on this earth who knows what you’ve found. And I need you.” He said it very simply, and she saw that it was true. “Please.”

After a long moment, she sat down.

There was a piano in the corner, a big Steinway, which he said he’d had brought up to the fourth floor by six men and a crane because he’d wanted one. She’d known, somehow, from the moment she saw it, that he was going to ask.

“Play them for me,” he said. “The groups. From your tables. The way you played them for your grandmother.”

“How do you know I—”

“Because you’d have to,” said Thorne. “Anyone would. Seven strengths. Seven notes. Play them.”

So she sat at the Steinway in the blazing electric room above the Thames, with the plaster dancer watching from the wall, and played her tables, do through si, plainly, every note in its place, the way she’d played them for Clara in the dark at Clifton twenty-one years before.

When she finished, she turned round.

Thorne was standing by the fire with his eyes closed and his hands open at his sides. As she watched, his right hand moved, by itself, and turned palm down, as if pressing something flat. Then palm up. Then the fingers spread. She had seen those gestures. She’d seen them on the plaster dancer by the wall. And she had heard their names, without knowing they were names, from a guard’s small son on a wall at Giza in the dusk.

“Nen,” said Augustus Thorne, quite softly, with his eyes closed. “That’s the red. The hill. Sa. The gold.” His face was very still. “I’ve never had names for them. I’ve never known there were names. They just came. Now. When you played.” He opened his eyes. “What are they?”

“I don’t know,” said Eleanor. “A child sang them at Giza. My grandmother sang them, at the end. I’ve never known what they were.” She heard her own voice shaking. “You hear it. In the piano. In my numbers.”

“Faintly. Like a man hearing an orchestra from the street.” He came and stood by the piano and looked down at her tables on the stand. “But I hear it. And you count it. Do you see what that means?” His pale eyes were very bright. “I can hear and I can’t count. When it comes to me, in the storms, it comes like weather. Too much at once. I can’t hold it. I can’t write it down. I’ve tried for thirty years. It goes through me and leaves me with a headache and a colour and nothing on paper. And you—” he touched the tables “—you can count it perfectly and you can’t hear a note. We’re two halves of one instrument, Miss Pryce. The only two halves left in the world.”

He told her what he wanted at midnight, with the lamps of the Embankment stretching away along the black river below the window.

“It’s not a message,” he said. “I’ve thought so for years, and it isn’t. It isn’t Martians or angels or a lost race signalling from the center of the earth. I’ve had every crank in America in my office with every theory, and they’re all wrong. It’s the Earth itself.” He said it the way you’d state the price of copper. “The whole planet rings. Like a bell. Lightning strikes it ten thousand times a day, all over the world, and it rings, the space between the ground and the sky, and the ringing goes round and round the world. That’s what’s in your wires. That’s what I hear in the hill. That’s what the old ones heard in that room at Giza, with their hands on the copper. They found it, God knows how, five thousand years ago. And they built machines to listen to it. That’s all those buildings ever were. Ears.”

She didn’t say anything. She had never heard anyone say it out loud before. She had barely let herself think it.

“And what an ear can hear,” said Thorne, “a mouth can speak into.”

He went to the window.

“The Earth has a note. One great deep note, under all the others. I hear it. The red. I’ve heard it all my life; it’s the loudest thing in the world. Everything else is overtones. Find that note, exactly. Build a tower tall enough and a ground deep enough and strike it, at exactly the right pitch, at the right moment, with enough power—and the whole planet rings. Like a bell when you hit it right. And then you can put anything you like on that ringing, and it goes everywhere at once. Power. Light. Messages. Every house on earth lit from one tower, without a single wire. Every ship at sea talking to every other. No more telegraph lines, no more cables, no more men like your father kneeling in front of a battery trough at midnight.” He turned round. “I’ve filed the patent already. In America and here. Terrestrial resonance transmission. I’ve bought land in Colorado, on the high plain under Pikes Peak, where the storms are the biggest in the world and the air is thin and dry. I’m going to build it there. Next year.”

“One note,” said Eleanor.

“One note.”

“It isn’t one note.” She heard herself say it before she knew she was going to. “Mr. Thorne. It’s seven. Seven groups. Seven strengths. That’s what my tables show. You hear the red loudest because it’s the deepest, and because it’s the last one you have left. But the pattern isn’t in the red. It’s in how the seven move against each other. It’s in the intervals.
